Problem 18

Heavy Particle on a Cone

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 10
Conical Surface

A heavy particle of mass $m$ is moving without friction on a cone which is described as:

$$ z^2 = x^2 + y^2 $$

The frame of reference $Oxyz$ is inertial and $Oz$ is the ascending vertical. The initial coordinates of the particle are $(a, 0, a)$ and its velocity is $\sqrt{ag}\,\mathbf{j}$. Reduce the problem to integral equations and determine the maximum and minumum [sic] value of $z$ along the motion.

Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ slides without friction on the cone of the statement, whose vertex is the origin $O$ and whose axis is the ascending vertical $Oz$. The statement gives the initial position and velocity and asks for the reduction of the problem to integrals and for the extreme values of the height $z$ (Problems.pdf, p. 10). The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7: constraints and coordinates, frames, kinematics, forces, equations of motion, integration.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$, origin at the vertex of the cone (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathbf k$ vertical upward (ascending $Oz$), right-handed
Cylindrical basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by the azimuth $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$ (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24)
Surface basis$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf e_g, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf n\}$: $\mathbf e_g$ along the generator through $P$ (upward), $\mathbf n$ normal to the cone towards its axis; $\mathcal B_1$ turned about $\mathbf e_\theta$
Cylindrical coordinates$R$ (distance to the axis), $\theta$ (azimuth from the plane $Oxz$), $z$ (height) (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.23)
Constraint$f(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 0$ with $z \gt 0$ (upper nappe, the one that contains the initial point); in cylindrical coordinates $z = R$
Classificationholonomic (an equation among the coordinates), stationary (no explicit time), smooth (ideal: the reaction is normal to the surface); the particle rides on the inner face of the cone, so the contact is unilateral and is checked by the sign of the reaction ([Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedoma free particle has 3; one surface constraint leaves 2: $R(t)$ and $\theta(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$], $a$ [m]
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf k$
Reaction$\mathbf N = N\,\mathbf n$, one unknown component, since the normal subspace of a surface has dimension 1 (Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4)
Initial conditions$\mathbf r_0^P(0) = a\,\mathbf i + a\,\mathbf k$, $\mathbf v_0^P(0) = \sqrt{ag}\,\mathbf j$: $R(0) = a$, $\theta(0) = 0$, $\dot R(0) = 0$, $\dot\theta(0) = \sqrt{g/a}$ (from $a\dot\theta(0) = \sqrt{ag}$, see the kinematics)
Hypothesesno friction (statement); the reaction may only push the particle away from the surface towards the axis; the result shows that it always does
Figure 1. Meridian plane of $P$, with $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper. The shaded triangle (hypotenuse $R\sqrt2$, legs $R$ and $z$) gives the 45-degree generator; the surface basis $\mathbf e_g$, $\mathbf n$ is $\mathbf e_R$, $\mathbf k$ turned counterclockwise by $\pi/4$, a negative rotation about $\mathbf e_\theta$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

The cylindrical basis is seen from above, along $\mathbf k$: with $\mathbf i$ to the right and $\mathbf j$ up, $\mathbf k$, the cross product of $\mathbf i$ and $\mathbf j$, points out of the paper (⊙), and a positive rotation about $\mathbf k$ is counterclockwise.

Figure 2. Rotation about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper). The triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf i$, which is the minus sign of $-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i$; the dashed circle is the path seen from above..
Cylindrical basis: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View along $\mathbf k$ (⊙). The unit vector $\mathbf e_R$ is $\mathbf i$ turned counterclockwise by $\theta$: its right triangle has hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf i$ and opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along $\mathbf j$. The unit vector $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by the same angle: its adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ lies along $\mathbf j$ and its opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along minus $\mathbf i$, because turning the upward vector counterclockwise leans it to the left; that is the origin of the minus sign.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_R &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(adjacent leg on $\mathbf i$, opposite leg on $\mathbf j$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta &= -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(adjacent leg on $\mathbf j$, opposite leg on $-\mathbf i$)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \cos^2\theta\,\mathbf i\times\mathbf j - \sin^2\theta\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed: $\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = -\mathbf k$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= \cos\theta\left(\cos\theta\cdot 1 - 0\right) - \left(-\sin\theta\right)\left(\sin\theta\cdot 1 - 0\right) + 0 = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1, \quad \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} = \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times rows 1 and 2; row 3 is the unit row of $\mathbf k$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: every entry is a direction cosine, dimensionless: $[1]$; $\theta$ in radians, dimensionless

Meaning: the columns are $\mathbf e_R$, $\mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf k$ written in $\mathcal B_0$; $\mathbf e_R$ points horizontally away from the axis, towards the particle.

Normal of the cone from the gradient Law: the gradient of the implicit equation is normal to the surface; unit normal by normalisation Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.9; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.10; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.23
$$\begin{aligned} \nabla f &= 2x\,\mathbf i + 2y\,\mathbf j - 2z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(partial derivatives of $x^2 + y^2 - z^2$)} \\ &= 2R\left(\cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j\right) - 2z\,\mathbf k = 2R\,\mathbf e_R - 2z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($x = R\cos\theta$, $y = R\sin\theta$)} \\ &= 2R\left(\mathbf e_R - \mathbf k\right) \quad &\text{(on the cone $z = R$)} \\ |\nabla f| &= 2R\sqrt{1 + 1} = 2\sqrt2\,R \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_R \perp \mathbf k$, both unit)} \\ \mathbf n &= -\frac{\nabla f}{|\nabla f|} = \frac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right) \quad &\text{(sign chosen towards the axis and upwards, the side of the particle)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf n = \frac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right), \qquad V_N = \mathrm{span}\{\mathbf n\}}$$

Units: $[\nabla f] = [\mathrm{L}]$; $\mathbf n$ is dimensionless

Meaning: the normal lies in the meridian plane of $P$ and makes 45 degrees with the vertical; the reaction of the smooth cone can only act along it.

Surface basis: rotation about e_theta in the meridian plane Law: elementary rotation about the second axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.16; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19

View of the meridian plane of $P$ with $\mathbf e_R$ to the right and $\mathbf k$ up. Since $\mathbf e_\theta$ is the cross product of $\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf e_R$, it points into the paper (⊗) in this view, and a positive rotation about $\mathbf e_\theta$ (right-hand rule, thumb into the paper) is clockwise as seen. The generator $z = R$ rises at 45 degrees, so $\mathbf e_g$ is $\mathbf e_R$ turned counterclockwise by $\pi/4$: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19 with the negative angle $-\pi/4$. Its right triangle is the one of the generator itself: hypotenuse $R\sqrt2$ (the distance $OP$), adjacent leg $R$ (horizontal), opposite leg $z$, equal to $R$ (vertical); divided by the hypotenuse the legs give $\cos\frac{\pi}{4}$ and $\sin\frac{\pi}{4}$. The vector $\mathbf n$ is $\mathbf k$ turned by the same angle: adjacent leg along $\mathbf k$, opposite leg along minus $\mathbf e_R$ (turning the upward vector counterclockwise leans it to the left, towards the axis): that is the minus sign.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_g &= \cos\tfrac{\pi}{4}\,\mathbf e_R + \sin\tfrac{\pi}{4}\,\mathbf k = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right) \quad &\text{(adjacent leg on $\mathbf e_R$, opposite leg on $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf n &= -\sin\tfrac{\pi}{4}\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\tfrac{\pi}{4}\,\mathbf k = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right) \quad &\text{(same vector as from the gradient)} \\ \mathbf e_g\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta + \mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta\right) = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\mathbf k - \mathbf e_R\right) = \mathbf n \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta = \mathbf k$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_R$: right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(1\cdot\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2} - 0\right) - 0 + \left(-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\right)\left(0 - 1\cdot\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\right) = \tfrac12 + \tfrac12 = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{11} &= \tfrac12 + \tfrac12 = 1, \quad \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{13} = \tfrac12 - \tfrac12 = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times rows 1 and 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf e_\theta$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2} & 0 & -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2} \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2} & 0 & \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2} \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_1R_2] = 1, \quad [{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_g$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ span the tangent plane $V_T$ of the cone at $P$ and $\mathbf n$ spans the normal line $V_N$; the basis separates the directions where the particle may move from the one where the reaction acts.

Position vector of the particle Law: position vector in cylindrical coordinates with the constraint substituted Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.28; [Lecture 3, Slide 4]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OP} = R\,\mathbf e_R + z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(horizontal leg plus vertical leg of the meridian triangle)} \\ &= R\,\mathbf e_R + R\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(constraint $z = R$ substituted: only $R$ and $\theta$ remain)} \\ \mathbf r_0^P(0) &= a\,\mathbf i + a\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($R(0) = a$, $\theta(0) = 0$ so $\mathbf e_R(0) = \mathbf i$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_R + R\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the two free coordinates parametrise the cone, so the constraint holds identically and never has to be imposed again.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the cylindrical unit vectors Law: derivative of a unit vector of a rotating basis, $\dot{\mathbf u} = \boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf u$ Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ turns about $\mathbf k$ at the rate of $\theta$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_R &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_R = \mathbf e_\theta$, right-handed $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf k} &= \mathbf 0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k$ is the rotation axis and is fixed in $S_0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf e}_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R, \qquad \dot{\mathbf k} = \mathbf 0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the horizontal unit vectors turn with the meridian plane of the particle.

Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: velocity and acceleration as time derivatives of the position vector in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.30
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^P &= \frac{d}{dt}\left(R\,\mathbf e_R + R\,\mathbf k\right) = \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\,\dot{\mathbf e}_R + \dot R\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule; $\mathbf k$ constant)} \\ &= \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \dot R\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(derivative of $\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \ddot R\,\mathbf e_R + \dot R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \left(\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + R\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta + \ddot R\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= \left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_R + \left(2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + \ddot R\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(derivative of $\mathbf e_\theta$; terms grouped)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= \dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2 + \dot R^2 = 2\dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(orthonormal components)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P(0) &= a\,\dot\theta(0)\,\mathbf j = \sqrt{ag}\,\mathbf j \Rightarrow \dot\theta(0) = \sqrt{g/a}, \quad \dot R(0) = 0 \quad &\text{(at $t = 0$, $\mathbf e_\theta = \mathbf j$; comparison with the data)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf v_0^P = \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \dot R\,\mathbf k, \qquad \mathbf a_0^P = \left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_R + \left(2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + \ddot R\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: velocity $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$: $[\dot R] = [R\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; acceleration $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$: $[\ddot R] = [R\dot\theta^2] = [\dot R\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the velocity has no component along $\mathbf n$ (its $\mathbf e_R$ and $\mathbf k$ components are equal, so it lies in the tangent plane), as a stationary constraint requires; the initial velocity is horizontal, along the parallel.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram, drawn in the meridian plane, shows the weight and the normal reaction. The frame $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ during the circular motion: the weight $-mg\,\mathbf k$ and the reaction $N\mathbf n$ with $N = \sqrt2\,mg$. Their resultant is horizontal and points to the axis: it is the centripetal force of the circle of radius $a$..
Newton's second law with the reaction, projected on the surface basis Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with the constraint force; projection on the tangent and normal directions Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.11; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

The weight and the acceleration are written in $\mathcal B_2$ with the inverse relations of the surface basis, read from the rows of $[{}_1R_2]$.

$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf k + N\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{(weight plus normal reaction; 3 equations, unknowns $R$, $\theta$, $N$)} \\ \mathbf e_R &= \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\mathbf e_g - \mathbf n\right), \quad \mathbf k = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\mathbf e_g + \mathbf n\right) \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3 of $[{}_1R_2]$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf e_g &= \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2 + \ddot R\right) = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(2\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right) \quad &\text{(components on $\mathbf e_R$ and $\mathbf k$ projected)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf n &= \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(-\ddot R + R\dot\theta^2 + \ddot R\right) = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\,R\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(same, on $\mathbf n$)} \\ \mathbf e_g:\ \tfrac{m}{\sqrt2}\left(2\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right) &= -\tfrac{mg}{\sqrt2} \quad &\text{(tangent: $\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf e_g = 0$, the reaction drops out)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ m\left(2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{(tangent: neither the weight nor the reaction has an azimuthal part)} \\ \mathbf n:\ \tfrac{m}{\sqrt2}\,R\dot\theta^2 &= -\tfrac{mg}{\sqrt2} + N \quad &\text{(normal: it holds the reaction)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{2\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2 + g = 0, \qquad 2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta = 0, \qquad N = \frac{m}{\sqrt2}\left(g + R\dot\theta^2\right)}$$

Units: every term of the first two equations in $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[N] = [\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the two tangent projections are the equations of motion of the two degrees of freedom, free of the reaction; the normal projection gives the reaction once the motion is known (dynamical decoupling). Since $N$ is a sum of positive terms, the cone always pushes the particle towards the axis: the unilateral contact never opens.

First integral of the azimuthal equation Law: the azimuthal equation is an exact derivative; integration with Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.7; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.30
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(R^2\dot\theta\right) &= 2R\dot R\,\dot\theta + R^2\ddot\theta = R\left(2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right) \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $R^2$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(R^2\dot\theta\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{(azimuthal equation times $R$)} \\ \int_0^t \frac{d}{dt'}\left(R^2\dot\theta\right)dt' &= \left[R^2\dot\theta\right]_0^t = R^2\dot\theta - R(0)^2\dot\theta(0) = 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule between $0$ and $t$)} \\ R^2\dot\theta &= a^2\sqrt{g/a} = a\sqrt{ag} \equiv h \quad &\text{(initial data)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{R^2\dot\theta = h = a\sqrt{ag}, \qquad \dot\theta = \frac{a\sqrt{ag}}{R^2}}$$

Units: $[R^2\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-1}}]$; $[a\sqrt{ag}] = [\mathrm{L}][\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: no force has a moment about the vertical axis (the weight is vertical and the reaction lies in the meridian plane), so this areal quantity is constant: the particle turns faster when it is closer to the axis.

Energy integral derived from the meridian equation Law: the equation of motion multiplied by the rate of the coordinate is an exact derivative (chain rule); Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.12
$$\begin{aligned} R\dot\theta^2 &= R\,\frac{h^2}{R^4} = \frac{h^2}{R^3} \quad &\text{(first integral substituted)} \\ 2\dot R\ddot R - \frac{h^2}{R^3}\,\dot R + g\dot R &= 0 \quad &\text{(meridian equation times $\dot R$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\dot R^2\right) &= 2\dot R\ddot R, \quad \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{h^2}{2R^2}\right) = -\frac{h^2}{R^3}\,\dot R, \quad \frac{d}{dt}\left(gR\right) = g\dot R \quad &\text{(chain rule on each term)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\dot R^2 + \frac{h^2}{2R^2} + gR\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{(sum of the three derivatives)} \\ \left[\dot R^2 + \frac{h^2}{2R^2} + gR\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule between $0$ and $t$)} \\ \dot R^2 + \frac{h^2}{2R^2} + gR &= 0 + \frac{a^3 g}{2a^2} + ga = \tfrac32\,ga \quad &\text{($\dot R(0) = 0$, $R(0) = a$, $h^2 = a^3 g$)} \\ \tfrac12 m|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + mgz &= m\left(\dot R^2 + \frac{R^2\dot\theta^2}{2} + gR\right) = \tfrac32\,mga \quad &\text{(the same constant times $m$: kinetic plus potential energy)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot R^2 = \tfrac32\,ga - gR - \frac{a^3 g}{2R^2}}$$

Units: every term in $[\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$: $[ga] = [gR] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}]$; $[a^3 g/R^2] = [\mathrm{L^3}][\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{L^2}]$; energy $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the mechanical energy is conserved: the weight derives from a potential and the reaction of the stationary smooth cone does no work, since it is normal to the velocity.

Answers and Checks

Reduction to integrals

Quadratures of the motion Law: separation of variables in the energy integral; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.44; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.7

For any data of the same kind (energy constant $E$ per unit mass written as $e$, areal constant $h$, start at $R_0$), the two first integrals give the motion by two quadratures.

$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dR}{dt} &= \pm\sqrt{\Phi(R)}, \quad \Phi(R) = e - gR - \frac{h^2}{2R^2} \quad &\text{(energy integral solved for $\dot R$)} \\ \frac{dR'}{\pm\sqrt{\Phi(R')}} &= dt' \quad &\text{(separation of variables)} \\ \int_{R_0}^{R}\frac{dR'}{\pm\sqrt{\Phi(R')}} &= \int_0^t dt' = \left[t'\right]_0^t = t \quad &\text{(integration; Barrow's rule on the right)} \\ \theta(t) - \theta(0) &= \int_0^t \dot\theta\,dt' = \int_0^t \frac{h}{R(t')^2}\,dt' \quad &\text{(areal integral, Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{t = \int_{R_0}^{R}\frac{dR'}{\pm\sqrt{e - gR' - h^2/(2R'^2)}}, \qquad \theta(t) = \theta(0) + \int_0^t\frac{h\,dt'}{R(t')^2}, \qquad z(t) = R(t)}$$

Units: $[\Phi] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$, so $[dR/\sqrt\Phi] = [\mathrm{L}]/[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}] = [\mathrm{T}]$; $[h/R^2] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the radius, and with it the height, oscillates between the zeros of $\Phi$; the azimuth follows from the radius.

Maximum and minimum of z

Turning points of the radius for the data of the statement Law: the motion is possible only where the squared rate is not negative; factorisation of the cubic Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28; Problems.pdf, p. 10
$$\begin{aligned} \Phi(R) &= \tfrac32\,ga - gR - \frac{a^3 g}{2R^2} = -\frac{g}{2R^2}\left(2R^3 - 3aR^2 + a^3\right) \quad &\text{(common denominator $2R^2$)} \\ 2a^3 - 3a\cdot a^2 + a^3 &= 0 \quad &\text{($R = a$ is a root: the start)} \\ \left(R - a\right)\left(2R^2 - aR - a^2\right) &= 2R^3 - aR^2 - a^2R - 2aR^2 + a^2R + a^3 = 2R^3 - 3aR^2 + a^3 \quad &\text{(division by $R - a$, checked by expansion)} \\ 2R^2 - aR - a^2 &= \left(R - a\right)\left(2R + a\right) \quad &\text{(roots $a$ and $-a/2$ of the quadratic)} \\ \Phi(R) &= -\frac{g\left(2R + a\right)}{2R^2}\left(R - a\right)^2 \quad &\text{(double root at $a$)} \\ \Phi(R) &\le 0 \ \text{for } R \gt 0, \quad \Phi(R) = 0 \iff R = a \quad &\text{($2R + a \gt 0$, square not negative)} \\ \dot R^2 = \Phi(R) \ge 0 &\Rightarrow R(t) \equiv a \Rightarrow \dot\theta = \frac{a\sqrt{ag}}{a^2} = \sqrt{g/a} \quad &\text{(only the double root is accessible)} \\ \theta(t) &= \int_0^t\sqrt{g/a}\,dt' = \left[\sqrt{g/a}\,t'\right]_0^t = \sqrt{g/a}\,t \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule, $\theta(0) = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{R(t) \equiv a, \quad z_{\max} = z_{\min} = a, \quad \theta(t) = \sqrt{g/a}\,t}$$

Units: $[\Phi] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L^3}]/[\mathrm{L^2}] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$; $[\sqrt{g/a}] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the data are exactly those of a steady circular orbit: the particle runs round the horizontal circle of radius $a$ at height $a$ with constant speed $\sqrt{ag}$; the height neither rises nor falls.

Check of the circular motion and of the reaction Law: Newton's second law on the circular orbit; sign of the unilateral reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf a_0^P &= -a\,\frac{g}{a}\,\mathbf e_R = -g\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{($R = a$, $\dot R = \ddot R = 0$, $\ddot\theta = 0$, $\dot\theta^2 = g/a$)} \\ N &= \frac{m}{\sqrt2}\left(g + a\,\frac{g}{a}\right) = \sqrt2\,mg \quad &\text{(reaction formula)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf k + N\,\mathbf n &= -mg\,\mathbf k + \sqrt2\,mg\,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}\left(-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right) = -mg\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{(sum of the forces)} \\ m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{(equal: Newton's law holds)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf N = mg\left(-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k\right), \qquad |\mathbf N| = \sqrt2\,mg \gt 0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the vertical part of the reaction carries the weight and its horizontal part, pointing to the axis, supplies the centripetal force $m v^2/a$ with $v^2 = ag$; the cone pushes, so the contact holds.

Limiting case: a slightly perturbed start Law: linearisation of the meridian equation about the circular orbit Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.6; Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6

If the start radius is moved to $a$ plus a small $\rho$ with the same areal constant $h$, the meridian equation is linearised in $\rho$.

$$\begin{aligned} 2\ddot\rho &= \frac{h^2}{\left(a + \rho\right)^3} - g \quad &\text{(meridian equation with $R\dot\theta^2 = h^2/R^3$)} \\ \frac{h^2}{\left(a + \rho\right)^3} &\approx \frac{h^2}{a^3}\left(1 - \frac{3\rho}{a}\right) = g - \frac{3g}{a}\,\rho \quad &\text{(first-order Taylor expansion; $h^2 = a^3 g$)} \\ \ddot\rho + \frac{3g}{2a}\,\rho &= 0 \quad &\text{(the constant terms cancel; division by 2)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\omega_\rho = \sqrt{\frac{3g}{2a}}}$$

Units: $[\omega_\rho] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the orbit is stable: a small disturbance makes the height oscillate about $a$ instead of drifting away, so the extreme values of $z$ stay close to $a$.

Part of the statementAnswer
Equations of motion$2\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2 + g = 0$, $2\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta = 0$; reaction $N = \frac{m}{\sqrt2}(g + R\dot\theta^2)$ along $\mathbf n = (-\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k)/\sqrt2$
Integral equations$R^2\dot\theta = a\sqrt{ag}$; $\dot R^2 = \frac32 ga - gR - \frac{a^3 g}{2R^2}$; $t = \int_a^R dR'/\pm\sqrt{\Phi(R')}$, $\theta = \int_0^t h\,dt'/R^2$
Maximum and minimum of $z$$\Phi(R) = -\frac{g(2R + a)}{2R^2}(R - a)^2 \le 0$, so $R \equiv a$: $z_{\max} = z_{\min} = a$ (uniform circular motion, $\dot\theta = \sqrt{g/a}$, reaction $\sqrt2\,mg$)

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 73 lists item 18 with no answer, so there is no key to compare with. Independent check: the box of the circular motion substitutes the result back into Newton's law, and the linearised meridian equation shows the orbit is stable.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 19

Heavy Particle on a Paraboloid

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 10
Paraboloid SurfaceIn-Class Session 8

A heavy particle of mass $m$ is moving without friction on the paraboloid which is described as:

$$ az = r^2 $$

where $a$ is a positive constant and $r$ is the distance to the $Oz$ vertical axis. If $r = a$ initially, and the particle is released with a velocity $v_0$ tangent to the local parallel, reduce the problem to integrals of the form $\int [...] dr = \int [...] dt$. Determine the maximum and minimum of $r$ along the motion.

Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ slides without friction on the paraboloid of revolution of the statement, whose axis is the vertical $Oz$ and whose vertex is the origin. It starts on the parallel of radius $a$ with a velocity $v_0$ tangent to that parallel. The statement asks for the reduction to integrals of the form of an integral in $r$ equal to an integral in $t$, and for the extreme values of $r$ (Problems.pdf, p. 10). The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$, origin at the vertex of the paraboloid (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathbf k$ vertical upward (the paraboloid opens upwards since $a \gt 0$), right-handed
Cylindrical basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_r, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by the azimuth $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$ (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24)
Surface basis$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf e_m, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf n\}$: $\mathbf e_m$ tangent to the meridian parabola (upward), $\mathbf n$ normal to the surface towards the axis; $\mathcal B_1$ turned about $\mathbf e_\theta$ by the slope angle $\varphi$ of the meridian
Cylindrical coordinates$r$ (distance to the axis, the $r$ of the statement), $\theta$ (azimuth), $z$ (height) (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.23)
Constraint$f(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 - a z = 0$; in cylindrical coordinates $z = r^2/a$
Classificationholonomic, stationary, smooth (ideal); the particle rides on the inner (upper) face, a unilateral contact checked by the sign of the reaction ([Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom3 minus 1 surface constraint: 2, namely $r(t)$ and $\theta(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$], $a$ [m], $v_0$ [m s$^{-1}$]
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf k$
Reaction$\mathbf N = N\,\mathbf n$, one unknown component (Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4)
Initial conditions$r(0) = a$, $\theta(0) = 0$ (azimuth origin chosen at the start), $\dot r(0) = 0$ and $a\,\dot\theta(0) = v_0$ (velocity tangent to the parallel), $z(0) = a$
Hypothesesno friction (statement); $v_0 \gt 0$ (sense of the azimuth chosen along the initial velocity)
Figure 1. Meridian of the paraboloid, $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper. The slope triangle (legs $a$ and $2r$, hypotenuse $\sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}$) gives $\cos\varphi$ and $\sin\varphi$ of the tangent $\mathbf e_m$; the normal $\mathbf n$ leans towards the axis, hence its component $-\sin\varphi$ on $\mathbf e_r$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Seen from above, along $\mathbf k$, with $\mathbf i$ to the right and $\mathbf j$ up, $\mathbf k$ (the cross product of $\mathbf i$ and $\mathbf j$) points out of the paper (⊙) and a positive rotation about $\mathbf k$ is counterclockwise.

Figure 2. Rotation about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper); the opposite leg of the triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$ lies along $-\mathbf i$. Seen from above, the path stays in the ring between the parallels of radii $a$ and $r_\ast$..
Cylindrical basis: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View along $\mathbf k$ (⊙). $\mathbf e_r$ is $\mathbf i$ turned counterclockwise by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by the same angle: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf j$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on minus $\mathbf i$, since the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_r &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j, \quad \mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(the two right triangles)} \\ \mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= \cos\theta\cdot\cos\theta - \left(-\sin\theta\right)\sin\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row, unit entry)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1, \quad \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} = \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(rows times rows)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: the columns are $\mathbf e_r$, $\mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf k$ in $\mathcal B_0$; $\mathbf e_r$ points from the axis to the particle.

Normal and meridian tangent of the paraboloid Law: the gradient of the implicit equation is normal to the surface; the meridian tangent completes the right-handed triad Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.9; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.10; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3

Meridian section through $P$ (view with $\mathbf e_r$ to the right and $\mathbf k$ up, $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper ⊗, since $\mathbf e_\theta$ is the cross product of $\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf e_r$). The slope of the meridian $z = r^2/a$ is $2r/a$; the slope triangle has horizontal leg $a$, vertical leg $2r$ and hypotenuse $D = \sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}$, so the slope angle $\varphi$ has $\cos\varphi = a/D$ (adjacent over hypotenuse) and $\sin\varphi = 2r/D$ (opposite over hypotenuse). The tangent $\mathbf e_m$ is $\mathbf e_r$ turned counterclockwise by $\varphi$; with the axis $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper a positive rotation is clockwise, so this is Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19 with the angle $-\varphi$. The normal $\mathbf n$ is $\mathbf k$ turned by the same angle and leans towards the axis, minus $\mathbf e_r$: the minus sign.

$$\begin{aligned} \nabla f &= 2x\,\mathbf i + 2y\,\mathbf j - a\,\mathbf k = 2r\,\mathbf e_r - a\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(partial derivatives; $x = r\cos\theta$, $y = r\sin\theta$)} \\ |\nabla f| &= \sqrt{4r^2 + a^2} = D \quad &\text{(orthogonal components)} \\ \mathbf n &= -\frac{\nabla f}{D} = -\frac{2r}{D}\,\mathbf e_r + \frac{a}{D}\,\mathbf k = -\sin\varphi\,\mathbf e_r + \cos\varphi\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(sign chosen upwards, towards the axis)} \\ \mathbf e_m &= \cos\varphi\,\mathbf e_r + \sin\varphi\,\mathbf k = \frac{a}{D}\,\mathbf e_r + \frac{2r}{D}\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(slope triangle: adjacent leg on $\mathbf e_r$, opposite on $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf e_m\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \cos\varphi\,\mathbf k + \sin\varphi\left(-\mathbf e_r\right) = \mathbf n \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta = \mathbf k$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_r$: right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= \cos\varphi\cos\varphi - 0 + \left(-\sin\varphi\right)\left(-\sin\varphi\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{13} &= \cos\varphi\sin\varphi - \sin\varphi\cos\varphi = 0, \quad \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{11} = 1 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf e_\theta$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \tfrac{a}{D} & 0 & -\tfrac{2r}{D} \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr \tfrac{2r}{D} & 0 & \tfrac{a}{D} \end{pmatrix}, \quad D = \sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}, \quad \det[{}_1R_2] = 1}$$

Units: $[\nabla f] = [\mathrm{L}]$; $[D] = [\mathrm{L}]$; the entries are ratios of lengths, dimensionless

Meaning: $\mathbf e_m$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ span the tangent plane $V_T$ at $P$ and $\mathbf n$ the normal line $V_N$; the basis turns with the particle and tilts more as it climbs.

Position vector of the particle Law: position vector in cylindrical coordinates with the constraint substituted Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.28
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= r\,\mathbf e_r + z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(horizontal and vertical legs in the meridian plane)} \\ &= r\,\mathbf e_r + \frac{r^2}{a}\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(constraint substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = r\,\mathbf e_r + \frac{r^2}{a}\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[r^2/a] = [\mathrm{L^2}]/[\mathrm{L}] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the two coordinates $r$ and $\theta$ parametrise the paraboloid, so the constraint holds identically.

Kinematics

Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; derivatives of the unit vectors of a basis that turns about $\mathbf k$; chain and product rules Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.30
$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathbf e}_r &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_r = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_r \quad &\text{($\boldsymbol\omega_{10} = \dot\theta\,\mathbf k$)} \\ \dot z &= \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{r^2}{a}\right) = \frac{2r}{a}\,\dot r \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \ddot z &= \frac{2}{a}\left(\dot r\,\dot r + r\,\ddot r\right) = \frac{2}{a}\left(\dot r^2 + r\ddot r\right) \quad &\text{(product rule on $r\dot r$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \dot r\,\mathbf e_r + r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \frac{2r\dot r}{a}\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule on $r\,\mathbf e_r$; $\mathbf k$ constant)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \ddot r\,\mathbf e_r + \dot r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \left(\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta - r\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r + \ddot z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= \left(\ddot r - r\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_r + \left(2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + \frac{2}{a}\left(\dot r^2 + r\ddot r\right)\mathbf k \quad &\text{(grouped)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= \dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + r^2\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(orthonormal components)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf v_0^P = \dot r\,\mathbf e_r + r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \frac{2r\dot r}{a}\,\mathbf k, \qquad \mathbf a_0^P = \left(\ddot r - r\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_r + \left(2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + \frac{2}{a}\left(\dot r^2 + r\ddot r\right)\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[\dot r] = [r\dot\theta] = [r\dot r/a] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$, e.g. $[\dot r^2/a] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the velocity is tangent to the surface: its $\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf k$ components are in the ratio $a$ to $2r$, the direction of $\mathbf e_m$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram in the meridian plane shows the weight and the normal reaction; $S_0$ is inertial, so there are no inertia forces.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ on the parallel of radius $a$ when $v_0^2 = 2ga$: the weight and the reaction $N\mathbf n$ with $N = \sqrt5\,mg$; the vertical part of the reaction carries the weight, its horizontal part $2mg$ is the centripetal force..
Newton's second law with the reaction, projected on the surface basis Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with the constraint force; tangent projections free of the reaction, normal projection for the reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.11; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

Write the acceleration components as $A_r$, $A_\theta$, $A_z$ (those of the kinematics box).

$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf k + N\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{(all forces: weight and reaction)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf e_m &= \frac{a A_r + 2r A_z}{D}, \quad \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf n = \frac{-2r A_r + a A_z}{D} \quad &\text{(components of $\mathbf e_m$, $\mathbf n$ in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ \mathbf e_m:\ m\left[a\left(\ddot r - r\dot\theta^2\right) + \frac{4r}{a}\left(\dot r^2 + r\ddot r\right)\right] &= -2mgr \quad &\text{($-mg\,\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf e_m = -2mgr/D$; times $D$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ m\left(2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{(no azimuthal force)} \\ -2r\left(\ddot r - r\dot\theta^2\right) + 2\left(\dot r^2 + r\ddot r\right) &= 2r^2\dot\theta^2 + 2\dot r^2 \quad &\text{(the $r\ddot r$ terms cancel in $D\,\mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf n$)} \\ \mathbf n:\ \frac{m}{D}\left(2r^2\dot\theta^2 + 2\dot r^2\right) &= -\frac{mga}{D} + N \quad &\text{($-mg\,\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf n = -mga/D$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\ddot r\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + \frac{4r\dot r^2}{a^2} - r\dot\theta^2 + \frac{2g}{a}\,r = 0, \quad 2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta = 0, \quad N = \frac{m\left(ga + 2\dot r^2 + 2r^2\dot\theta^2\right)}{\sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}}}$$

Units: first equation divided by $a$: every term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[N] = [\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{L}] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two equations of motion free of the reaction for the two degrees of freedom, and the reaction afterwards; $N$ is always positive, so the surface always pushes and the particle never leaves it.

Areal first integral Law: the azimuthal equation is an exact derivative; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.7; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.30
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(r^2\dot\theta\right) &= 2r\dot r\dot\theta + r^2\ddot\theta = r\left(2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta\right) = 0 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules; azimuthal equation)} \\ \left[r^2\dot\theta\right]_0^t &= r^2\dot\theta - a^2\,\frac{v_0}{a} = 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule; $\dot\theta(0) = v_0/a$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{r^2\dot\theta = a v_0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-1}}]$ on both sides

Meaning: no force has a moment about the axis $Oz$, so the azimuthal rate grows as the particle approaches the axis.

Energy integral derived from the meridian equation Law: the equation of motion multiplied by the rate of the coordinate is an exact derivative (chain rule); Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.12
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left[\dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right)\right] &= 2\dot r\ddot r\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + \frac{8r\dot r^3}{a^2} \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $r^2$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{a^2 v_0^2}{r^2}\right) &= -\frac{2a^2v_0^2}{r^3}\,\dot r = -2r\dot\theta^2\,\dot r \quad &\text{(chain rule; $\dot\theta = a v_0/r^2$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{2g}{a}\,r^2\right) &= \frac{4g}{a}\,r\dot r \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ 2\dot r\times\left[\text{meridian equation}\right] &= \frac{d}{dt}\left[\dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + \frac{a^2v_0^2}{r^2} + \frac{2g}{a}\,r^2\right] = 0 \quad &\text{(sum of the three derivatives)} \\ \left[\dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + r^2\dot\theta^2 + \frac{2g}{a}\,r^2\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule; $a^2v_0^2/r^2 = r^2\dot\theta^2$)} \\ \dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + r^2\dot\theta^2 + \frac{2g}{a}\,r^2 &= 0 + v_0^2 + 2ga \quad &\text{(initial data)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) + r^2\left(\dot\theta^2 + \frac{2g}{a}\right) = v_0^2 + 2ga}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$: $[g r^2/a] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: it is twice the mechanical energy per unit mass, $|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + 2gz$: the reaction of the stationary smooth surface does no work and the weight derives from the potential $mgz$.

Answers and Checks

Reduction to quadratures

Integral in r equal to an integral in t Law: elimination of the azimuthal rate with the areal integral; factorisation; separation of variables and Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.44; Problems.pdf, p. 10

Call $r_\ast$ the length with $r_\ast^2 = a v_0^2/(2g)$.

$$\begin{aligned} \dot r^2\left(1 + \frac{4r^2}{a^2}\right) &= v_0^2 + 2ga - \frac{a^2v_0^2}{r^2} - \frac{2g}{a}\,r^2 \quad &\text{(areal integral substituted)} \\ &= -\frac{2g}{a r^2}\left[r^4 - \left(a^2 + \frac{a v_0^2}{2g}\right)r^2 + \frac{a^3v_0^2}{2g}\right] \quad &\text{(common factor $-2g/(a r^2)$)} \\ &= \frac{2g}{a r^2}\left(a^2 - r^2\right)\left(r^2 - r_\ast^2\right) \quad &\text{(quadratic in $r^2$: roots $a^2$ and $r_\ast^2$, sum and product checked)} \\ \dot r^2 &= \frac{2ga\left(a^2 - r^2\right)\left(r^2 - r_\ast^2\right)}{r^2\left(a^2 + 4r^2\right)} \quad &\text{(division by $1 + 4r^2/a^2$)} \\ \int_a^{r}\frac{r'\sqrt{a^2 + 4r'^2}}{\sqrt{2ga\left(a^2 - r'^2\right)\left(r'^2 - r_\ast^2\right)}}\,dr' &= \pm\int_0^t dt' = \pm t \quad &\text{(separation of variables; Barrow's rule from $r(0) = a$)} \\ \theta(t) &= \int_0^t \frac{a v_0}{r(t')^2}\,dt' \quad &\text{(areal integral; Barrow's rule, $\theta(0) = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\int_a^{r}\frac{r'\sqrt{a^2 + 4r'^2}\,dr'}{\sqrt{2ga\left(a^2 - r'^2\right)\left(r'^2 - r_\ast^2\right)}} = \pm t, \qquad \theta = \int_0^t\frac{a v_0\,dt'}{r^2}, \qquad r_\ast = v_0\sqrt{\frac{a}{2g}}}$$

Units: $[r\sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}\,dr] = [\mathrm{L^3}]$; $[\sqrt{ga\,\mathrm{L^4}}] = [\mathrm{L^3\,T^{-1}}]$; the ratio is $[\mathrm{T}]$; $[r_\ast] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}][\mathrm{T}] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the radicand is positive only between $a$ and $r_\ast$: the radius oscillates between these two values, which are the turning points.

Maximum and minimum of r

Turning points in the three cases Law: the motion is confined where the squared rate is not negative Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28
$$\begin{aligned} \left(a^2 - r^2\right)\left(r^2 - r_\ast^2\right) &\ge 0 \iff \min(a, r_\ast) \le r \le \max(a, r_\ast) \quad &\text{(both factors must have the same sign)} \\ r_\ast \lt a &\iff v_0^2 \lt 2ga \quad &\text{($r_\ast^2 = a v_0^2/(2g)$ compared with $a^2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{v_0 \lt \sqrt{2ga}:\ r_{\max} = a,\ r_{\min} = v_0\sqrt{\tfrac{a}{2g}}; \quad v_0 = \sqrt{2ga}:\ r \equiv a; \quad v_0 \gt \sqrt{2ga}:\ r_{\min} = a,\ r_{\max} = v_0\sqrt{\tfrac{a}{2g}}}$$

Units: $[\sqrt{ga}] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, a speed, as $v_0$

Meaning: slower than the circular speed the particle slides down from the start parallel and comes back; faster, it climbs; at exactly $\sqrt{2ga}$ it stays on the parallel of radius $a$.

Check: the circular orbit and its reaction Law: Newton's second law on the parallel of radius a Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} -a\dot\theta^2 + \frac{2g}{a}\,a &= 0 \Rightarrow a^2\dot\theta^2 = 2ga \quad &\text{(meridian equation with $r = a$, $\dot r = \ddot r = 0$)} \\ N &= \frac{m\left(ga + 0 + 2\cdot 2ga\right)}{\sqrt{a^2 + 4a^2}} = \frac{5mga}{\sqrt5\,a} = \sqrt5\,mg \quad &\text{(reaction formula with $a^2\dot\theta^2 = 2ga$)} \\ N\,\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf k &= \sqrt5\,mg\,\frac{a}{\sqrt5\,a} = mg, \quad N\,\mathbf n\cdot\left(-\mathbf e_r\right) = \sqrt5\,mg\,\frac{2a}{\sqrt5\,a} = 2mg \quad &\text{(components of the reaction)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{v_0^2 = 2ga, \qquad N = \sqrt5\,mg: \ \text{vertical part } mg,\ \text{inward part } 2mg = \frac{m v_0^2}{a}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the vertical part of the reaction carries the weight and its inward part is the centripetal force; this agrees with the double root of the quadrature.

Limiting case: no initial velocity Law: behaviour of the turning points when the speed vanishes Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28
$$\begin{aligned} r_\ast &= v_0\sqrt{\frac{a}{2g}} \to 0 \quad &\text{($v_0 \to 0$)} \\ r^2\dot\theta &= a v_0 \to 0 \quad &\text{(the areal constant vanishes)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{v_0 = 0:\ \dot\theta \equiv 0,\ 0 \le r \le a,\ \text{oscillation through the vertex in a fixed meridian plane}}$$

Units: lengths $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: without azimuthal speed nothing keeps the particle away from the axis, so it slides through the vertex in a vertical plane, like a bead on the meridian parabola; for small $v_0 \gt 0$ the rate at the inner turning radius, $a v_0/r_\ast^2 = 2g/v_0$, is large, so the particle swings round the axis by nearly $\pi$ each time it passes close to it.

Part of the statementAnswer
Equations of motion$\ddot r(1 + 4r^2/a^2) + 4r\dot r^2/a^2 - r\dot\theta^2 + 2gr/a = 0$; $2\dot r\dot\theta + r\ddot\theta = 0$; $N = m(ga + 2\dot r^2 + 2r^2\dot\theta^2)/\sqrt{a^2 + 4r^2}$ along $\mathbf n$
First integrals$r^2\dot\theta = a v_0$; $\dot r^2(1 + 4r^2/a^2) + r^2(\dot\theta^2 + 2g/a) = v_0^2 + 2ga$
Quadrature$\int_a^r \frac{r'\sqrt{a^2 + 4r'^2}\,dr'}{\sqrt{2ga(a^2 - r'^2)(r'^2 - r_\ast^2)}} = \pm t$, with $r_\ast = v_0\sqrt{a/(2g)}$; $\theta = \int_0^t a v_0\,dt'/r^2$
Maximum and minimum of $r$$v_0 \lt \sqrt{2ga}$: $r_{\max} = a$, $r_{\min} = r_\ast$; $v_0 = \sqrt{2ga}$: $r \equiv a$; $v_0 \gt \sqrt{2ga}$: $r_{\min} = a$, $r_{\max} = r_\ast$

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 73, item 19, prints the energy quadrature and the areal integral of the boxes above, identical term by term, and the same three cases for the extreme radii: match. The key prints the second turning radius as $v_0\sqrt{a/2g}$, the same value.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 23

Simple Pendulum (Unilateral Constraint)

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 11
Pendulum & Liftoff

A simple pendulum consists of a mass $m$ hanging from a string of length $L$ and fixed at a pivot point $O$. The simple pendulum is equivalent to the problem of a heavy particle constrained to move along a smooth circumference. A unilateral constraint is considered, such that the particle move [sic] along the interior of the circumference and it might fall under certain conditions.

Analyze the motion of the particle when it is pushed from the lowest point of the circumference with velocity $v_0$. At what point does the particle leave the circumference? Is this point ever reached?

If the circumference rotates around the vertical axis with a constant angular velocity equal to $\omega$, write down the new equations of motion. Compute the locations along the circumference in which the particle is in equilibrium.

Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ moves on the inside of a smooth vertical circumference of radius $L$ and centre $O$, which is the simple pendulum with a string that can go slack. The statement asks, first with the circumference at rest, for the motion after a push $v_0$ at the lowest point, the point where the particle leaves the circumference and whether that point is reached; then, with the circumference turning about the vertical diameter at the constant rate $\omega$, for the equations of motion and the equilibrium positions (Problems.pdf, p. 11). The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7 and writes both parts at once: the fixed circumference is the case of zero $\omega$.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$, origin at the centre of the circumference (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathbf j$ vertical upward (the axis of the turning diameter), $\mathbf i$ and $\mathbf k$ horizontal, right-handed; the plane $Oxy$ holds the circumference at $t = 0$, as in Notes.pdf, Fig. 4.3
Basis of the plane of the circumference$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j, \mathbf k_1\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by the angle $\omega t$ about $\mathbf j$; the circumference lies in the plane of $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf j$ (for zero $\omega$, $\mathcal B_1$ is $\mathcal B_0$)
Polar basis$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k_1\}$: $\mathbf e_R$ from $O$ to $P$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ along increasing $\theta$, $\mathbf k_1$ normal to the plane
Coordinate$\theta$, the angle of $OP$ from the downward vertical, positive towards $\mathbf i_1$ (the coordinate of Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7)
Constraints(1) plane of the circumference: $\mathbf r_0^P\cdot\mathbf k_1(t) = 0$, holonomic, bilateral, stationary for zero $\omega$ and rheonomic (time-dependent) when it turns; (2) circumference: $|\mathbf r_0^P| = L$ while in contact, holonomic, stationary, unilateral (the particle is inside and may move towards $O$, $|\mathbf r_0^P| \le L$) ([Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom3 minus 2 constraints: one unknown function, $\theta(t)$. The rheonomic plane makes time appear explicitly (the lecture counts $t$ as an extra coordinate, [Lecture 3, Slide 21]), but $\omega t$ is prescribed, so $\theta$ is the only coordinate to solve for
Data$m$ [kg], $L$ [m], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$], $v_0$ [m s$^{-1}$], $\omega$ [rad s$^{-1}$]
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf j$
Reactionnormal subspace of a curve, dimension 2 (Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.16): $\mathbf N = -N\,\mathbf e_R + N_b\,\mathbf k_1$, with $N \ge 0$ (the circumference pushes towards $O$) and $N_b$ of either sign (the plane holds the particle on both sides)
Initial conditions (fixed circumference)$\theta(0) = 0$ (lowest point), $L\dot\theta(0) = v_0 \gt 0$
Hypothesessmooth contact; the reaction of the inner face can only push towards the centre, so the particle leaves the circumference when $N$ reaches zero (Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4)
Figure 1. Plane of the circumference, $\mathbf k_1$ out of the paper. $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical, so the polar angle from $\mathbf i_1$ is $\theta - \pi/2$; the triangle (hypotenuse $L$, legs $L\sin\theta$ and $L\cos\theta$) gives $\mathbf e_R = \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf j$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotation of the plane about $\mathbf j$ (out of the paper). The triangle of $\mathbf i_1$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf k$, the minus sign of $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\omega t\,\mathbf i - \sin\omega t\,\mathbf k$; the circumference is seen edge-on along $\mathbf i_1$..
Plane of the circumference: rotation about the vertical j Law: elementary rotation about the second axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19

View from above, along $\mathbf j$: since $\mathbf j$ is the cross product of $\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf i$, drawing $\mathbf k$ to the right and $\mathbf i$ up makes $\mathbf j$ point out of the paper (⊙), and a positive rotation about $\mathbf j$ is counterclockwise. The plane turns counterclockwise by $\omega t$. The vector $\mathbf i_1$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\omega t$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\omega t$ along $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\omega t$ along minus $\mathbf k$, because the upward vector of the view turned counterclockwise leans to the left, towards minus $\mathbf k$ (the minus sign). The vector $\mathbf k_1$ is $\mathbf k$ turned by the same angle: adjacent leg $\cos\omega t$ along $\mathbf k$, opposite leg $\sin\omega t$ along $\mathbf i$.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i_1 &= \cos\omega t\,\mathbf i - \sin\omega t\,\mathbf k, \quad \mathbf k_1 = \sin\omega t\,\mathbf i + \cos\omega t\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(the two right triangles of the view)} \\ \mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j &= \cos\omega t\,\mathbf k - \sin\omega t\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf j = \cos\omega t\,\mathbf k + \sin\omega t\,\mathbf i = \mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right-handed: $\mathbf k\times\mathbf j = -\mathbf i$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= \cos\omega t\left(1\cdot\cos\omega t - 0\right) - 0 + \sin\omega t\left(0 - 1\cdot\left(-\sin\omega t\right)\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\omega t + \sin^2\omega t = 1, \quad \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{13} = -\cos\omega t\sin\omega t + \sin\omega t\cos\omega t = 0 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf j$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\omega t & 0 & \sin\omega t \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr -\sin\omega t & 0 & \cos\omega t \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines; $[\omega t] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}][\mathrm{T}] = [1]$

Meaning: the columns are $\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf j$, $\mathbf k_1$ written in $\mathcal B_0$; for zero $\omega$ the matrix is the identity and the circumference stays in the plane $Oxy$.

Polar basis in the plane: angle from the downward vertical Law: elementary rotation about the third axis, with the angle related to the usual polar angle Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24; Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7

View along $\mathbf k_1$, with $\mathbf i_1$ to the right and $\mathbf j$ up: $\mathbf k_1$, the cross product of $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf j$, points out of the paper (⊙), and positive angles are counterclockwise. The angle $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical, minus $\mathbf j$, so the usual polar angle of $OP$, measured from $\mathbf i_1$, is $\theta - \pi/2$, and $\mathbf e_R$ is Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20 with that angle. The right triangle of $OP$ has hypotenuse $L$, the horizontal leg $L\sin\theta$ (opposite to $\theta$, along $\mathbf i_1$) and the vertical leg $L\cos\theta$ (adjacent to $\theta$, along minus $\mathbf j$): this is why $\mathbf e_R$ has a minus sign on $\mathbf j$. The vector $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf e_R$ turned by a quarter turn counterclockwise.

$$\begin{aligned} \cos\left(\theta - \tfrac{\pi}{2}\right) &= \cos\theta\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2} + \sin\theta\sin\tfrac{\pi}{2} = \sin\theta \quad &\text{(cosine of a difference)} \\ \sin\left(\theta - \tfrac{\pi}{2}\right) &= \sin\theta\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2} - \cos\theta\sin\tfrac{\pi}{2} = -\cos\theta \quad &\text{(sine of a difference)} \\ \mathbf e_R &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(opposite leg along $\mathbf i_1$, adjacent leg along $-\mathbf j$, over $L$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_R$ turned by $\pi/2$ counterclockwise)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \sin^2\theta\,\mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j - \cos^2\theta\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf i_1 = \left(\sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta\right)\mathbf k_1 = \mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= 1\cdot\left(\sin\theta\cdot\sin\theta - \cos\theta\cdot\left(-\cos\theta\right)\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{12} &= -\sin\theta\cos\theta + \cos\theta\sin\theta = 0, \quad \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{11} = \sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(rows times rows)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr -\cos\theta & \sin\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \mathbf i_1 = \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \mathbf j = -\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: at the lowest point $\mathbf e_R$ is minus $\mathbf j$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf i_1$; the inverse relations, read from the rows, are used to project the weight.

Position vector of the particle Law: position vector of a point on a circumference of centre O Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.33
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OP} = L\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{(both constraints substituted: radius $L$, in the plane)} \\ &= L\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 - L\cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(legs of the right triangle)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = L\,\mathbf e_R = L\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 - L\cos\theta\,\mathbf j}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: one coordinate $\theta$ fixes the particle while it is in contact; the height of $P$ above $O$ is $-L\cos\theta$.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the unit vectors Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis; chain and product rules Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26
$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathbf i}_2 &= \omega\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf i_1 = -\omega\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf k}_2 = \omega\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf k_1 = \omega\,\mathbf i_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf j} = \mathbf 0 \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ turns about $\mathbf j$ at the rate $\omega$; $\mathbf j\times\mathbf i_1 = -\mathbf k_1$, $\mathbf j\times\mathbf k_1 = \mathbf i_1$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_R &= \cos\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \sin\theta\,\dot{\mathbf i}_2 + \sin\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $\sin\theta$, $-\cos\theta$)} \\ &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - \omega\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(grouped with $\mathbf e_\theta$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta &= -\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\theta\,\dot{\mathbf i}_2 + \cos\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf j = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R - \omega\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(same rules)} \\ \dot{\mathbf k}_2 &= \omega\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\right) \quad &\text{($\mathbf i_1$ written in $\mathcal B_2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf e}_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - \omega\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R - \omega\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf k}_2 = \omega\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\right)}$$

Units: $[\dot\theta] = [\omega] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: besides turning with $\theta$ inside the plane, the polar vectors are dragged out of the plane by the rotation of the circumference.

Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: time derivatives of the position vector in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.34; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.35
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^P &= L\,\dot{\mathbf e}_R = L\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - L\omega\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{($L$ constant)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= L\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + L\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta - L\omega\cos\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf k_1 - L\omega\sin\theta\,\dot{\mathbf k}_2 \quad &\text{(product rule; $\omega$ constant; chain rule on $\sin\theta$)} \\ &= L\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - L\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R - 2L\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1 - L\omega^2\sin\theta\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\right) \quad &\text{(derivatives of the unit vectors)} \\ &= -L\left(\dot\theta^2 + \omega^2\sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf e_R + L\left(\ddot\theta - \omega^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta - 2L\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(grouped)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf v_0^P = L\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - L\omega\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \mathbf a_0^P = -L\left(\dot\theta^2 + \omega^2\sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf e_R + L\left(\ddot\theta - \omega^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta - 2L\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1}$$

Units: $[L\dot\theta] = [L\omega] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: for zero $\omega$ these are Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.34 and Eq. 4.35; with rotation the particle is also carried round the vertical axis on a circle of radius $L\sin\theta$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram shows the weight, the reaction of the circumference towards $O$ and the reaction of the turning plane, normal to it. The frame $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$: the weight, whose components are $mg\cos\theta$ outwards and $mg\sin\theta$ against the motion, the inward reaction $-N\mathbf e_R$ of the circumference, which must stay non-negative, and the reaction $N_b\mathbf k_1$ of the turning plane..
Newton's second law with all the reactions, projected on the polar basis Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with the constraint forces of a curve; tangent projection free of reactions Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.36; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.16
$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf j - N\,\mathbf e_R + N_b\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(weight and the two reaction components; unknowns $\theta$, $N$, $N_b$)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j &= mg\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf j$ in $\mathcal B_2$, second row of $[{}_1R_2]$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ mL\left(\ddot\theta - \omega^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\right) &= -mg\sin\theta \quad &\text{(tangent: no reaction)} \\ \mathbf e_R:\ -mL\left(\dot\theta^2 + \omega^2\sin^2\theta\right) &= mg\cos\theta - N \quad &\text{(normal in the plane)} \\ \mathbf k_1:\ -2mL\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta &= N_b \quad &\text{(normal to the plane)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\ddot\theta + \left(\frac{g}{L} - \omega^2\cos\theta\right)\sin\theta = 0, \quad N = mL\left(\dot\theta^2 + \omega^2\sin^2\theta\right) + mg\cos\theta, \quad N_b = -2mL\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta}$$

Units: $[g/L] = [\omega^2] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$, as $[\ddot\theta]$; $[N] = [N_b] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: these are the new equations of motion asked for the turning circumference; for zero $\omega$ they reduce to the pendulum of Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.37 and Eq. 4.38 (with $N$ in the role of the tension) and the plane exerts no force (Eq. 4.37 is printed with $+F_t$; with the tension $-F_t\,\mathbf e_R$ of the notes the term is $-F_t$, as $-N$ here).

Fixed circumference

Energy integral derived from the pendulum equation Law: the tangent equation multiplied by the angular rate is an exact derivative; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.39; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.42; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.12
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\theta\ddot\theta + \frac{g}{L}\sin\theta\,\dot\theta &= 0 \quad &\text{(zero $\omega$; equation times $\dot\theta$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12\dot\theta^2\right) &= \dot\theta\ddot\theta, \quad \frac{d}{dt}\left(-\frac{g}{L}\cos\theta\right) = \frac{g}{L}\sin\theta\,\dot\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\tfrac12\dot\theta^2 - \frac{g}{L}\cos\theta\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule between $0$ and $t$)} \\ \tfrac12\dot\theta^2 - \frac{g}{L}\cos\theta &= \frac{v_0^2}{2L^2} - \frac{g}{L} \quad &\text{($\theta(0) = 0$, $\dot\theta(0) = v_0/L$)} \\ L^2\dot\theta^2 &= v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta\right) \quad &\text{(times $2L^2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{v^2(\theta) = L^2\dot\theta^2 = v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta\right)}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the mechanical energy is conserved: the reactions of the fixed smooth circumference are normal to the velocity and do no work; the speed falls as the particle rises.

Motion reduced to a quadrature Law: separation of variables; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.44
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d\theta}{dt} &= \frac{1}{L}\sqrt{v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta\right)} \quad &\text{(energy integral, $\dot\theta \gt 0$ after the push)} \\ \int_0^{\theta}\frac{L\,d\theta'}{\sqrt{v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta'\right)}} &= \int_0^t dt' = \left[t'\right]_0^t = t \quad &\text{(separation of variables; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{t(\theta) = \int_0^{\theta}\frac{L\,d\theta'}{\sqrt{v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta'\right)}}}$$

Units: $[L\,d\theta/v] = [\mathrm{L}]/[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}] = [\mathrm{T}]$

Meaning: valid while the particle is in contact and moves forward; the denominator vanishes at a turning point and the reaction formula tells whether contact is lost first.

Reaction of the circumference and the point where it vanishes Law: the normal projection with the energy integral; separation from a unilateral constraint when the reaction vanishes Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.37; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]
$$\begin{aligned} N &= mL\dot\theta^2 + mg\cos\theta = \frac{m}{L}\left[v_0^2 - 2gL\left(1 - \cos\theta\right)\right] + mg\cos\theta \quad &\text{(zero $\omega$; energy integral)} \\ &= \frac{m v_0^2}{L} - mg\left(2 - 3\cos\theta\right) \quad &\text{(terms in $mg$ collected)} \\ \frac{dN}{d\theta} &= -3mg\sin\theta \lt 0 \ \text{for } 0 \lt \theta \lt \pi \quad &\text{(the reaction decreases as the particle rises)} \\ N(\theta_d) &= 0 \iff \cos\theta_d = \frac{2gL - v_0^2}{3gL} \quad &\text{(separation condition)} \\ v^2(\theta_d) &= v_0^2 - 2gL + 2gL\,\frac{2gL - v_0^2}{3gL} = \frac{v_0^2 - 2gL}{3} \quad &\text{(energy integral at $\theta_d$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{N(\theta) = \frac{m v_0^2}{L} - mg\left(2 - 3\cos\theta\right), \qquad \cos\theta_d = \frac{2gL - v_0^2}{3gL}}$$

Units: $[m v_0^2/L] = [mg] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$; $\cos\theta_d$ dimensionless: $[gL] = [v_0^2] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: contact is lost where the inward reaction would have to become a pull; it can only happen in the upper half, where the weight has an inward component (Eq. 4.37 is printed with $+F_t$; with the tension $-F_t\,\mathbf e_R$ of the notes the term is $-F_t$, as $-N$ here).

Is the separation point reached? The three regimes Law: the particle reaches a point only if the speed squared stays positive up to it; sign of the reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.43; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4

The speed vanishes at the angle with $\cos\theta_\ast = 1 - v_0^2/(2gL)$ (energy integral equal to zero).

$$\begin{aligned} v_0^2 \le 2gL &\Rightarrow \cos\theta_\ast \ge 0, \quad N(\theta_\ast) = 0 + mg\cos\theta_\ast \ge 0 \quad &\text{(the particle stops in the lower half while still pressed)} \\ 2gL \lt v_0^2 \lt 5gL &\Rightarrow -1 \lt \cos\theta_d \lt 0, \quad v^2(\theta_d) = \frac{v_0^2 - 2gL}{3} \gt 0 \quad &\text{(separation in the upper half, reached with speed)} \\ v_0^2 \ge 5gL &\Rightarrow N(\pi) = \frac{m v_0^2}{L} - 5mg \ge 0, \quad N(\theta) \ge N(\pi) \quad &\text{(the reaction never vanishes: complete turns)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{ll} v_0 \le \sqrt{2gL}: & \text{oscillation in the lower half, } \theta_d \text{ never reached} \cr \sqrt{2gL} \lt v_0 \lt \sqrt{5gL}: & \text{the particle leaves at } \theta_d = \arccos\dfrac{2gL - v_0^2}{3gL} \in \left(\tfrac{\pi}{2}, \pi\right) \cr v_0 \ge \sqrt{5gL}: & \text{complete loops, never leaves} \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\sqrt{gL}] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, a speed

Meaning: a small push gives the classical pendulum; a medium push lifts the particle above the horizontal diameter, where it falls off the circumference and continues as a projectile under gravity alone until it meets the circumference again; only a push of at least $\sqrt{5gL}$ keeps it in contact round the top.

Turning circumference

First integral of the turning case and the work of the plane Law: the tangent equation multiplied by the angular rate (chain rule); power of a time-dependent constraint Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\theta\ddot\theta + \frac{g}{L}\sin\theta\,\dot\theta - \omega^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\,\dot\theta &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of motion times $\dot\theta$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(-\tfrac12\omega^2\sin^2\theta\right) &= -\omega^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\,\dot\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\tfrac12\dot\theta^2 - \frac{g}{L}\cos\theta - \tfrac12\omega^2\sin^2\theta\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(sum of exact derivatives; Barrow's rule)} \\ \mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= N_b\,\mathbf k_1\cdot\left(-L\omega\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) = 2mL^2\omega^2\dot\theta\sin\theta\cos\theta \quad &\text{(power of the reaction: only the plane works)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\tfrac12\dot\theta^2 - \frac{g}{L}\cos\theta - \tfrac12\omega^2\sin^2\theta = \text{const}, \qquad \mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v_0^P = 2mL^2\omega^2\dot\theta\sin\theta\cos\theta \ne 0}$$

Units: first integral $[\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$ in every term; power $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-3}}]$

Meaning: the turning plane is a rheonomic constraint and does work, so the mechanical energy in $S_0$ is not conserved; the conserved quantity above is a different one, read off the equation of motion.

Equilibrium positions on the turning circumference Law: equilibrium: the coordinate stays constant, so its rate and acceleration vanish in the equation of motion; admissibility by the sign of the unilateral reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.6; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} \left(\frac{g}{L} - \omega^2\cos\theta_e\right)\sin\theta_e &= 0 \quad &\text{(constant $\theta$: $\dot\theta = \ddot\theta = 0$)} \\ \sin\theta_e = 0 &\Rightarrow \theta_e = 0 \ \text{or} \ \theta_e = \pi \quad &\text{(lowest and highest points)} \\ \cos\theta_e = \frac{g}{\omega^2 L} &\Rightarrow \theta_e = \pm\arccos\frac{g}{\omega^2 L}, \ \text{only if } \omega^2 \gt \frac{g}{L} \quad &\text{(a cosine cannot exceed 1; at equality the two roots merge with $\theta_e = 0$)} \\ N(0) &= mg \gt 0, \quad N(\pi) = -mg \lt 0 \quad &\text{(reaction at rest: $N = mL\omega^2\sin^2\theta_e + mg\cos\theta_e$)} \\ N(\theta_e) &= mL\omega^2\sin^2\theta_e + \frac{mg^2}{\omega^2 L} \gt 0, \quad N_b = 0 \quad &\text{(off-axis positions, $\dot\theta = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_e = 0 \ \text{always}; \quad \theta_e = \pm\arccos\frac{g}{\omega^2 L} \ \text{if } \omega^2 \gt \frac{g}{L}; \quad \theta_e = \pi \ \text{excluded (it needs a pull)}}$$

Units: $[g/(\omega^2 L)] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]/([\mathrm{T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}]) = [1]$; $[N(\theta_e)] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: above the critical rate $\sqrt{g/L}$ the particle can ride at rest relative to the circumference on a horizontal circle of radius $L\sin\theta_e$, where the inward reaction and the weight give the centripetal force; the top would be an equilibrium only for a bead threaded on a wire, not for the inside of the circumference.

Stability of the equilibria Law: linearisation of the equation of motion about an equilibrium Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.6; Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6

Write the equation as the second derivative of $\theta$ equal to $F(\theta)$, with $F(\theta) = -(g/L - \omega^2\cos\theta)\sin\theta$. A small deviation $\delta$ obeys the second derivative of $\delta$ equal to $F'(\theta_e)\,\delta$: stable when $F'(\theta_e)$ is negative.

$$\begin{aligned} F(\theta) &= -\frac{g}{L}\sin\theta + \frac{\omega^2}{2}\sin 2\theta \quad &\text{($\sin\theta\cos\theta = \tfrac12\sin 2\theta$)} \\ F'(\theta) &= -\frac{g}{L}\cos\theta + \omega^2\cos 2\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule on $\sin 2\theta$)} \\ F'(0) &= \omega^2 - \frac{g}{L} \quad &\text{(lowest point)} \\ F'(\theta_e) &= -\omega^2\cos^2\theta_e + \omega^2\left(2\cos^2\theta_e - 1\right) = -\omega^2\sin^2\theta_e \quad &\text{($g/L = \omega^2\cos\theta_e$; $\cos 2\theta = 2\cos^2\theta - 1$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_e = 0 \ \text{stable if } \omega^2 \lt \frac{g}{L},\ \text{unstable if } \omega^2 \gt \frac{g}{L}; \qquad \theta_e = \pm\arccos\frac{g}{\omega^2L} \ \text{stable}}$$

Units: $[F'] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: when the rate exceeds $\sqrt{g/L}$ the lowest point loses its stability and the particle settles on one of the two symmetric off-axis positions, which approach the horizontal diameter as the rate grows.

Answers and Checks

Part of the statementAnswer
Motion, fixed circumference$\ddot\theta + (g/L)\sin\theta = 0$; $v^2 = v_0^2 - 2gL(1 - \cos\theta)$; $t = \int_0^\theta L\,d\theta'/\sqrt{v_0^2 - 2gL(1 - \cos\theta')}$; reaction $N = mv_0^2/L - mg(2 - 3\cos\theta)$ towards $O$
Point where it leaves the circumference$\cos\theta_d = (2gL - v_0^2)/(3gL)$, angle from the lowest point
Is it reached?no if $v_0 \le \sqrt{2gL}$ (oscillation); yes if $\sqrt{2gL} \lt v_0 \lt \sqrt{5gL}$, with $\theta_d$ between $\pi/2$ and $\pi$ and speed $v^2 = (v_0^2 - 2gL)/3$ there; no if $v_0 \ge \sqrt{5gL}$ (complete loops)
Equations of motion, turning circumference$\ddot\theta + (g/L - \omega^2\cos\theta)\sin\theta = 0$; $N = mL(\dot\theta^2 + \omega^2\sin^2\theta) + mg\cos\theta$; $N_b = -2mL\omega\dot\theta\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf k_1$
Equilibrium positions$\theta_e = 0$ (stable if $\omega^2 \lt g/L$); $\theta_e = \pm\arccos(g/(\omega^2 L))$ if $\omega^2 \gt g/L$ (stable); $\theta_e = \pi$ only for a bilateral constraint, excluded here

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 73 lists item 23 with no answer, so there is no key to compare with. Independent checks: for zero $\omega$ the equations are those of Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7; and the separation angle lies between the boundaries computed in the limiting cases below.

Limiting cases of the separation point Law: the separation formula at the boundaries of its regime Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} v_0^2 \to 2gL &\Rightarrow \cos\theta_d \to 0,\ \theta_d \to \tfrac{\pi}{2},\ v^2(\theta_d) \to 0 \quad &\text{(arrives at the horizontal diameter at rest, with zero reaction)} \\ v_0^2 \to 5gL &\Rightarrow \cos\theta_d \to -1,\ \theta_d \to \pi,\ v^2(\theta_d) \to gL \quad &\text{(at the top the weight alone gives the centripetal acceleration $v^2/L = g$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_d \in \left(\tfrac{\pi}{2}, \pi\right) \ \text{moves continuously from the horizontal diameter to the top}}$$

Units: $[v^2] = [gL] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the three regimes join continuously, and at the top the critical speed is the classical $\sqrt{gL}$ of a loop.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 24

Particle on a Circumference with a Spring

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 12
Wire & Elastic PotentialOscillations - Lecture 6

A heavy particle $P$ is constrained to move along a circumference and it is attached to a spring as shown in the figure. The mass of the particle is $m$, and the radius of the circumference is $a$. The other end of the spring is attached to a point of the $y$ axis with coordinates $(0, \beta a)$ and the constant of the spring is equal to $k$.

  • (a)
    Compute the potential of the forces acting on the particle as a function of the angle $\theta$ (shown in the figure).
  • (b)
    Identify the equilibrium positions.

The particle departs from the point $(a, 0)$ of the circumference with an initial velocity $\mathbf{v}_0 = v_0\mathbf{j}$.

  • (c)
    Reduce the motion to integrals (i.e., quadratures).
  • (d)
    If $\beta = \frac{mg}{ka}$, obtain the position of the particle as a function of time.

Consider now the situation in which an additional force acts on the particle. The force is proportional to, and in the same direction as, the instantaneous velocity. The constant of proportionality is $2m\mu$. The initial conditions are the same as before.

  • (e)
    Obtain the position of the particle as a function of time.
  • (f)
    Obtain the reaction of the circumference.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ slides on a smooth circumference of radius $a$ and centre $O$ in a vertical plane, pulled by a linear spring of constant $k$ anchored at the point $A$ of the vertical axis at height $\beta a$ (Problems.pdf, p. 12). The statement asks for the potential, the equilibria, the quadratures, the motion in the special case of the spring constant matched to the weight, and then the motion and the reaction when an extra force along the velocity acts. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$, origin at the centre of the circumference (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$ of the figure: $\mathbf i$ horizontal to the right, $\mathbf j$ vertical upward, $\mathbf k$ the cross product of $\mathbf i$ and $\mathbf j$, out of the paper (⊙)
Polar basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$
Coordinate$\theta$, angle of $OP$ from the axis $Ox$, counterclockwise (figure)
Points$O$ centre; $A$ anchor at $(0, \beta a)$; $P$ particle
Constraintscircumference $x^2 + y^2 - a^2 = 0$ and plane $z = 0$: a curve given as the intersection of two surfaces (Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.8); holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral (the particle is threaded on the circumference)
Degrees of freedom3 minus 2: 1, the angle $\theta(t)$
Data$m$ [kg], $a$ [m], $k$ [N m$^{-1}$], $\beta$ [dimensionless], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$], $v_0$ [m s$^{-1}$], $\mu$ [s$^{-1}$]
Applied forcesweight $-mg\,\mathbf j$; spring $-k\,\overrightarrow{AP}$; parts (e) and (f) also $\mathbf F_\mu = 2m\mu\,\mathbf v_0^P$
Reactionnormal subspace of a curve, dimension 2 (Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.16): $\mathbf N = N_R\,\mathbf e_R + N_k\,\mathbf k$
Initial conditions$\theta(0) = 0$ (the point $(a, 0)$); $\mathbf v_0^P(0) = v_0\,\mathbf j$, hence $a\dot\theta(0) = v_0$ (see the kinematics)
Hypothesesthe spring has zero natural length (the statement gives only its constant; with this reading the force is $-k$ times the vector from $A$ to $P$ (it pulls $P$ towards $A$), the zero-length spring of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5); in parts (e) and (f) the spring and weight are those of part (d), $\beta = mg/(ka)$, since the statement continues from part (d), and only with this balance does part (e) have a closed-form position; the extra force is taken literally, along the velocity
Figure 1. Geometry of the spring. The triangle of $OP$ (legs $a\cos\theta$, $a\sin\theta$) and the legs of $\overrightarrow{AP}$ ($a\cos\theta$ and $a(\sin\theta - \beta)$, drawn downwards) give $|\overrightarrow{AP}|^2 = a^2(1 + \beta^2 - 2\beta\sin\theta)$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Polar basis: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24
