View of the figure, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper (⊙), so positive angles are counterclockwise. $\mathbf e_R$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by $\theta$: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf j$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along minus $\mathbf i$, since the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_R &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j, \quad \mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(the two right triangles)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= 1\cdot\left(\cos\theta\cos\theta - \left(-\sin\theta\right)\sin\theta\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1, \quad \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} = \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(rows times rows)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \mathbf i = \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \mathbf j = \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_R$ is normal to the circumference and $\mathbf e_\theta$ tangent to it; the inverse relations (rows of the matrix) project the weight and the spring force.

Position vector of P and the spring vector Law: position vectors; vector from the anchor to the particle; its modulus Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.5; Problems.pdf, p. 12

The right triangle of $OP$ has hypotenuse $a$, horizontal leg $a\cos\theta$ (adjacent to $\theta$) and vertical leg $a\sin\theta$ (opposite). The vector $\overrightarrow{AP}$ has the same horizontal leg and the vertical leg $a\sin\theta$ minus the height $\beta a$ of $A$.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= a\,\mathbf e_R = a\cos\theta\,\mathbf i + a\sin\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(constraints substituted: radius $a$, plane $z = 0$)} \\ \overrightarrow{AP} &= \mathbf r_0^P - \beta a\,\mathbf j = a\cos\theta\,\mathbf i + a\left(\sin\theta - \beta\right)\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\overrightarrow{OA} = \beta a\,\mathbf j$)} \\ |\overrightarrow{AP}|^2 &= a^2\cos^2\theta + a^2\left(\sin^2\theta - 2\beta\sin\theta + \beta^2\right) = a^2\left(1 + \beta^2 - 2\beta\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(sum of squares; Pythagorean identity)} \\ \overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\mathbf e_R &= a\cos^2\theta + a\left(\sin\theta - \beta\right)\sin\theta = a\left(1 - \beta\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(components of $\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\mathbf e_\theta &= -a\cos\theta\sin\theta + a\left(\sin\theta - \beta\right)\cos\theta = -a\beta\cos\theta \quad &\text{(components of $\mathbf e_\theta$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = a\,\mathbf e_R, \qquad \overrightarrow{AP} = a\left(1 - \beta\sin\theta\right)\mathbf e_R - a\beta\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad |\overrightarrow{AP}|^2 = a^2\left(1 + \beta^2 - 2\beta\sin\theta\right)}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$ and $[\mathrm{L^2}]$; $\beta$ dimensionless

Meaning: the spring is longest at the bottom and shortest at the top of the circumference.

Kinematics

Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: derivatives of the polar unit vectors; time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.34; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.35
$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathbf e}_R &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{($\boldsymbol\omega_{10} = \dot\theta\,\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \frac{d}{dt}\left(a\,\mathbf e_R\right) = a\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($a$ constant)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + a\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = -a\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R + a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(product rule)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P(0) &= a\dot\theta(0)\,\mathbf e_\theta(0) = a\dot\theta(0)\,\mathbf j = v_0\,\mathbf j \Rightarrow \dot\theta(0) = \frac{v_0}{a} \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_\theta(0) = \mathbf j$; data)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf v_0^P = a\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \mathbf a_0^P = -a\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R + a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \dot\theta(0) = \frac{v_0}{a}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[v_0/a] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the velocity is tangent to the circumference; the acceleration has the centripetal part along minus $\mathbf e_R$ and the tangential part along $\mathbf e_\theta$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram shows the weight, the spring force towards $A$, the reaction (in the plane along $\mathbf e_R$; out of the plane along $\mathbf k$) and, in parts (e) and (f), the force along the velocity. The frame $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 2. Forces on $P$: weight, spring $-k\,\overrightarrow{AP}$, the reaction $N_R\mathbf e_R + N_k\mathbf k$ of the circumference and, in parts (e) and (f), the force $2m\mu\,\mathbf v_0^P$ along the velocity..
Newton's second law with all the forces, projected on the polar basis Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with the constraint force of a curve; tangent projection free of the reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.16; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

The switch $\epsilon$ is 0 for parts (a) to (d) and 1 for parts (e) and (f).

$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf j - k\,\overrightarrow{AP} + \epsilon\,2m\mu\,\mathbf v_0^P + N_R\,\mathbf e_R + N_k\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(all forces)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j &= -mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_R - mg\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf j$ in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ -k\,\overrightarrow{AP} &= -ka\left(1 - \beta\sin\theta\right)\mathbf e_R + ka\beta\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(spring vector in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ ma\ddot\theta &= \left(ka\beta - mg\right)\cos\theta + \epsilon\,2m\mu a\dot\theta \quad &\text{(tangent: no reaction)} \\ \mathbf e_R:\ -ma\dot\theta^2 &= -mg\sin\theta - ka\left(1 - \beta\sin\theta\right) + N_R \quad &\text{(normal in the plane)} \\ \mathbf k:\ 0 &= N_k \quad &\text{(no motion and no applied force out of the plane)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{ma\ddot\theta = \left(ka\beta - mg\right)\cos\theta + \epsilon\,2m\mu a\dot\theta, \qquad N_R = ka + \left(mg - ka\beta\right)\sin\theta - ma\dot\theta^2, \qquad N_k = 0}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$: $[ka] = [\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}]$, $[m\mu a\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{T^{-1}}][\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the tangent equation is the equation of motion of the single degree of freedom; the radial one gives the reaction afterwards. Weight and spring enter the tangent equation only through the combination $ka\beta - mg$.

Answers and Checks

(a) Potential of the forces

Potential of the weight and of the spring Law: a force is conservative when it is minus the gradient of a potential; potential as minus the work along the constraint, with Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.12
$$\begin{aligned} -\nabla\left(mgy\right) &= -mg\,\mathbf j, \quad -\nabla\left(\tfrac12 k\,|\mathbf r - \overrightarrow{OA}|^2\right) = -k\left(\mathbf r - \overrightarrow{OA}\right) = -k\,\overrightarrow{AP} \quad &\text{(gradients: both forces derive from a potential)} \\ V &= mgy + \tfrac12 k\,|\overrightarrow{AP}|^2 = mga\sin\theta + \tfrac12 ka^2\left(1 + \beta^2 - 2\beta\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(on the circumference)} \\ &= \tfrac12 ka^2\left(1 + \beta^2\right) + \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\sin\theta \quad &\text{(terms in $\sin\theta$ collected)} \\ V(\theta) - V(0) &= -\int_0^{\theta}\left(\mathbf F_g + \mathbf F_s\right)\cdot\mathbf e_\theta\,a\,d\theta' = -\int_0^{\theta}\left(ka\beta - mg\right)a\cos\theta'\,d\theta' \quad &\text{(work along $d\mathbf r = a\,\mathbf e_\theta\,d\theta'$)} \\ &= -\left(ka\beta - mg\right)a\left[\sin\theta'\right]_0^{\theta} = \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\sin\theta \quad &\text{(antiderivative $\sin\theta'$; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{V(\theta) = \tfrac12 ka^2\left(1 + \beta^2\right) + \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\sin\theta}$$

Units: $[mga] = [ka^2] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$, an energy

Meaning: the direct sum and the work along the circumference agree; the reaction does no work, so it has no potential and does not enter.

(b) Equilibrium positions

Equilibria and their stability Law: equilibria are the critical points of the potential; stable at a minimum Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.6
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dV}{d\theta} &= \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(also the tangent equation at rest)} \\ mg \ne ka\beta &\Rightarrow \cos\theta_e = 0 \Rightarrow \theta_e = \tfrac{\pi}{2} \ \text{or} \ \theta_e = -\tfrac{\pi}{2} \quad &\text{(top and bottom)} \\ \frac{d^2V}{d\theta^2} &= -\left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\sin\theta \quad &\text{(second derivative)} \\ \frac{d^2V}{d\theta^2}\Big|_{\pi/2} &= \left(ka\beta - mg\right)a, \quad \frac{d^2V}{d\theta^2}\Big|_{-\pi/2} = \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a \quad &\text{(the sign decides the stability)} \\ mg = ka\beta &\Rightarrow \frac{dV}{d\theta} \equiv 0 \quad &\text{(constant potential)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{ll} ka\beta \gt mg: & \text{top } \theta_e = \tfrac{\pi}{2} \text{ stable, bottom unstable} \cr ka\beta \lt mg: & \text{bottom } \theta_e = -\tfrac{\pi}{2} \text{ stable, top unstable} \cr ka\beta = mg: & \text{every position is an equilibrium (neutral)} \end{array}}$$

Units: $[ka\beta] = [mg] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the spring pulls the particle up with an effective vertical force $ka\beta$ that competes with the weight; when the two balance, nothing drives the particle along the circumference.

(c) Reduction to quadratures

Energy integral and quadrature Law: the tangent equation multiplied by the angular rate is an exact derivative (chain rule); separation of variables; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.44
$$\begin{aligned} ma^2\dot\theta\ddot\theta - \left(ka\beta - mg\right)a\cos\theta\,\dot\theta &= 0 \quad &\text{(zero $\epsilon$; tangent equation times $a\dot\theta$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 ma^2\dot\theta^2\right) &= ma^2\dot\theta\ddot\theta, \quad \frac{d}{dt}V(\theta) = \frac{dV}{d\theta}\,\dot\theta = \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\cos\theta\,\dot\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\tfrac12 ma^2\dot\theta^2 + V(\theta)\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule)} \\ \tfrac12 ma^2\dot\theta^2 + \left(mg - ka\beta\right)a\sin\theta &= \tfrac12 m v_0^2 \quad &\text{($\theta(0) = 0$, $a\dot\theta(0) = v_0$; constant part of $V$ cancels)} \\ \dot\theta^2 &= \frac{v_0^2}{a^2} - \frac{2\left(mg - ka\beta\right)}{ma}\sin\theta \quad &\text{(solved for the squared rate)} \\ \int_0^{\theta}\frac{d\theta'}{\sqrt{\dfrac{v_0^2}{a^2} - \dfrac{2\left(mg - ka\beta\right)}{ma}\sin\theta'}} &= \int_0^t dt' = t \quad &\text{(separation, $\dot\theta \gt 0$ at the start; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{t = \int_0^{\theta}\frac{d\theta'}{\sqrt{v_0^2/a^2 - 2\left(mg - ka\beta\right)\sin\theta'/(ma)}} \quad \text{(while $\dot\theta \gt 0$; the sign of the root changes at a turning point)}}$$

Units: $[v_0^2/a^2] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[(mg - ka\beta)/(ma)] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{M\,L}] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; the integral is $[\mathrm{T}]$

Meaning: the mechanical energy is conserved (smooth fixed constraint, conservative forces); the radicand decides whether the particle turns back or goes round the circumference.

(d) Motion when the spring balances the weight

Uniform circular motion Law: the tangent equation with the balanced coefficient; two integrations with Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.5
$$\begin{aligned} ka\beta - mg &= ka\,\frac{mg}{ka} - mg = 0 \quad &\text{($\beta = mg/(ka)$)} \\ ma\ddot\theta &= 0 \Rightarrow \dot\theta(t) - \dot\theta(0) = \int_0^t 0\,dt' = 0 \Rightarrow \dot\theta = \frac{v_0}{a} \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule)} \\ \theta(t) - \theta(0) &= \int_0^t \frac{v_0}{a}\,dt' = \left[\frac{v_0}{a}\,t'\right]_0^t = \frac{v_0}{a}\,t \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule, $\theta(0) = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P(t) = a\cos\frac{v_0 t}{a}\,\mathbf i + a\sin\frac{v_0 t}{a}\,\mathbf j}$$

Units: $[v_0 t/a] = [1]$; position $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the spring cancels the tangential part of the weight everywhere, so the particle runs round the circumference with the constant speed $v_0$.

(e) Motion with the force along the velocity

Exponentially growing angular rate Law: the tangent equation with the extra force; separation of variables; substitution and Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Problems.pdf, p. 12

Call $\Omega$ the angular rate, the time derivative of $\theta$.

$$\begin{aligned} ma\dot\Omega &= 0 + 2m\mu a\,\Omega \Rightarrow \frac{d\Omega}{\Omega} = 2\mu\,dt \quad &\text{(unit $\epsilon$, balanced coefficient; separation)} \\ \int_{v_0/a}^{\Omega}\frac{d\Omega'}{\Omega'} &= \left[\ln\Omega'\right]_{v_0/a}^{\Omega} = \ln\frac{a\Omega}{v_0} = \int_0^t 2\mu\,dt' = 2\mu t \quad &\text{(antiderivative $\ln$; Barrow's rule on both sides)} \\ \Omega(t) &= \frac{v_0}{a}\,e^{2\mu t} \quad &\text{(exponential of both sides)} \\ w &= 2\mu t', \quad dw = 2\mu\,dt', \quad t' = 0 \to w = 0, \quad t' = t \to w = 2\mu t \quad &\text{(substitution and its limits)} \\ \theta(t) &= \int_0^t \frac{v_0}{a}\,e^{2\mu t'}\,dt' = \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}\int_0^{2\mu t} e^{w}\,dw = \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}\left[e^{w}\right]_0^{2\mu t} \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule, $\theta(0) = 0$)} \\ &= \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}\left(e^{2\mu t} - 1\right) \quad &\text{(limits substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta(t) = \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}\left(e^{2\mu t} - 1\right), \qquad \mathbf r_0^P(t) = a\cos\theta(t)\,\mathbf i + a\sin\theta(t)\,\mathbf j}$$

Units: $[\mu t] = [1]$; $[v_0/(\mu a)] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]/([\mathrm{T^{-1}}][\mathrm{L}]) = [1]$, radians

Meaning: a force along the velocity feeds energy into the particle at the rate $2m\mu v^2$, so the angular rate grows exponentially.

(f) Reaction of the circumference

Reaction with the force along the velocity Law: the normal projections of Newton's law with the motion of part (e) Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.16; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.5
$$\begin{aligned} N_R &= ka + \left(mg - ka\beta\right)\sin\theta - ma\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(radial equation; the extra force is tangent)} \\ &= ka - ma\,\frac{v_0^2}{a^2}\,e^{4\mu t} \quad &\text{(balanced coefficient; $\dot\theta = (v_0/a)\,e^{2\mu t}$)} \\ N_k &= 0 \quad &\text{(out-of-plane equation)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf N(t) = \left(ka - \frac{m v_0^2}{a}\,e^{4\mu t}\right)\mathbf e_R\big(\theta(t)\big)}$$

Units: $[ka] = [m v_0^2/a] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the outward component starts at $ka - m v_0^2/a$ (the radial pull of the spring and weight against the centripetal need) and decreases without bound: the circumference must push the particle inwards harder and harder as it speeds up.

Reading of the extra force

The statement says that the extra force is proportional to the velocity and in the same direction, with constant $2m\mu$, and the solution follows it literally. If a viscous damping force opposite to the velocity was meant (the usual model of Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.19), the same steps with the sign of $\mu$ reversed give an angle that grows as $1 - e^{-2\mu t}$ towards the finite limit $v_0/(2\mu a)$, and a radial reaction that tends to $ka$ as the particle comes to rest.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Potential$V(\theta) = \frac12 ka^2(1 + \beta^2) + (mg - ka\beta)\,a\sin\theta$
(b) Equilibria$\theta_e = \pm\pi/2$ if $ka\beta \ne mg$ (top stable if $ka\beta \gt mg$, bottom stable if $ka\beta \lt mg$); every $\theta$ if $ka\beta = mg$
(c) Quadrature$\dot\theta^2 = v_0^2/a^2 - 2(mg - ka\beta)\sin\theta/(ma)$; $t = \int_0^\theta d\theta'/\sqrt{v_0^2/a^2 - 2(mg - ka\beta)\sin\theta'/(ma)}$
(d) Position for $\beta = mg/(ka)$$\theta = v_0 t/a$; $\mathbf r_0^P = a\cos(v_0t/a)\,\mathbf i + a\sin(v_0t/a)\,\mathbf j$
(e) Position with the force $2m\mu\,\mathbf v$$\theta = \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}(e^{2\mu t} - 1)$; $\mathbf r_0^P = a\cos\theta\,\mathbf i + a\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$
(f) Reaction$\mathbf N = (ka - \frac{m v_0^2}{a}e^{4\mu t})\,\mathbf e_R$, no component out of the plane

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 73 lists item 24 with no answer, so there is no key to compare with. Independent checks: the potential obtained as a direct sum and as minus the work along the circumference agree (box of part (a)); and the limiting cases below recover part (d).

Limiting case: vanishing extra force Law: first-order expansion of the exponential Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4
$$\begin{aligned} e^{2\mu t} - 1 &= 2\mu t + \tfrac12\left(2\mu t\right)^2 + \dots \quad &\text{(Taylor series of the exponential)} \\ \theta(t) &= \frac{v_0}{2\mu a}\left(2\mu t + 2\mu^2 t^2 + \dots\right) \to \frac{v_0 t}{a} \quad &\text{($\mu \to 0$)} \\ N_R &\to ka - \frac{m v_0^2}{a} \quad &\text{($e^{4\mu t} \to 1$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mu \to 0:\ \theta \to \frac{v_0 t}{a}, \quad N_R \to ka - \frac{m v_0^2}{a}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{rad}]$ and $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: without the extra force the uniform motion of part (d) is recovered, with a constant reaction.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 29

Heavy Particle on a Tilted Cylinder

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 17
Unilateral LiftoffIn-Class Session 6

A heavy particle of mass $m$ is constrained to move without friction along the surface of an infinitely-long, circular cylinder of radius $R$. The cylinder axis ($Oz$) forms an angle $\alpha$ with the vertical direction ($Oz_1$). A sketch is shown on the left panel of the figure below. The position of the particle is completely specified by providing the coordinate $z$ along the axis $Oz$ and the angle $\theta$ as shown on the right panel. Initially, the particle is located at $z = 0, \theta = \pi/2$ and the time derivatives are $\dot{z} = v_0$ and $\dot{\theta} = \omega_0$.

  • (a)
    Determine the value of $z$ as a function of time, $z(t)$.
  • (b)
    Reduce the determination of the angle $\theta(t)$ to integrals of the form $\int [...] d\theta = \int [...] dt$.
  • (c)
    Determine the reaction from the cylinder as a function of $\theta$.
  • (d)
    For the particular case $\omega_0^2 = \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}$, describe qualitatively the motion of the particle.
  • (e)
    For the particular case $\omega_0^2 = \frac{4g\sin\alpha}{R}$, describe qualitatively the motion of the particle.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle of mass $m$ slides without friction on an infinitely long circular cylinder of radius $R$ whose axis $Oz$ is tilted by the angle $\alpha$ from the vertical $Oz_1$. Its position is given by the axial coordinate $z$ and the angle $\theta$ of the cross-section (Problems.pdf, p. 17). The statement asks for $z(t)$, the quadrature of $\theta(t)$, the reaction as a function of $\theta$, and a qualitative description for two initial angular rates. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_1\}$ with $\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j_1, \mathbf k_1\}$ of the left panel: $\mathbf k_1$ vertical upward, $\mathbf i_1$ horizontal to the right, $\mathbf j_1$ horizontal, right-handed
Basis of the cylinder$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$ of the axes $x, y, z$ of the figure: $\mathbf k$ along the axis of the cylinder, $\mathbf j$ coincident with $\mathbf j_1$ ($y_1 \equiv y$), $\mathbf i$ completing the right-handed triad; fixed in $S_0$
Cylindrical basis$\mathcal B_3 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_2$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$ (right panel)
Coordinates$z$ along the axis and $\theta$ from the axis $Ox$ in the cross-section (statement)
Constraint$f = x^2 + y^2 - R^2 = 0$ in the axes of the cylinder; holonomic, stationary, smooth; the particle is "constrained to move along the surface", read as bilateral (the sign of the reaction is discussed in part (c))
Degrees of freedom3 minus 1: 2, namely $z(t)$ and $\theta(t)$
Data$m$ [kg], $R$ [m], $\alpha$ [rad], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$], $v_0$ [m s$^{-1}$], $\omega_0$ [rad s$^{-1}$]
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf k_1$
Reaction$\mathbf N = N\,\mathbf e_R$ (normal subspace of a surface, dimension 1; $N$ positive outwards)
Initial conditions$z(0) = 0$, $\theta(0) = \pi/2$, $\dot z(0) = v_0$, $\dot\theta(0) = \omega_0$ (statement)
Hypotheses$0 \lt \alpha \lt \pi/2$ as in the figure; $\omega_0 \gt 0$ in parts (d) and (e)
Figure 1. Rotation about $\mathbf j_1$ (into the paper): a positive angle is clockwise in this view. The axis $\mathbf k$ leans towards $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf i$ dips below the horizontal; the leg of $\mathbf i$ along $-\mathbf k_1$ is the minus sign of $\mathbf i = \cos\alpha\,\mathbf i_1 - \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k_1$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Basis of the cylinder: rotation about j1 Law: elementary rotation about the second axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19; Problems.pdf, p. 17

View of the left panel, $\mathbf i_1$ to the right and $\mathbf k_1$ up. Since $\mathbf j_1$ is the cross product of $\mathbf k_1$ and $\mathbf i_1$, it points into the paper (⊗), and a positive rotation about $\mathbf j_1$ (thumb into the paper) is clockwise as seen. The axis $\mathbf k$ is $\mathbf k_1$ leaned clockwise towards $\mathbf i_1$ by $\alpha$: a positive rotation $\alpha$, Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19 with the angle $\alpha$. The right triangle of $\mathbf k$ has hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\alpha$ along $\mathbf k_1$ and opposite leg $\sin\alpha$ along $\mathbf i_1$. The axis $\mathbf i$ is $\mathbf i_1$ turned clockwise by the same angle: adjacent leg $\cos\alpha$ along $\mathbf i_1$, opposite leg $\sin\alpha$ along minus $\mathbf k_1$, because turning the right-pointing vector clockwise dips it below the horizontal (the minus sign; the figure draws $x$ pointing down to the right).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i &= \cos\alpha\,\mathbf i_1 - \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \mathbf j = \mathbf j_1, \quad \mathbf k = \sin\alpha\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(the two right triangles)} \\ \mathbf i\times\mathbf j &= \cos\alpha\,\mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_1 - \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k_1\times\mathbf j_1 = \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\alpha\,\mathbf i_1 = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed: $\mathbf k_1\times\mathbf j_1 = -\mathbf i_1$)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= \cos\alpha\left(\cos\alpha - 0\right) - 0 + \sin\alpha\left(0 + \sin\alpha\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\alpha + \sin^2\alpha = 1, \quad \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{13} = -\cos\alpha\sin\alpha + \sin\alpha\cos\alpha = 0 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf j$)} \\ \mathbf k_1 &= -\sin\alpha\,\mathbf i + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(third row of the matrix)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\alpha & 0 & \sin\alpha \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr -\sin\alpha & 0 & \cos\alpha \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \mathbf k_1 = -\sin\alpha\,\mathbf i + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: the axis $Ox$ of the cross-section points partly downwards, so gravity has a component along $\mathbf i$: the generator at $\theta$ equal to 0 is the lowest one.

Figure 2. Cross-section, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper. Gravity has the component $g\sin\alpha$ along $\mathbf i$ in this plane, so $\theta = 0$ is the lowest generator; the triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$ (opposite leg along $-\mathbf i$) gives its minus sign..
Cylindrical basis of the cross-section: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View of the right panel, along the axis: $\mathbf i$ to the right, $\mathbf j$ up, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper (⊙), as the figure marks; positive angles are counterclockwise. $\mathbf e_R$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\theta$ (adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on $\mathbf j$); $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by $\theta$ (adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf j$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on minus $\mathbf i$: the upward vector leans to the left, the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_R &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j, \quad \mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(the two right triangles)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k, \quad \det[{}_2R_3] = \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(right-handed; expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_2R_3][{}_2R_3]^T\right)_{12} &= \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 2; diagonal entries equal 1)} \\ \mathbf i &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(first row of the matrix)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_2R_3] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \mathbf i = \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta}$$

Units: dimensionless: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_R$ is the outward normal of the cylinder at the particle, $\mathbf e_\theta$ and $\mathbf k$ span its tangent plane.

Position vector and the weight in the cylindrical basis Law: position vector in cylindrical coordinates; change of basis of the weight Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.28; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.16
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= R\,\mathbf e_R + z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(constraint substituted: radius $R$)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf k_1 &= mg\sin\alpha\,\mathbf i - mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_1$ in $\mathcal B_2$)} \\ &= mg\sin\alpha\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - mg\sin\alpha\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\mathbf i$ in $\mathcal B_3$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_R + z\,\mathbf k, \qquad -mg\,\mathbf k_1 = mg\sin\alpha\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_R - mg\sin\alpha\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$; $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the weight splits into a part along the axis, $mg\cos\alpha$, and a part in the cross-section, $mg\sin\alpha$, that points along $\mathbf i$, towards the lowest generator.

Kinematics

Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: derivatives of the cylindrical unit vectors; time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.30
$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathbf e}_R &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R, \quad \dot{\mathbf k} = \mathbf 0 \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_3$ turns about the fixed axis $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \dot z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule; $R$ constant)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= R\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + R\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta + \ddot z\,\mathbf k = -R\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R + R\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \ddot z\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(product rule again)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf v_0^P = R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \dot z\,\mathbf k, \qquad \mathbf a_0^P = -R\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R + R\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \ddot z\,\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the velocity lies in the tangent plane of the cylinder; the centripetal part of the acceleration points to the axis.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram, drawn in the cross-section, shows the in-plane part of the weight, the reaction along $\mathbf e_R$ and, as a ⊗ symbol, the axial part of the weight. The frame $S_0$ is inertial.

Figure 3. Forces on the particle: the weight, split into $mg\sin\alpha$ along $\mathbf i$ in the section and $-mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf k$ along the axis, and the reaction $N\mathbf e_R$ of the smooth cylinder..
Newton's second law with the reaction, projected on the cylindrical basis Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with the constraint force; tangent projections free of the reaction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.11; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf k_1 + N\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{(weight and reaction; unknowns $z$, $\theta$, $N$)} \\ \mathbf k:\ m\ddot z &= -mg\cos\alpha \quad &\text{(tangent along the axis)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ mR\ddot\theta &= -mg\sin\alpha\sin\theta \quad &\text{(tangent along the cross-section)} \\ \mathbf e_R:\ -mR\dot\theta^2 &= mg\sin\alpha\cos\theta + N \quad &\text{(normal)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\ddot z = -g\cos\alpha, \qquad \ddot\theta + \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\sin\theta = 0, \qquad N = -mR\dot\theta^2 - mg\sin\alpha\cos\theta}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[g\sin\alpha/R] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[N] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the two degrees of freedom decouple: a uniformly decelerated motion along the axis and a pendulum of length $R$ in the reduced gravity $g\sin\alpha$ in the cross-section; the reaction follows afterwards.

Answers and Checks

(a) Axial coordinate

z as a function of time Law: two integrations of a constant acceleration with Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.5; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.6
$$\begin{aligned} \dot z(t) - \dot z(0) &= \int_0^t \left(-g\cos\alpha\right)dt' = \left[-g\cos\alpha\,t'\right]_0^t = -g\cos\alpha\,t \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule)} \\ \dot z(t) &= v_0 - g\cos\alpha\,t \quad &\text{($\dot z(0) = v_0$)} \\ z(t) - z(0) &= \int_0^t\left(v_0 - g\cos\alpha\,t'\right)dt' = \left[v_0 t' - \tfrac12 g\cos\alpha\,t'^2\right]_0^t \quad &\text{(antiderivative; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{z(t) = v_0 t - \tfrac12 g\cos\alpha\,t^2}$$

Units: $[v_0 t] = [g t^2] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the particle climbs along the axis until the time $v_0/(g\cos\alpha)$ and then slides back down, whatever happens in the cross-section.

(b) Quadrature of the angle

First integral of the angular equation and its quadrature Law: the angular equation multiplied by the angular rate is an exact derivative (chain rule); separation of variables; Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.39; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.44
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\theta\ddot\theta + \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\sin\theta\,\dot\theta &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation times $\dot\theta$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12\dot\theta^2\right) &= \dot\theta\ddot\theta, \quad \frac{d}{dt}\left(-\frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta\right) = \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\sin\theta\,\dot\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\tfrac12\dot\theta^2 - \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule)} \\ \dot\theta^2 &= \omega_0^2 + \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta \quad &\text{($\theta(0) = \pi/2$, $\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2} = 0$, $\dot\theta(0) = \omega_0$)} \\ \int_{\pi/2}^{\theta}\frac{d\theta'}{\sqrt{\omega_0^2 + \dfrac{2g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta'}} &= \int_0^t dt' = t \quad &\text{(separation, $\dot\theta \gt 0$; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\int_{\pi/2}^{\theta}\frac{d\theta'}{\sqrt{\omega_0^2 + 2g\sin\alpha\cos\theta'/R}} = t \quad \text{(while } \dot\theta \gt 0\text{; the sign changes at a turning point)}}$$

Units: $[\omega_0^2] = [g/R] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; the integral is $[\mathrm{T}]$

Meaning: the energy of the motion in the cross-section is conserved separately from the axial one, because the two equations are decoupled.

(c) Reaction of the cylinder

Reaction as a function of the angle Law: normal projection with the first integral Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.11; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} N &= -mR\left(\omega_0^2 + \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta\right) - mg\sin\alpha\cos\theta \quad &\text{(first integral substituted)} \\ &= -m\left(R\omega_0^2 + 3g\sin\alpha\cos\theta\right) \quad &\text{(terms collected)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf N(\theta) = -m\left(R\omega_0^2 + 3g\sin\alpha\cos\theta\right)\mathbf e_R}$$

Units: $[R\omega_0^2] = [g] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[N] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: where the bracket is positive the cylinder pushes the particle towards the axis (as for a particle sliding on the inside); where it is negative it pulls it outwards, which a bilateral constraint can do and a one-sided contact cannot.

(d) Case of the smaller angular rate

Oscillation about the lowest generator Law: turning points where the squared angular rate vanishes; reaction at those points Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.43; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\theta^2 &= \frac{g\sin\alpha}{R}\left(1 + 2\cos\theta\right) \quad &\text{($\omega_0^2 = g\sin\alpha/R$)} \\ \dot\theta^2 &= 0 \iff \cos\theta = -\tfrac12 \iff \theta = \pm\tfrac{2\pi}{3} \quad &\text{(turning points)} \\ N &= -mg\sin\alpha\left(1 + 3\cos\theta\right) \quad &\text{(reaction of part (c))} \\ N\left(\pm\tfrac{2\pi}{3}\right) &= -mg\sin\alpha\left(1 - \tfrac32\right) = \tfrac12\,mg\sin\alpha \gt 0 \quad &\text{(outward, opposite to its sign at the lowest generator)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{-\tfrac{2\pi}{3} \le \theta \le \tfrac{2\pi}{3}: \ \text{periodic oscillation about } \theta = 0, \quad z(t) = v_0 t - \tfrac12 g\cos\alpha\,t^2}$$

Units: $[g\sin\alpha/R] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[N] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the particle starts on the side of the cylinder moving up, rises to the angle $2\pi/3$, swings back through the lowest generator to minus $2\pi/3$ and repeats, while it climbs and then slides back along the axis: on the unrolled cylinder the path is a wave drawn along a parabola. Beyond the angle where the cosine is minus one third the reaction changes sign, so this motion needs the bilateral constraint.

(e) Case of the larger angular rate

Complete turns round the axis Law: the squared angular rate never vanishes; reaction sign Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.43; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\theta^2 &= \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R}\left(2 + \cos\theta\right) \ge \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R} \gt 0 \quad &\text{($\omega_0^2 = 4g\sin\alpha/R$; $\cos\theta \ge -1$)} \\ \dot\theta_{\min}^2 &= \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R} \ (\theta = \pi), \quad \dot\theta_{\max}^2 = \frac{6g\sin\alpha}{R} \ (\theta = 0) \quad &\text{(top and bottom generators)} \\ N &= -mg\sin\alpha\left(4 + 3\cos\theta\right) \le -mg\sin\alpha \lt 0 \quad &\text{(always towards the axis)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot\theta \ \text{never vanishes: complete, non-uniform turns, fastest at the lowest generator}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the particle winds round the cylinder on a helix-like path whose pitch follows the axial velocity, which falls to zero at the time $v_0/(g\cos\alpha)$ and then reverses; the cylinder always pushes it towards the axis, so a one-sided contact, with the particle on the inner face of the cylinder, would also hold.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) $z(t)$$z = v_0 t - \frac12 g\cos\alpha\,t^2$
(b) Quadrature of $\theta$$\dot\theta^2 = \omega_0^2 + \frac{2g\sin\alpha}{R}\cos\theta$; $\int_{\pi/2}^{\theta} d\theta'/\sqrt{\omega_0^2 + 2g\sin\alpha\cos\theta'/R} = t$
(c) Reaction$\mathbf N = -m(R\omega_0^2 + 3g\sin\alpha\cos\theta)\,\mathbf e_R$
(d) $\omega_0^2 = g\sin\alpha/R$oscillation between $\theta = \pm 2\pi/3$ about the lowest generator; the reaction changes sign (bilateral constraint needed)
(e) $\omega_0^2 = 4g\sin\alpha/R$complete non-uniform turns, $\dot\theta^2$ between $2g\sin\alpha/R$ and $6g\sin\alpha/R$; reaction always towards the axis

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 74 lists item 29 with no answer, so there is no key to compare with. Independent checks: the limiting cases below.

Limiting cases: vertical and horizontal axis Law: behaviour of the equations at the extreme tilts Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.38
$$\begin{aligned} \alpha \to 0:\ \ddot\theta &\to 0,\ \ddot z \to -g,\ N \to -mR\omega_0^2 \quad &\text{(vertical axis)} \\ \alpha \to \tfrac{\pi}{2}:\ \ddot z &\to 0,\ \ddot\theta + \frac{g}{R}\sin\theta \to 0 \quad &\text{(horizontal axis)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\alpha \to 0:\ \text{uniform rotation and free fall along the axis}; \quad \alpha \to \tfrac{\pi}{2}:\ \text{simple pendulum of length } R \text{ and uniform axial motion}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$, $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: with a vertical axis gravity has no part in the cross-section and the reaction is just the centripetal force; with a horizontal axis the cross-section is vertical and the problem is the simple pendulum of Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 31

Two Particles on Cone with String

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 19
Multi-Particle Cable Constraint

Two heavy point particles $P_1$ and $P_2$ of mass $m$ each are connected by an inextensible, massless string of total length $\ell$. Particle $P_1$ is constrained to move without friction over the surface of a cone of semiangle $\alpha$ (the particle cannot leave the surface of the cone). The string passes through an aperture at the cone vertex $O$, and it can slide through it without friction. Particle $P_2$ hangs along the cone axis, and is constrained to move along it. We define an inertial reference frame $S_0$ as shown in the figure. We call $\xi$ the length of the fraction of string between $O$ and $P_1$, and $\theta$ the angle formed between the $Ox$ axis and the projection onto the $Oxy$ plane of the $\mathbf{OP}_1$ vector. In the following, assume that the string is always tense and that the particles do not reach point $O$ during motion.

  • (a)
    Write down the general expression of the position, velocity and acceleration vectors of $P_1$ and $P_2$ as a function of $\theta, \xi$ and the parameters of the problem.
  • (b)
    Write down the expression of all forces that act on $P_1$ and $P_2$.
  • (c)
    Write down the differential equations that, if solved, would yield the time evolution of $\theta, \xi$, the tension on the string, and any other reaction force in the problem.
  • (d)
    What is the mathematical condition that the function $\xi(t)$ must satisfy for the string to remain tense all the time?
  • (e)
    How fast should $P_1$ rotate in the $\theta$ direction to have a circular trajectory over a cone parallel? What is the value of the string tension and any other reaction force in the problem in that case? Give your answer as a function of $\xi$ and the parameters of the problem.
  • (f)
    Taking these equations as the starting point (or based on the physical considerations of the problem) write down two first-integrals of the problem that do not depend on the string tension or any other reaction force in the problem.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

Two heavy particles of mass $m$ are joined by an inextensible massless string of length $\ell$ that slides without friction through an aperture at the vertex $O$ of a cone of semi-angle $\alpha$. $P_1$ moves without friction on the cone and cannot leave it; $P_2$ hangs inside the cone along its axis and is guided along it (Problems.pdf, p. 19). The coordinates are the length $\xi$ of string between $O$ and $P_1$ and the azimuth $\theta$ of $P_1$. The statement asks for the kinematics, the forces, the equations, the condition for a tense string, the circular motion on a parallel and two first integrals. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure, origin at the vertex (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathbf k$ vertical upward (axis $OZ$); the cone opens downwards from $O$
Cylindrical basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$; $\mathbf e_R$ horizontal, towards the meridian of $P_1$
Cone basis$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf e_\xi, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf n\}$: $\mathbf e_\xi$ along the generator from $O$ to $P_1$, $\mathbf n$ the outward normal of the cone; $\mathcal B_1$ turned about $\mathbf e_\theta$
Coordinates$\xi$ (string length $OP_1$) and $\theta$ (azimuth of the projection of $\overrightarrow{OP_1}$ from $OX$) (statement)
Constraints$P_1$ on the cone (1 equation, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral: it cannot leave the surface); $P_2$ on the axis, $x_2 = y_2 = 0$ (2 equations, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral); string, $\xi + |\overrightarrow{OP_2}| = \ell$ (1 equation joining the two particles, holonomic, stationary, unilateral: a string can only pull) ([Lecture 3, Slide 17]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom6 coordinates of two particles minus 4 constraint equations: 2, namely $\xi(t)$ and $\theta(t)$
Data$m$ [kg] each, $\ell$ [m], $\alpha$ [rad] with $0 \lt \alpha \lt \pi/2$, $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]
Applied forcesweights $-mg\,\mathbf k$ on each particle
Constraint forceson $P_1$: tension $-T\,\mathbf e_\xi$ (towards $O$) and normal reaction $N\,\mathbf n$; on $P_2$: tension $T\,\mathbf k$ and the guide reaction $\mathbf R_2$, normal to the axis (two unknown horizontal components); the same $T$ on both ends (massless string, smooth aperture)
Initial conditionsnot given: generic $\xi(0)$, $\dot\xi(0)$, $\theta(0)$, $\dot\theta(0)$
Hypothesesstring always tense and particles never at $O$ (statement); $0 \lt \xi \lt \ell$
Figure 1. Meridian plane of $P_1$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper. The triangle (hypotenuse $\xi$, legs $\xi\cos\alpha$ along the axis and $\xi\sin\alpha$ horizontal) gives $\mathbf e_\xi = \sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_R - \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k$; the cone basis is $\mathbf e_R$, $\mathbf k$ turned clockwise by $\pi/2 - \alpha$, a positive rotation about $\mathbf e_\theta$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotation about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper); $\theta$ is the azimuth of the projection of $\overrightarrow{OP_1}$, and the triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$ (opposite leg along $-\mathbf i$) gives its minus sign..
Cylindrical basis: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View from above along $\mathbf k$ (⊙): $\mathbf i$ to the right, $\mathbf j$ up, counterclockwise positive. $\mathbf e_R$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by $\theta$: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ on $\mathbf j$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ on minus $\mathbf i$ (the upward vector leans to the left: the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_R &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j, \quad \mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(the two right triangles)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(right-handed; expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} &= \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 2; diagonal entries equal 1)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_R$ points horizontally from the axis towards the meridian plane of $P_1$.

Cone basis: rotation about e_theta in the meridian plane Law: elementary rotation about the second axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.16; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3

View of the meridian plane of $P_1$ with $\mathbf e_R$ to the right and $\mathbf k$ up: $\mathbf e_\theta$, the cross product of $\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf e_R$, points into the paper (⊗), so a positive rotation about it is clockwise as seen. The generator $OP_1$ makes the semi-angle $\alpha$ with the downward axis, so $\mathbf e_\xi$ is $\mathbf e_R$ turned clockwise by $\pi/2 - \alpha$: a positive rotation of that angle. Its right triangle is the one of $OP_1$: hypotenuse $\xi$, vertical leg $\xi\cos\alpha$ along minus $\mathbf k$ (adjacent to $\alpha$) and horizontal leg $\xi\sin\alpha$ along $\mathbf e_R$ (opposite to $\alpha$); that is the minus sign on $\mathbf k$. The normal $\mathbf n$ is $\mathbf k$ turned by the same angle: it leans towards $\mathbf e_R$, outwards, with components $\cos\alpha$ on $\mathbf e_R$ and $\sin\alpha$ on $\mathbf k$.

$$\begin{aligned} \cos\left(\tfrac{\pi}{2} - \alpha\right) &= \sin\alpha, \quad \sin\left(\tfrac{\pi}{2} - \alpha\right) = \cos\alpha \quad &\text{(complementary angles)} \\ \mathbf e_\xi &= \sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_R - \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k, \quad \mathbf n = \cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_R + \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(columns of the rotation about the second axis, angle $\pi/2 - \alpha$)} \\ \mathbf e_\xi\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k - \cos\alpha\left(\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta\right) = \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_R = \mathbf n \quad &\text{(right-handed; $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= \sin\alpha\cdot\sin\alpha - 0 + \cos\alpha\left(0 + \cos\alpha\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{13} &= -\sin\alpha\cos\alpha + \cos\alpha\sin\alpha = 0, \quad \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{11} = 1 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3)} \\ \mathbf e_R &= \sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf n, \quad \mathbf k = -\cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi + \sin\alpha\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3 of the matrix)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \sin\alpha & 0 & \cos\alpha \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr -\cos\alpha & 0 & \sin\alpha \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_1R_2] = 1, \quad [{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_\xi$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ span the tangent plane of the cone at $P_1$; $\mathbf n$, normal to the cone, is the only direction of its reaction.

Position vectors of both particles Law: position vectors with the constraints substituted Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^{P_1} &= \xi\,\mathbf e_\xi = \xi\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_R - \xi\cos\alpha\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(along the generator; legs of the meridian triangle)} \\ |\overrightarrow{OP_2}| &= \ell - \xi \quad &\text{(inextensible string)} \\ \mathbf r_0^{P_2} &= -\left(\ell - \xi\right)\mathbf k \quad &\text{(on the axis, below $O$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^{P_1} = \xi\,\mathbf e_\xi, \qquad \mathbf r_0^{P_2} = \left(\xi - \ell\right)\mathbf k}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: when $P_1$ moves away from $O$ along the generator, $P_2$ rises by the same length.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the cone basis Law: derivative of a unit vector of a basis turning about the fixed axis k Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{20} &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\alpha$ is constant: the cone basis turns only with $\theta$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\xi &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\left(\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_R - \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k\right) = \dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_R = \mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf k = \mathbf 0$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R = -\dot\theta\left(\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf n\right) \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_R$ in $\mathcal B_2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf e}_\xi = \dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = -\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi - \dot\theta\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the generator sweeps round the cone; the azimuthal vector turns inwards, partly along the generator and partly into the cone.

(a) Position, velocity and acceleration of both particles Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Problems.pdf, p. 19
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^{P_1} &= \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\xi + \xi\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\xi = \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\xi + \xi\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(product rule)} \\ \mathbf a_0^{P_1} &= \ddot\xi\,\mathbf e_\xi + \dot\xi\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta + \left(\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta + \xi\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= \left(\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right)\mathbf e_\xi + \left(2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin\alpha\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{(derivative of $\mathbf e_\theta$; grouped)} \\ \mathbf v_0^{P_2} &= \dot\xi\,\mathbf k, \quad \mathbf a_0^{P_2} = \ddot\xi\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\ell$ constant; $\mathbf k$ fixed)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^{P_1} = \xi\,\mathbf e_\xi, \quad \mathbf v_0^{P_1} = \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\xi + \xi\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta \cr \mathbf a_0^{P_1} = \left(\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right)\mathbf e_\xi + \left(2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin\alpha\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n \cr \mathbf r_0^{P_2} = \left(\xi - \ell\right)\mathbf k, \quad \mathbf v_0^{P_2} = \dot\xi\,\mathbf k, \quad \mathbf a_0^{P_2} = \ddot\xi\,\mathbf k \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$ in every term: e.g. $[\xi\dot\theta^2] = [\dot\xi\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $\xi\sin\alpha$ is the radius of the parallel of $P_1$; the velocity of $P_1$ has no normal component, as the cone requires, while its acceleration has one: the part of the centripetal acceleration that the normal reaction must supply.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagrams of the two particles show the weights, the tension pulling each one towards the aperture, the normal reaction of the cone on $P_1$ and the reaction of the guide on $P_2$. The frame $S_0$ is inertial.

Figure 3. Forces on $P_1$ and $P_2$. The string pulls each particle towards $O$ with the same tension $T$; the cone acts on $P_1$ along its normal $\mathbf n$ and the guide on $P_2$ only normal to the axis. Drawn for the circular motion, with $T = mg$..
(b) Forces on both particles Law: weight; tension of a massless string through a smooth aperture; reactions in the normal subspaces Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} -mg\,\mathbf k &= mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi - mg\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{($\mathbf k$ in $\mathcal B_2$)} \\ \mathbf F^{P_1} &= mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_\xi - mg\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n - T\,\mathbf e_\xi + N\,\mathbf n \quad &\text{(weight, tension towards $O$, normal reaction)} \\ \mathbf F^{P_2} &= -mg\,\mathbf k + T\,\mathbf k + R_{2x}\,\mathbf i + R_{2y}\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(weight, tension towards $O$, guide reaction normal to the axis)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf F^{P_1} = \left(mg\cos\alpha - T\right)\mathbf e_\xi + \left(N - mg\sin\alpha\right)\mathbf n, \qquad \mathbf F^{P_2} = \left(T - mg\right)\mathbf k + R_{2x}\,\mathbf i + R_{2y}\,\mathbf j}$$

Units: every force $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: no force on $P_1$ has an azimuthal component: gravity, the string and the normal all lie in the meridian plane.

(c) Newton's second law for each particle and the decoupled equations Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with all the constraint forces; elimination of the tension Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.11; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^{P_1} &= \mathbf F^{P_1}, \quad m\,\mathbf a_0^{P_2} = \mathbf F^{P_2} \quad &\text{(all forces of box (b), reactions included)} \\ P_1,\ \mathbf e_\xi:\ m\left(\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right) &= mg\cos\alpha - T \quad &\text{(along the generator)} \\ P_1,\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ m\left(2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\sin\alpha &= 0 \quad &\text{(azimuthal)} \\ P_1,\ \mathbf n:\ -m\xi\dot\theta^2\sin\alpha\cos\alpha &= N - mg\sin\alpha \quad &\text{(normal to the cone)} \\ P_2,\ \mathbf k:\ m\ddot\xi &= T - mg \quad &\text{(along the axis)} \\ P_2,\ \mathbf i,\ \mathbf j:\ 0 &= R_{2x}, \quad 0 = R_{2y} \quad &\text{(normal to the axis)} \\ m\left(\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right) &= mg\cos\alpha - m\left(\ddot\xi + g\right) \quad &\text{(tension from the equation of $P_2$ substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} 2\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right) = 0, \qquad 2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta = 0 \cr T = m\left(g + \ddot\xi\right), \qquad N = m\sin\alpha\left(g - \xi\dot\theta^2\cos\alpha\right), \qquad \mathbf R_2 = \mathbf 0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\ddot\xi] = [\xi\dot\theta^2] = [g] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[T] = [N] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: six scalar equations for the six unknowns $\xi$, $\theta$, $T$, $N$ and the two guide components; the first two contain no reaction and give the motion, the others give the tension and the reactions afterwards. The effective inertia of the string motion is $2m$, since both particles move with the rate of $\xi$.

Answers and Checks

(d) Tense string

Condition for the string to stay tense Law: a string can only pull: the tension must stay positive (unilateral constraint) Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]
$$\begin{aligned} T &= m\left(g + \ddot\xi\right) \gt 0 \iff \ddot\xi \gt -g \quad &\text{(equation of $P_2$)} \\ \ddot\xi &= \tfrac12\left[\xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha - g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)\right] \quad &\text{(decoupled equation of $\xi$)} \\ T &= m\left[g + \tfrac12\xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha - \tfrac12 g + \tfrac12 g\cos\alpha\right] = \tfrac{m}{2}\left[g\left(1 + \cos\alpha\right) + \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right] \quad &\text{(substitution)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\ddot\xi(t) \gt -g \ \text{for all } t; \qquad T = \tfrac{m}{2}\left[g\left(1 + \cos\alpha\right) + \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha\right] \gt 0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the hanging particle must never accelerate downwards faster than free fall; along any motion of these equations the tension is a sum of positive terms, so the condition holds automatically while both particles are away from $O$.

Discrepancy with the official solution

The key (Problems.pdf, p. 74, item 31) states the condition as the second derivative of $\xi$ greater than $g$, with no minus sign. This solution keeps the second derivative greater than minus $g$: the equation of $P_2$ gives the tension as $m$ times the sum of $g$ and that second derivative, and the key's own circular motion of part (e), with the tension $mg$ and a constant $\xi$, would violate the condition as printed.

(e) Circular motion on a parallel

Angular rate, tension and reaction on a parallel Law: constant generator length in the decoupled equations; the reactions follow Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Problems.pdf, p. 19
$$\begin{aligned} -\xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{($\dot\xi = \ddot\xi = 0$; the azimuthal equation gives $\ddot\theta = 0$)} \\ \dot\theta^2 &= \frac{g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)}{\xi\sin^2\alpha} = \frac{g}{\xi\left(1 + \cos\alpha\right)} \quad &\text{($\sin^2\alpha = (1 - \cos\alpha)(1 + \cos\alpha)$)} \\ T &= m\left(g + 0\right) = mg \quad &\text{(equation of $P_2$)} \\ N &= m\sin\alpha\left(g - \frac{g\cos\alpha}{1 + \cos\alpha}\right) = \frac{mg\sin\alpha}{1 + \cos\alpha} = \frac{mg\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)}{\sin\alpha} \quad &\text{(reaction formula; same identity)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot\theta = \sqrt{\frac{g}{\xi\left(1 + \cos\alpha\right)}}, \quad -T\,\mathbf e_\xi = mg\left(-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_R + \cos\alpha\,\mathbf k\right), \quad N\,\mathbf n = \frac{mg\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)}{\sin\alpha}\left(\cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_R + \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k\right), \quad \mathbf R_2 = \mathbf 0}$$

Units: $[\sqrt{g/\xi}] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$; forces $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $P_2$ hangs at rest, so the string carries exactly its weight; on $P_1$ the horizontal parts of tension and reaction give the centripetal force and their vertical parts carry the weight. The reaction is positive: the cone pushes outwards on $P_1$.

(f) Two first integrals

Areal integral and energy integral derived from the equations of motion Law: exact derivatives (product and chain rules); Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(\xi^2\dot\theta\right) &= 2\xi\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi^2\ddot\theta = \xi\left(2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right) = 0 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules; azimuthal equation)} \\ \left[\xi^2\dot\theta\right]_0^t &= 0 \Rightarrow \xi^2\dot\theta = C_1 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule)} \\ 2\dot\xi\ddot\xi - \frac{C_1^2\sin^2\alpha}{\xi^3}\,\dot\xi + g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)\dot\xi &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of $\xi$ times $\dot\xi$; $\xi\dot\theta^2 = C_1^2/\xi^3$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\dot\xi^2\right) &= 2\dot\xi\ddot\xi, \quad \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{C_1^2\sin^2\alpha}{2\xi^2}\right) = -\frac{C_1^2\sin^2\alpha}{\xi^3}\,\dot\xi \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\dot\xi^2 + \frac{C_1^2\sin^2\alpha}{2\xi^2} + g\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right)\xi\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(sum of exact derivatives; Barrow's rule)} \\ 2\dot\xi^2 + \xi^2\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + 2g\xi\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right) &= C_2 \quad &\text{(times 2; $C_1^2/\xi^2 = \xi^2\dot\theta^2$)} \\ E &= m\dot\xi^2 + \tfrac12 m\xi^2\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha - mg\xi\cos\alpha - mg\left(\ell - \xi\right) = \tfrac{m}{2}\,C_2 - mg\ell \quad &\text{(kinetic plus potential energy of both particles)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\xi^2\dot\theta = C_1, \qquad 2\dot\xi^2 + \xi^2\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + 2g\xi\left(1 - \cos\alpha\right) = C_2}$$

Units: $[C_1] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-1}}]$; every term of the second integral $[\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the first is the angular momentum of $P_1$ about the axis divided by $m\sin^2\alpha$, constant because no force has a moment about the axis; the second is the mechanical energy of the pair: the stationary smooth constraints do no work and the string only transfers energy from one particle to the other, its works on the two ends cancelling.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^{P_1} = \xi\,\mathbf e_\xi$; $\mathbf v_0^{P_1} = \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\xi + \xi\dot\theta\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta$; $\mathbf a_0^{P_1} = (\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha)\,\mathbf e_\xi + (2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta)\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\theta - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin\alpha\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n$; $\mathbf r_0^{P_2} = (\xi - \ell)\,\mathbf k$, $\mathbf v_0^{P_2} = \dot\xi\,\mathbf k$, $\mathbf a_0^{P_2} = \ddot\xi\,\mathbf k$
(b) Forces$P_1$: $-mg\,\mathbf k$, $-T\,\mathbf e_\xi$, $N\,\mathbf n$; $P_2$: $-mg\,\mathbf k$, $T\,\mathbf k$, $\mathbf R_2 \perp \mathbf k$
(c) Equations$2\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + g(1 - \cos\alpha) = 0$; $2\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta = 0$; $T = m(g + \ddot\xi)$; $N = m\sin\alpha(g - \xi\dot\theta^2\cos\alpha)$; $\mathbf R_2 = \mathbf 0$
(d) Tense string$\ddot\xi \gt -g$ (always satisfied: $T = \frac m2[g(1 + \cos\alpha) + \xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha]$)
(e) Circular parallel$\xi\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha = g(1 - \cos\alpha)$; $T = mg$; $N = mg(1 - \cos\alpha)/\sin\alpha$ along $\mathbf n = \cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_R + \sin\alpha\,\mathbf k$; $\mathbf R_2 = \mathbf 0$
(f) First integrals$\xi^2\dot\theta = C_1$; $2\dot\xi^2 + \xi^2\dot\theta^2\sin^2\alpha + 2g\xi(1 - \cos\alpha) = C_2$

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 74, item 31, gives two degrees of freedom, the same two first integrals, the same condition for the circular parallel and the same tension and normal force (match), with two remarks. The key writes the direction of the normal force with $\mathbf e_\theta$ where this solution has $\mathbf k$: a combination of a horizontal radial and a horizontal azimuthal vector would not be normal to the cone, so the key's symbol is read as the vertical unit vector, and the magnitudes agree. The condition for a tense string differs by a sign (warning box of part (d)).

Limiting case: a flat table with a hole Law: the equations with a right semi-angle Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4

With a semi-angle of a right angle the cone becomes the horizontal plane through $O$ and $P_1$ moves on a table, with $P_2$ hanging through the hole.

$$\begin{aligned} \cos\alpha &\to 0, \quad \sin\alpha \to 1 \quad &\text{($\alpha \to \pi/2$)} \\ 2\ddot\xi - \xi\dot\theta^2 + g &= 0, \quad \dot\theta^2 \to \frac{g}{\xi}, \quad N \to mg \quad &\text{(equation of $\xi$, circular rate, reaction)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\alpha \to \tfrac{\pi}{2}:\ m\xi\dot\theta^2 = mg \ \text{on a circle}, \quad N = mg}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the classical table-with-a-hole problem: on a circle the centripetal force of $P_1$ is the weight of $P_2$ carried by the string, and the table carries the weight of $P_1$.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 32

Particle with String on Spool

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 20
Rheonomic Spool ConstraintIn-Class Session 6

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ is connected by a string of total length $l = 4\pi a$ to a cylindrical spool of radius $a$ and center at $O$ as shown in the figure. The spool rotates at a known constant angular rate $\omega$ counterclockwise. The string is massless, inelastic, thin, and remains always in tension during the motion of the particle. We define the inertial reference frame $Oxy$ as shown in the figure. The other end of the string is attached to the spool at a point $A$ of its perimeter, and we shall call $B$ the instantaneous point of tangency between the straight segment of the string (the segment $BP$) and the spool. We will denote the angle between the local horizontal (axis $Ox$) and the radius $OB$ as $\phi$. Initially, $\mathbf{OA} = -a\mathbf{i}$, and $B$ never reaches $A$.

  • (a)
    Discuss the number of degrees of freedom of the motion of the particle.
  • (b)
    Write down the position, velocity and acceleration vectors of $P$ with respect to $Oxy$ as a function of the constants of the problem, time, the angle $\phi$, and its derivatives.
  • (c)
    Write down the mathematical expression of the forces acting on $P$.
  • (d)
    Is the mechanical energy of $P$ conserved? If so, write down the equation of conservation of mechanical energy; otherwise, explain why it is not conserved.
  • (e)
    Write down a closed set of equations that, if solved, would yield $\phi$ (and the other unknowns of the problem, if any) as a function of time. Note that you do not need to solve the equations.
  • (f)
    Consider now that $\omega = 0$. Can you write down a first integral of motion that only involves $\phi$ and $\dot{\phi}$ (but not $\ddot{\phi}$) in this case? You may want to rethink your previous answers for this particular case.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ hangs from a string of length $l$, equal to $4\pi a$, whose other end is fixed at the point $A$ of a spool of radius $a$ and centre $O$; the spool turns counterclockwise at the constant rate $\omega$, and the string leaves it at the tangency point $B$, at the angle $\phi$ from $Ox$ (Problems.pdf, p. 20). The statement asks for the degrees of freedom, the kinematics of $P$, its forces, whether its mechanical energy is conserved, a closed set of equations and, for a spool at rest, a first integral. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure, origin at the centre of the spool (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$: $\mathbf i$ along $Ox$ (to the right), $\mathbf j$ along $Oy$ (vertical, upward), $\mathbf k$ out of the paper (right-handed)
Basis of the tangency point$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j_1, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\phi$ about $\mathbf k$: $\mathbf i_1$ along $OB$, $\mathbf j_1$ tangent to the spool at $B$ in the counterclockwise sense
Coordinate$\phi$, the angle from $Ox$ to the radius $OB$, counterclockwise positive (statement)
Spoolturns about $\mathbf k$ at the constant rate $\omega \ge 0$; the attachment $A$ turns with it, from $\overrightarrow{OA} = -a\,\mathbf i$ at $t = 0$, so its polar angle is $\pi + \omega t$
Hypotheses$P$ moves in the plane $Oxy$ of the figure (the plane of the cross-section of the spool); the string is massless, inextensible, thin and always tense (statement); the wound part runs from $B$ counterclockwise to $A$ (figure), and $B$ never reaches $A$; $P$ never touches the spool
Constraintsplane of motion, $\mathbf r_0^P\cdot\mathbf k = 0$ (holonomic, stationary, bilateral); string: the straight segment $BP$ is tangent to the spool and its length is $l$ minus the wound arc $AB$ (holonomic, rheonomic when $\omega \ne 0$ because $A$ moves with time, unilateral: the string can only pull) ([Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom (a)3 coordinates of $P$ minus 2 constraint equations: one unknown function, $\phi(t)$. For $\omega \ne 0$ the string constraint contains $t$ explicitly (the lecture adds $t$ as an extra coordinate, [Lecture 3, Slide 21]), but $\omega t$ is prescribed, so $\phi$ is the only coordinate to solve for; for $\omega = 0$ the constraint is stationary and the problem has exactly one degree of freedom ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $a$ [m], $l = 4\pi a$ [m], $\omega$ [rad s$^{-1}$], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf j$
Constraint forcetension of the string, $T \ge 0$ along $PB$, towards $B$ (Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5)
Figure 1. Plane of the motion, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper. The free segment is tangent at $B$ and leaves along $-\mathbf j_1$; it makes the angle $\phi$ with the downward vertical, so its legs are $s\cos\phi$ (vertical) and $s\sin\phi$ (horizontal), and $\mathbf r_0^P = a\,\mathbf i_1 - s\,\mathbf j_1$ with $s = a(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotation by $\phi$ about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper). The triangle of $\mathbf j_1$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf i$, which gives the minus sign in $\mathbf j_1 = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j$..
Basis of the tangency point: rotation about k Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20

View along $\mathbf k$, which points out of the paper (⊙) because $\mathbf k$ is the cross product of $\mathbf i$ (to the right) and $\mathbf j$ (up): a positive rotation about $\mathbf k$ is counterclockwise, as $\phi$ in the figure. $\mathbf i_1$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\phi$: in its right triangle the hypotenuse is 1, the adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ lies along $\mathbf i$ and the opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf j_1$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by $\phi$: the adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ lies along $\mathbf j$ and the opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along minus $\mathbf i$, because the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i_1 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf i + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf i_1$: adjacent leg on $\mathbf i$, opposite leg on $\mathbf j$)} \\ \mathbf j_1 &= -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf j_1$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf i$, adjacent leg on $\mathbf j$)} \\ \mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_1 &= \cos^2\phi\,\mathbf i\times\mathbf j - \sin^2\phi\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = \left(\cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed: $\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = -\mathbf k$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= 1\cdot\left(\cos\phi\cdot\cos\phi - \left(-\sin\phi\right)\sin\phi\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} &= \cos\phi\sin\phi - \sin\phi\cos\phi = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 2; row 3 is the unit row of $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf j &= \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(second row of the matrix, Eq. 1.14)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\phi & -\sin\phi & 0 \cr \sin\phi & \cos\phi & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I, \quad \mathbf j = \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf i_1$ points from $O$ to the tangency point $B$ and $\mathbf j_1$ along the spool at $B$; the last relation projects the weight on $\mathcal B_1$.

Free length of the string Law: inextensible string: total length equals wound arc plus straight segment Source: [Lecture 3, Slide 17]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]; Problems.pdf, p. 20

The wound part goes from $B$, at the polar angle $\phi$, counterclockwise to $A$, at the polar angle $\pi + \omega t$; an arc of a circle of radius $a$ is the radius times the angle it spans. The free length is $s = |BP|$.

$$\begin{aligned} \text{arc } BA &= a\left(\pi + \omega t - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(radius times the angle spanned from $B$ to $A$)} \\ s &= l - a\left(\pi + \omega t - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(constant total length $l$)} \\ &= 4\pi a - \pi a - a\omega t + a\phi \quad &\text{($l = 4\pi a$; brackets removed)} \\ &= a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right) \quad &\text{(common factor $a$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{s(t) = a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right), \qquad 0 \lt \pi + \omega t - \phi \ (\text{$B$ never reaches $A$})}$$

Units: $[a] = [\mathrm{L}]$; $[\omega t] = [\phi] = [1]$, so $[s] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: turning the spool counterclockwise winds the string ($s$ decreases with $t$), while a larger $\phi$ unwinds it; with $\omega = 0$ the free length depends only on $\phi$.

Position vector of the particle Law: position vector as a sum of partial vectors; tangency of the free segment Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4; Problems.pdf, p. 20

$\overrightarrow{OB}$ has length $a$ along $\mathbf i_1$. The free segment is tangent to the spool at $B$ (right angle at $B$ in the figure), so it is along plus or minus $\mathbf j_1$; the wound part continues from $B$ in the sense of plus $\mathbf j_1$ (counterclockwise, towards $A$), so the free part leaves $B$ along minus $\mathbf j_1$, down and to the right in the figure. Because $BP$ is perpendicular to $OB$, which makes the angle $\phi$ with the horizontal, $BP$ makes the angle $\phi$ with the downward vertical: in the right triangle of $BP$ the hypotenuse is $s$, the vertical leg $s\cos\phi$ (adjacent to $\phi$, downwards) and the horizontal leg $s\sin\phi$ (opposite to $\phi$, to the right).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OB} + \overrightarrow{BP} \quad &\text{(sum of partial vectors)} \\ &= a\,\mathbf i_1 - s\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(radius along $\mathbf i_1$; free segment along $-\mathbf j_1$)} \\ &= a\left(\cos\phi\,\mathbf i + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j\right) - s\left(-\sin\phi\,\mathbf i + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j\right) \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$)} \\ &= \left(a\cos\phi + s\sin\phi\right)\mathbf i + \left(a\sin\phi - s\cos\phi\right)\mathbf j \quad &\text{(components grouped)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = a\,\mathbf i_1 - a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\mathbf j_1, \qquad y_P = a\sin\phi - a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\cos\phi}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$ in every term

Meaning: $P$ lies on the involute of the spool traced by the end of the string; the horizontal leg $s\sin\phi$ and the vertical leg $s\cos\phi$ of the free segment are the dashed projections of the figure.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the basis of the tangency point Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= \dot\phi\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ turns about the fixed $\mathbf k$ with the angle $\phi$)} \\ \mathbf k\times\mathbf i_1 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf j - \sin\phi\,\mathbf i = \mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf i = \mathbf j$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf j = -\mathbf i$)} \\ \mathbf k\times\mathbf j_1 &= -\sin\phi\,\mathbf j - \cos\phi\,\mathbf i = -\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(same products)} \\ \dot{\mathbf i}_1 &= \boldsymbol\omega_{10}\times\mathbf i_1 = \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(Eq. 1.62)} \\ \dot{\mathbf j}_1 &= \boldsymbol\omega_{10}\times\mathbf j_1 = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(Eq. 1.62)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf i}_1 = \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1, \qquad \dot{\mathbf j}_1 = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1}$$

Units: $[\dot\phi] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the basis follows the tangency point $B$, not the spool: it turns with $\dot\phi$, whatever $\omega$ is.

Rate of change of the free length Law: chain rule on the free length; the spool rate is constant Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Problems.pdf, p. 20
$$\begin{aligned} \dot s &= \frac{d}{dt}\left[a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\right] = a\left(0 - \omega + \dot\phi\right) \quad &\text{($a$, $\pi$ and $\omega$ constant; chain rule on $\phi(t)$)} \\ \ddot s &= a\left(\ddot\phi - 0\right) = a\ddot\phi \quad &\text{(derivative of $\dot s$; $\omega$ constant)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot s = a\left(\dot\phi - \omega\right), \qquad \ddot s = a\ddot\phi}$$

Units: $[a\dot\phi] = [a\omega] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the free length grows when $B$ advances counterclockwise faster than the attachment $A$.

(b) Velocity and acceleration of the particle Law: time derivatives of the position vector in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^P &= a\,\dot{\mathbf i}_1 - \dot s\,\mathbf j_1 - s\,\dot{\mathbf j}_1 \quad &\text{(product rule on $s\,\mathbf j_1$; $a$ constant)} \\ &= a\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - a\left(\dot\phi - \omega\right)\mathbf j_1 + s\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(derivatives of $\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf j_1$ and $s$)} \\ &= s\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + a\omega\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(the terms in $a\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1$ cancel)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \dot s\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + s\ddot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + s\dot\phi\,\dot{\mathbf i}_1 + a\omega\,\dot{\mathbf j}_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; $a\omega$ constant)} \\ &= a\left(\dot\phi - \omega\right)\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + s\ddot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + s\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf j_1 - a\omega\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(derivatives of $s$, $\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf j_1$)} \\ &= \left(s\ddot\phi + a\dot\phi^2 - 2a\omega\dot\phi\right)\mathbf i_1 + s\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(the two terms in $a\omega\dot\phi$ added)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^P = a\,\mathbf i_1 - a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\mathbf j_1 \cr \mathbf v_0^P = a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + a\omega\,\mathbf j_1 \cr \mathbf a_0^P = a\left[\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\ddot\phi + \dot\phi^2 - 2\omega\dot\phi\right]\mathbf i_1 + a\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf j_1 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[s\dot\phi] = [a\omega] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; $[s\ddot\phi] = [a\dot\phi^2] = [a\omega\dot\phi] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $P$ turns about the instantaneous tangency point $B$ on a circle of radius $s$ (the term $s\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1$, perpendicular to the string) while the spool reels the string in at the speed $a\omega$ along $\mathbf j_1$, from $P$ towards $B$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram of $P$ shows its weight and the tension of the string, directed from $P$ to $B$. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts; $P$ does not touch the spool, which acts only on the string.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$: the weight $-mg\,\mathbf j$ and the tension $T\,\mathbf j_1$ towards $B$. Only the weight has a component along $\mathbf i_1$, which gives the equation of motion free of $T$..
(c) Forces on the particle Law: weight; tension of a massless string along the string, pulling (constraint force of unknown magnitude) Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^B - \mathbf r_0^P &= a\,\mathbf i_1 - \left(a\,\mathbf i_1 - s\,\mathbf j_1\right) = s\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(the string pulls $P$ towards $B$: unit vector $\mathbf j_1$)} \\ \mathbf T &= T\,\mathbf j_1, \quad T \ge 0 \quad &\text{(tension, magnitude unknown; a string can only pull)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j &= -mg\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 - mg\cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j$ in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ \mathbf F &= -mg\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \left(T - mg\cos\phi\right)\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(sum: weight plus tension)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf F = -mg\,\mathbf j + T\,\mathbf j_1 = -mg\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \left(T - mg\cos\phi\right)\mathbf j_1, \qquad T \ge 0}$$

Units: $[mg] = [T] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the tension has no component along $\mathbf i_1$ (perpendicular to the string), so the projection on $\mathbf i_1$ will be free of the unknown $T$.

(e) Newton's second law: a closed set of equations Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame, projected on $\mathcal B_1$; the projection perpendicular to the string is free of the tension Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P &= -mg\,\mathbf j + T\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(all the forces; unknowns $\phi$ and $T$)} \\ \mathbf i_1:\ m\left(s\ddot\phi + a\dot\phi^2 - 2a\omega\dot\phi\right) &= -mg\sin\phi \quad &\text{(dot product with $\mathbf i_1$; no tension)} \\ \mathbf j_1:\ ms\dot\phi^2 &= -mg\cos\phi + T \quad &\text{(dot product with $\mathbf j_1$)} \\ a\left[\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\ddot\phi - 2\omega\dot\phi + \dot\phi^2\right] &= -g\sin\phi \quad &\text{(first projection divided by $m$; $s$ substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} a\left[\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\ddot\phi - 2\omega\dot\phi + \dot\phi^2\right] = -g\sin\phi \cr T = ma\left(3\pi - \omega t + \phi\right)\dot\phi^2 + mg\cos\phi \end{array}}$$
