Units: $[a\ddot\phi] = [a\omega\dot\phi] = [g] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[T] = [ma\dot\phi^2] = [mg] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two scalar equations for the two unknowns $\phi(t)$ and $T(t)$ (the third projection, on $\mathbf k$, is identically zero for planar motion): the first contains only $\phi$ and gives the motion, with a coefficient that depends explicitly on $t$; the second gives the tension afterwards, which must stay positive for the string to be tense.

(d) Power of the tension and the mechanical energy of P Law: theorem of kinetic energy (power of the forces); potential of the weight; power of a time-dependent constraint Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 16]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 m\,\mathbf v_0^P\cdot\mathbf v_0^P\right) &= m\,\mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf v_0^P = \mathbf F\cdot\mathbf v_0^P \quad &\text{(product rule; Newton's second law)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= -mg\,\dot y_P = -\frac{d}{dt}\left(mg\,y_P\right) \quad &\text{(the weight derives from the potential $mg\,y_P$)} \\ T\,\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= T\,\mathbf j_1\cdot\left(s\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + a\omega\,\mathbf j_1\right) = Ta\omega \quad &\text{(power of the tension; $\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf i_1 = 0$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 m\,|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + mg\,y_P\right) &= Ta\omega \quad &\text{(the two powers moved to their sides)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= s^2\dot\phi^2 + a^2\omega^2 \quad &\text{(orthonormal $\mathcal B_1$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E = \tfrac12 m\left(s^2\dot\phi^2 + a^2\omega^2\right) + mg\left(a\sin\phi - s\cos\phi\right), \qquad \dot E = Ta\omega \gt 0 \ \text{for } \omega \gt 0: \ E \ \text{not conserved}}$$

Units: $[E] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$; $[Ta\omega] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}][\mathrm{T^{-1}}] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-3}}]$, a power

Meaning: the string constraint depends explicitly on time, so the velocity of $P$ is not orthogonal to the tension: the spool, driven at constant rate by its motor, reels $P$ in and works on it at the rate $Ta\omega$; since the string is tense, the mechanical energy of $P$ grows. It is conserved only for $\omega = 0$.

Answers and Checks

(f) Spool at rest

With the spool at rest (zero $\omega$) the attachment $A$ stays at $-a\,\mathbf i$, the string constraint becomes stationary and the free length $a(3\pi + \phi)$ depends on $\phi$ alone. Part (a) becomes exactly one degree of freedom, the velocity of part (b) loses its term along $\mathbf j_1$, and the power of the tension of part (d) vanishes.

First integral of the motion for a spool at rest Law: the equation of motion multiplied by a suitable factor is an exact derivative (product and chain rules); Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 16]; Problems.pdf, p. 75
$$\begin{aligned} a\left[\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\ddot\phi + \dot\phi^2\right] + g\sin\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of motion with $\omega = 0$)} \\ a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi\ddot\phi + a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\dot\phi^3 + ga\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\sin\phi\,\dot\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(times $a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\dot\phi$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12 a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi^2\right] &= a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\dot\phi\cdot\dot\phi^2 + a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi\ddot\phi \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[a\sin\phi - a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\cos\phi\right] &= a\cos\phi\,\dot\phi - a\dot\phi\cos\phi + a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\sin\phi\,\dot\phi \quad &\text{(chain and product rules)} \\ &= a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\sin\phi\,\dot\phi \quad &\text{(the terms in $a\cos\phi\,\dot\phi$ cancel)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12 a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi^2 + g\left(a\sin\phi - a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\cos\phi\right)\right] &= 0 \quad &\text{(the second line is this sum of derivatives)} \\ \left[\tfrac12 a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi^2 + ga\left(\sin\phi - \left(3\pi + \phi\right)\cos\phi\right)\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule between $0$ and $t$)} \\ \tfrac12 m a^2\left(3\pi + \phi\right)^2\dot\phi^2 + mga\left[\sin\phi - \left(3\pi + \phi\right)\cos\phi\right] &= E \quad &\text{(times $m$; Barrow's rule gives a constant)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\tfrac12 m\left[a\left(3\pi + \phi\right)\right]^2\dot\phi^2 + mga\left[\sin\phi - \left(3\pi + \phi\right)\cos\phi\right] = E = \text{const}}$$

Units: $[m a^2\dot\phi^2] = [mga] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the mechanical energy of part (d) with $\omega = 0$: kinetic energy of the circular motion about $B$ plus the potential of the weight, with the height $y_P$ of the position box. It contains $\phi$ and its rate only, and with the initial data it reduces the motion to a quadrature.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Degrees of freedom$\omega = 0$: 1 degree of freedom ($\phi$). $\omega \ne 0$: one unknown coordinate $\phi(t)$, plus time as the extra coordinate of a rheonomic constraint (+1 CDOF, [Lecture 3, Slide 21]); $\omega t$ is prescribed
(b) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^P = a\,\mathbf i_1 - a(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\,\mathbf j_1$; $\mathbf v_0^P = a(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + a\omega\,\mathbf j_1$; $\mathbf a_0^P = a[(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\ddot\phi + \dot\phi^2 - 2\omega\dot\phi]\,\mathbf i_1 + a(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf j_1$, with $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\phi\,\mathbf i + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf j_1 = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j$
(c) Forcesweight $-mg\,\mathbf j = -mg(\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1)$; tension $T\,\mathbf j_1$ towards $B$, $T \ge 0$
(d) Energynot conserved for $\omega \ne 0$: $\dot E = Ta\omega$ (power of the tension of the moving spool)
(e) Closed set$a[(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\ddot\phi - 2\omega\dot\phi + \dot\phi^2] = -g\sin\phi$; $T = ma(3\pi - \omega t + \phi)\dot\phi^2 + mg\cos\phi$
(f) Spool at rest$\frac12 m[a(3\pi + \phi)]^2\dot\phi^2 + mga[\sin\phi - (3\pi + \phi)\cos\phi] = E$

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 75, item 32, prints the two projections of Newton's second law along $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf j_1$ exactly as in the box of part (e) (the same equation for $\phi$ and the same equation for the tension $T$), states that the mechanical energy is conserved only for a spool at rest, and prints the same first integral as the box of part (f): match, term by term.

Limiting case: tangency point held fixed Law: the velocity of the kinematics box with a constant angle Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3

If $\phi$ stays constant, $B$ does not move and the spool simply reels the straight string in.

$$\begin{aligned} \dot\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(fixed tangency point)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= 0\cdot\mathbf i_1 + a\omega\,\mathbf j_1 = a\omega\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(velocity of part (b))} \\ \dot s &= a\left(0 - \omega\right) = -a\omega \quad &\text{(rate of the free length)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot\phi = 0:\ \mathbf v_0^P = a\omega\,\mathbf j_1, \quad |\mathbf v_0^P| = -\dot s = a\omega}$$

Units: $[a\omega] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: $P$ moves straight towards $B$ at the peripheral speed of the spool, exactly the rate at which the string shortens: the kinematics is consistent with the inextensible string.

Limiting case: small oscillations of the spool at rest Law: equilibrium of the equation of motion and its linearisation about it Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6; [Lecture 6, Slide 15]

With $\omega = 0$, the state with constant zero $\phi$ satisfies the equation of motion (the sine vanishes): $B$ is at $(a, 0)$ and $P$ hangs vertically below it at the depth $3\pi a$. For a small $\phi$ the sine is replaced by its argument and the products of $\phi$ with its rate or acceleration, of second order, are dropped.

$$\begin{aligned} a\left[\left(3\pi + 0\right)\cdot 0 + 0\right] + g\sin 0 &= 0 \quad &\text{(constant zero $\phi$ is an equilibrium)} \\ T &= 0 + mg\cos 0 = mg \quad &\text{(tension at the equilibrium)} \\ 3\pi a\,\ddot\phi + a\phi\ddot\phi + a\dot\phi^2 + g\sin\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of motion with $\omega = 0$, bracket expanded)} \\ 3\pi a\,\ddot\phi + g\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{($\sin\phi \simeq \phi$; second-order terms dropped)} \\ \ddot\phi + \frac{g}{3\pi a}\,\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(divided by $3\pi a$: the form of Eq. 5.6)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\omega = 0,\ |\phi| \ll 1:\quad T = mg, \qquad \omega_n = \sqrt{\frac{g}{3\pi a}}}$$

Units: $[g/(3\pi a)] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{L}] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$, so $[\omega_n] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: near the vertical the particle swings like a simple pendulum whose length is the free length $3\pi a$ of the string, hanging from the tangency point; the string carries exactly the weight at rest.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 35

String with Two Particles on a Cylinder

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 23
Multi-Particle Cylinder ContactIn-Class Session 13Oscillations - Lecture 6 (part f)

Two heavy particles $P$ and $Q$ of mass $m$ each are connected by an inextensible massless string of constant length $L$. Part of this string rests on top of a cylinder of radius $R$ and center at $O$. The string can slide over the cylinder without friction, and remains tense at all times. Furthermore, $P$ and $Q$ are also connected by a spring of zero natural length and stiffness $k$. We define the inertial reference frame $S_0$ as shown in the figure. We denote as $A$ and $B$ the points of tangency of the string with the cylinder.

  • (a)
    How many degrees of freedom does this system have? Define your own generalized coordinates to parametrize the configuration of the system. You will use them in the rest of the problem.
  • (b)
    Write down the general expression of the position, velocity and acceleration vectors of $P$ and $Q$ relative to the $S_0$ reference frame as a function of your chosen coordinates and their time derivatives.
  • (c)
    Write down the expression of all forces acting on $P$ and $Q$.
  • (d)
    Discuss whether the mechanical energy of $P$ is conserved; if so, write down the expression of the corresponding potential energy.
  • (e)
    Consider now that we restrict the motion of the system to symmetric configurations about the $Oy$ axis only. What is the number of degrees of freedom now? Find the mathematical relations that exist between your previously-defined coordinates in this case.
  • (f)
    Find the approximate value of your coordinates when the system is in equilibrium and the configuration is symmetric about the $Oy$ axis. To do so, assume that the deviation from the vertical of the $AP$ and $BQ$ segments is small, and linearize the equations of motion. Find the angular frequency of the oscillations around this equilibrium configuration.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

Two heavy particles $P$ and $Q$ of mass $m$ hang from the two ends of an inextensible massless string of length $L$ that lies over a smooth fixed cylinder of radius $R$ and centre $O$, touching it between the tangency points $A$ and $B$; a spring of zero natural length and stiffness $k$ joins $P$ and $Q$ (Problems.pdf, p. 23). The statement asks for the degrees of freedom and a set of coordinates, the kinematics, the forces, the mechanical energy of $P$, the symmetric configurations and, for them, the equilibrium and the frequency of small oscillations. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7, with the coordinates of the official key.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure, origin at the centre of the cylinder (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$: $\mathbf i$ along $Ox$ (to the right), $\mathbf j$ along $Oy$ (vertical, upward), $\mathbf k$ along the axis of the cylinder, out of the paper
Basis at $A$$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_A, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k$: $\mathbf e_A$ from $O$ to $A$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ tangent to the cylinder at $A$, counterclockwise
Basis at $B$$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf e_B, \mathbf e_\psi, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\psi$ about $\mathbf k$: $\mathbf e_B$ from $O$ to $B$, $\mathbf e_\psi$ tangent at $B$, counterclockwise
Coordinates (a)$\theta$, polar angle of $A$ from $Ox$; $\psi$, polar angle of $B$ from $Ox$ (with $\psi \gt \theta$); $\xi$, length of the straight segment $AP$. The length of $BQ$ is the auxiliary quantity $\zeta = L - \xi - R(\psi - \theta)$
Hypothesesmotion in the plane $Oxy$ of the figure; the string is tense, touches the cylinder only along the arc from $A$ to $B$ over the top and slides on it without friction; the particles and the spring never touch the cylinder
Constraints$P$ and $Q$ in the plane (2 equations, holonomic, stationary, bilateral); string: $\xi + R(\psi - \theta) + \zeta = L$, with each straight segment tangent to the cylinder (1 equation joining the two particles, holonomic, stationary, unilateral: the string can only pull) ([Lecture 3, Slide 17]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom (a)6 coordinates of two particles minus 3 constraint equations: 3, namely $\theta(t)$, $\psi(t)$, $\xi(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $L$ [m], $R$ [m], $k$ [N m$^{-1}$], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]
Applied forcesweights $-mg\,\mathbf j$ on each particle; spring of zero natural length, $k\overrightarrow{PQ}$ on $P$ and $-k\overrightarrow{PQ}$ on $Q$ (Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5)
Constraint forcestension $T \ge 0$ of the string, along $PA$ on $P$ and along $QB$ on $Q$; the same magnitude at both ends (massless string on a smooth cylinder, [Lecture 3, Slide 17]); the cylinder acts on the string only
Figure 1. Plane of the motion, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper. $AP$ leaves $A$ along $-\mathbf e_\theta$ at the angle $\theta$ from the downward vertical and $BQ$ leaves $B$ along $\mathbf e_\psi$ at the angle $\pi - \psi$; hence $\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_A - \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta$ and $\mathbf r_0^Q = R\,\mathbf e_B + \zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotations about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper) by $\theta$ (left) and by $\psi$ (right). In both, the triangle of the tangent vector has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf i$, which gives the minus sign of $-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i$ and $-\sin\psi\,\mathbf i$..
Bases at the tangency points: two rotations about k Law: elementary rotations about the third axis; change of basis matrices and their checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View along $\mathbf k$, out of the paper (⊙), since $\mathbf k$ is the cross product of $\mathbf i$ (to the right) and $\mathbf j$ (up): positive angles are counterclockwise, as $\theta$ and $\psi$ in the figure. $\mathbf e_A$ is $\mathbf i$ turned by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf i$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j$ turned by $\theta$: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf j$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along minus $\mathbf i$ (the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left: the minus sign). The basis at $B$ is the same construction with the angle $\psi$.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_A &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf e_A$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta &= -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf i$)} \\ \mathbf e_B &= \cos\psi\,\mathbf i + \sin\psi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(same triangles with $\psi$)} \\ \mathbf e_\psi &= -\sin\psi\,\mathbf i + \cos\psi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(same triangles with $\psi$)} \\ \mathbf e_A\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed; $\mathbf i\times\mathbf j = \mathbf k$, $\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = -\mathbf k$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= 1\cdot\left(\cos\theta\cos\theta + \sin\theta\sin\theta\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} &= \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 2; row 3 is the unit row of $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf j &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf e_A + \cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(second row of $[{}_0R_1]$, Eq. 1.14)} \\ \mathbf j &= \sin\psi\,\mathbf e_B + \cos\psi\,\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(second row of $[{}_0R_2]$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad [{}_0R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\psi & -\sin\psi & 0 \cr \sin\psi & \cos\psi & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det = 1, \quad RR^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: each tangency point carries its own polar basis; the checks of $[{}_0R_2]$ are those of $[{}_0R_1]$ with $\psi$ in place of $\theta$.

Length of the second straight segment Law: inextensible string: straight segments plus the wound arc equal the total length Source: [Lecture 3, Slide 17]; Problems.pdf, p. 23

The string touches the cylinder from $A$, at the polar angle $\theta$, counterclockwise over the top to $B$, at $\psi$; the arc is the radius times the angle it spans.

$$\begin{aligned} \text{arc } AB &= R\left(\psi - \theta\right) \quad &\text{(radius times the angle from $A$ to $B$)} \\ \xi + R\left(\psi - \theta\right) + \zeta &= L \quad &\text{(constant length)} \\ \zeta &= L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right) \quad &\text{(solved for the length of $BQ$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\zeta = L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right)}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$ in every term ($\theta$, $\psi$ in radians)

Meaning: the length of $BQ$ is not a fourth coordinate: it follows from the three chosen ones.

Position vectors of both particles Law: position vectors as sums of partial vectors; tangency of the straight segments Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4; Problems.pdf, p. 23

Each straight segment is tangent to the cylinder at its tangency point, so it is perpendicular to the radius there. At $A$ the wound part continues counterclockwise (along $\mathbf e_\theta$), so $AP$ leaves along minus $\mathbf e_\theta$, down and to the right. At $B$ the wound part continues clockwise (along minus $\mathbf e_\psi$), so $BQ$ leaves along $\mathbf e_\psi$, down and to the left. Since $AP$ is perpendicular to $OA$, which makes the angle $\theta$ with the horizontal, $AP$ makes the angle $\theta$ with the downward vertical: in its right triangle the hypotenuse is $\xi$, the vertical leg $\xi\cos\theta$ (adjacent, downwards) and the horizontal leg $\xi\sin\theta$ (opposite, to the right). In the same way $BQ$ makes the angle $\pi - \psi$ with the downward vertical: hypotenuse $\zeta$, horizontal leg $\zeta\sin(\pi - \psi)$ to the left and vertical leg $\zeta\cos(\pi - \psi)$ downwards.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OA} + \overrightarrow{AP} = R\,\mathbf e_A - \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(radius to $A$; segment along $-\mathbf e_\theta$)} \\ &= \left(R\cos\theta + \xi\sin\theta\right)\mathbf i + \left(R\sin\theta - \xi\cos\theta\right)\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$)} \\ \mathbf r_0^Q &= \overrightarrow{OB} + \overrightarrow{BQ} = R\,\mathbf e_B + \zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(radius to $B$; segment along $\mathbf e_\psi$)} \\ &= \left(R\cos\psi - \zeta\sin\psi\right)\mathbf i + \left(R\sin\psi + \zeta\cos\psi\right)\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_2$ in $\mathcal B_0$)} \\ \sin\left(\pi - \psi\right) &= \sin\psi, \quad \cos\left(\pi - \psi\right) = -\cos\psi \quad &\text{(supplementary angles: legs $\zeta\sin\psi$ to the left, $-\zeta\cos\psi$ downwards)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_A - \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \mathbf r_0^Q = R\,\mathbf e_B + \left[L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right)\right]\mathbf e_\psi}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the position of each particle is the tangency point plus the hanging segment; in the figure $\psi$ lies between $\pi/2$ and $\pi$, so the cosine of $\psi$ is negative and the vertical leg of $BQ$ is positive downwards.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the two bases Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k, \quad \boldsymbol\omega_{20} = \dot\psi\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(each basis turns about the fixed $\mathbf k$ with its own angle)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_A &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_A = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_A = \cos\theta\,\mathbf j - \sin\theta\,\mathbf i$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf j - \cos\theta\,\mathbf i$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_B &= \dot\psi\,\mathbf e_\psi, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\psi = -\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B \quad &\text{(same products with $\psi$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf e}_A = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_B = \dot\psi\,\mathbf e_\psi, \quad \dot{\mathbf e}_\psi = -\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B}$$

Units: $[\dot\theta] = [\dot\psi] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the bases follow the tangency points, which slide round the cylinder as the configuration changes.

(b) Position, velocity and acceleration of P Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^P &= R\,\dot{\mathbf e}_A - \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\theta - \xi\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta \quad &\text{(product rule; $R$ constant)} \\ &= R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta - \dot\xi\,\mathbf e_\theta + \xi\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A \quad &\text{(derivatives of the basis)} \\ &= \xi\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(grouped)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \left(\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_A + \xi\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_A + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\theta + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= \left(\dot\xi\dot\theta + \xi\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_A + \xi\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_\theta + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\theta - \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A \quad &\text{(derivatives of the basis)} \\ &= \left(\xi\ddot\theta + 2\dot\xi\dot\theta - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_A + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi + \xi\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(grouped)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_A - \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \mathbf v_0^P = \xi\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\theta \cr \mathbf a_0^P = \left(\xi\ddot\theta + 2\dot\xi\dot\theta - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_A + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi + \xi\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_\theta \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\xi\dot\theta] = [\dot\xi] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; $[\xi\ddot\theta] = [\dot\xi\dot\theta] = [R\dot\theta^2] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the component along $\mathbf e_\theta$ (along the string, positive towards $A$) is the speed at which the string is pulled over $A$; the component along $\mathbf e_A$ is the swing of the segment.

(b) Position, velocity and acceleration of Q Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule; the length of $BQ$ differentiated with the chain rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\zeta &= -\dot\xi - R\left(\dot\psi - \dot\theta\right) \quad &\text{(derivative of the length of $BQ$; $L$, $R$ constant)} \\ R\dot\psi + \dot\zeta &= R\dot\theta - \dot\xi \quad &\text{(previous row with $R\dot\psi$ moved to the left: the rate along each segment is the same)} \\ R\ddot\psi + \ddot\zeta &= R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi \quad &\text{(time derivative of the previous row)} \\ \mathbf v_0^Q &= R\,\dot{\mathbf e}_B + \dot\zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi + \zeta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\psi \quad &\text{(product rule on $\zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi$; $R$ constant)} \\ &= R\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_\psi + \dot\zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi - \zeta\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B \quad &\text{(derivatives of $\mathbf e_B$ and $\mathbf e_\psi$)} \\ &= -\zeta\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(grouped; $R\dot\psi + \dot\zeta = R\dot\theta - \dot\xi$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^Q &= -\left(\dot\zeta\dot\psi + \zeta\ddot\psi\right)\mathbf e_B - \zeta\dot\psi\,\dot{\mathbf e}_B + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\psi + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\dot{\mathbf e}_\psi \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= -\left(\dot\zeta\dot\psi + \zeta\ddot\psi\right)\mathbf e_B - \zeta\dot\psi^2\,\mathbf e_\psi + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\psi - \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B \quad &\text{(derivatives of $\mathbf e_B$ and $\mathbf e_\psi$)} \\ &= -\left(\dot\zeta\dot\psi + \zeta\ddot\psi\right)\mathbf e_B - \zeta\dot\psi^2\,\mathbf e_\psi + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\psi - \left(R\dot\psi + \dot\zeta\right)\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B \quad &\text{($R\dot\theta - \dot\xi = R\dot\psi + \dot\zeta$)} \\ &= -\left(\zeta\ddot\psi + 2\dot\zeta\dot\psi + R\dot\psi^2\right)\mathbf e_B + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi - \zeta\dot\psi^2\right)\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(grouped)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^Q = R\,\mathbf e_B + \zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi, \quad \mathbf v_0^Q = -\zeta\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B + \left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right)\mathbf e_\psi \cr \mathbf a_0^Q = -\left(\zeta\ddot\psi + 2\dot\zeta\dot\psi + R\dot\psi^2\right)\mathbf e_B + \left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi - \zeta\dot\psi^2\right)\mathbf e_\psi \cr \zeta = L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right), \quad \dot\zeta = R\dot\theta - R\dot\psi - \dot\xi \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\zeta\dot\psi] = [R\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $Q$ moves along its segment ($\mathbf e_\psi$, away from $B$) at the same rate at which $P$ moves towards $A$: whatever the string gains on one side it loses on the other.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagrams show on each particle its weight, the tension towards its tangency point and the force of the spring towards the other particle. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ and $Q$. The string pulls each particle towards its tangency point with the same tension $T$; the spring of zero natural length pulls them towards each other with $\pm k\overrightarrow{PQ}$..
(c) Forces on both particles Law: weight; spring of zero natural length; tension of a massless string, equal at both ends on a smooth support Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]; Problems.pdf, p. 75
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^A - \mathbf r_0^P &= \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad \mathbf r_0^B - \mathbf r_0^Q = -\zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(directions from each particle to its tangency point)} \\ \overrightarrow{PQ} &= \mathbf r_0^Q - \mathbf r_0^P = R\left(\mathbf e_B - \mathbf e_A\right) + \zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi + \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(difference of the position vectors)} \\ \mathbf F_{S,P} &= k\overrightarrow{PQ} = kR\left(\mathbf e_B - \mathbf e_A\right) + k\left[L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right)\right]\mathbf e_\psi + k\xi\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(zero natural length: the force is $k$ times the extension vector)} \\ \mathbf F_{S,Q} &= -\mathbf F_{S,P} \quad &\text{(action and reaction of the massless spring)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf F^P = -mg\,\mathbf j + T\,\mathbf e_\theta + k\overrightarrow{PQ} \cr \mathbf F^Q = -mg\,\mathbf j - T\,\mathbf e_\psi - k\overrightarrow{PQ} \cr k\overrightarrow{PQ} = kR\left(\mathbf e_B - \mathbf e_A\right) + k\left[L - \xi - R\left(\psi - \theta\right)\right]\mathbf e_\psi + k\xi\,\mathbf e_\theta, \quad T \ge 0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[mg] = [T] = [kR] = [k\xi] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the tension is the only reaction on the particles, a single unknown $T(t)$ shared by both, since the smooth cylinder acts only on the string; the spring pair is equal and opposite.

Newton's second law for both particles Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame with all the forces, each particle projected on its own basis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

The weight is projected with the rows of the matrices; the spring with the dot products of $\overrightarrow{PQ}$, written $\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_A$ and so on.

$$\begin{aligned} P,\ \mathbf e_A:\ m\left(\xi\ddot\theta + 2\dot\xi\dot\theta - R\dot\theta^2\right) &= -mg\sin\theta + k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_A \quad &\text{(no tension: $\mathbf e_\theta\cdot\mathbf e_A = 0$)} \\ P,\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ m\left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi + \xi\dot\theta^2\right) &= -mg\cos\theta + T + k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(along $PA$)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf e_B:\ -m\left(\zeta\ddot\psi + 2\dot\zeta\dot\psi + R\dot\psi^2\right) &= -mg\sin\psi - k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_B \quad &\text{(no tension: $\mathbf e_\psi\cdot\mathbf e_B = 0$)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf e_\psi:\ m\left(R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi - \zeta\dot\psi^2\right) &= -mg\cos\psi - T - k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_\psi \quad &\text{(along $BQ$)} \\ m\left(2R\ddot\theta - 2\ddot\xi + \xi\dot\theta^2 - \zeta\dot\psi^2\right) &= -mg\left(\cos\theta + \cos\psi\right) + k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\left(\mathbf e_\theta - \mathbf e_\psi\right) \quad &\text{(sum of the two string projections: $T$ eliminated)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\text{3 equations free of } T \text{ (the } \mathbf e_A \text{, } \mathbf e_B \text{ projections and their string sum) for } \theta, \psi, \xi; \quad T \text{ from the } \mathbf e_\theta \text{ projection}}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: four scalar equations for the four unknowns $\theta$, $\psi$, $\xi$ and $T$; the motion decouples from the tension, which is computed afterwards and must stay positive.

(d) Work of the forces on P and the energy of the pair Law: power of a force; the tension transfers energy between the particles; potential of the weights and of the spring Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} T\,\mathbf e_\theta\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= T\left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right) \quad &\text{(power of the tension on $P$: not zero in general)} \\ -T\,\mathbf e_\psi\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q &= -T\left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right) \quad &\text{(power of the tension on $Q$)} \\ k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf v_0^P - k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q &= -k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\frac{d\overrightarrow{PQ}}{dt} = -\frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2\right) \quad &\text{(power of the spring on both; chain rule)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j\cdot\left(\mathbf v_0^P + \mathbf v_0^Q\right) &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left[mg\left(y_P + y_Q\right)\right] \quad &\text{(power of the weights)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12 m\left(|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + |\mathbf v_0^Q|^2\right)\right] &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left[mg\left(y_P + y_Q\right) + \tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2\right] \quad &\text{(sum of all powers; the two tension powers cancel)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} E_P \ \text{not conserved: } \dot E_P = T\left(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi\right) + \text{power of the spring, which depends on the motion of } Q \cr E = \tfrac12 m\left(|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + |\mathbf v_0^Q|^2\right) + V = \text{const}, \quad V = mg\left(R\sin\theta - \xi\cos\theta + R\sin\psi + \zeta\cos\psi\right) + \tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[mgR] = [k\xi^2] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$; powers $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-3}}]$

Meaning: the mechanical energy of $P$ alone is not conserved: the string works on $P$ whenever it slides over $A$, and the spring force on $P$ depends on where $Q$ is, so it has no potential of the position of $P$ alone. The pair is conservative: the string only transfers energy from one particle to the other and the spring is an internal conservative force, so the potential energy is the one in the box.

Answers and Checks

(e) Symmetric configurations

Coordinates of a configuration symmetric about Oy Law: mirror symmetry about the vertical axis; the length constraint Source: [Lecture 3, Slide 4]; Problems.pdf, p. 23

The mirror in $Oy$ changes the sign of $x$ and keeps $y$; it sends the polar angle $\theta$ to $\pi - \theta$.

$$\begin{aligned} \psi &= \pi - \theta \quad &\text{($B$ is the mirror image of $A$)} \\ R\cos\psi - \zeta\sin\psi &= -R\cos\theta - \zeta\sin\theta \quad &\text{($x_Q$ with $\cos(\pi - \theta) = -\cos\theta$, $\sin(\pi - \theta) = \sin\theta$)} \\ -R\cos\theta - \zeta\sin\theta &= -\left(R\cos\theta + \xi\sin\theta\right) \iff \zeta = \xi \quad &\text{(mirror condition $x_Q = -x_P$)} \\ \xi &= L - \xi - R\left(\pi - \theta - \theta\right) \quad &\text{(length constraint with both relations)} \\ \xi &= \frac{L - \pi R}{2} + R\theta \quad &\text{(solved for $\xi$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\psi = \pi - \theta, \qquad \xi = \zeta = \ell_0 + R\theta, \qquad \ell_0 = \frac{L - \pi R}{2}; \qquad \text{1 degree of freedom, } \theta}$$

Units: $[\ell_0] = [R\theta] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $\ell_0$ is the length of each hanging segment when $A$ and $B$ are at the ends of the horizontal diameter; the symmetric motion keeps one coordinate, and the vertical coordinates of $P$ and $Q$ are then equal.

(f) Equilibrium and small oscillations of the symmetric configuration

Figure 4. Symmetric equilibrium of part (f), angle exaggerated. Because the spring pulls the particles together, the segments lean inwards and $\theta_0 \simeq -2kR/(mg + 2k\ell_0)$ is negative..
Equation of motion of a symmetric motion Law: projection of Newton's second law perpendicular to the string (free of the tension), with the symmetric relations Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

With the relations of part (e), the spring is horizontal: $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ is minus twice the abscissa of $P$ times $\mathbf i$, and its projection on $\mathbf e_A$ uses the first row of $[{}_0R_1]$.

$$\begin{aligned} \overrightarrow{PQ} &= -2\left(R\cos\theta + \xi\sin\theta\right)\mathbf i \quad &\text{($x_Q = -x_P$, $y_Q = y_P$)} \\ \overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf e_A &= -2\left(R\cos\theta + \xi\sin\theta\right)\cos\theta \quad &\text{($\mathbf i\cdot\mathbf e_A = \cos\theta$)} \\ \dot\xi &= R\dot\theta \quad &\text{(derivative of $\xi = \ell_0 + R\theta$)} \\ 2\dot\xi\dot\theta - R\dot\theta^2 &= 2R\dot\theta^2 - R\dot\theta^2 = R\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(substitution in the acceleration along $\mathbf e_A$)} \\ m\left(\xi\ddot\theta + R\dot\theta^2\right) &= -mg\sin\theta - 2k\cos\theta\left(R\cos\theta + \xi\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(projection of $P$ on $\mathbf e_A$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\ddot\theta + mR\dot\theta^2 + mg\sin\theta + 2k\cos\theta\left[R\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\sin\theta\right] = 0}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the projection of $Q$ on $\mathbf e_B$ gives the same equation (mirror image of every term), so a symmetric start stays symmetric and no extra force is needed to keep it so.

Linearisation for nearly vertical segments: equilibrium Law: small deviation of the segments from the vertical (the angle $\theta$, as shown in the position box): first-order expansion of the equation of motion; equilibrium with zero rate and acceleration Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; [Lecture 6, Slide 15]

Every term is expanded to first order in $\theta$ and its derivatives: products of two small quantities are dropped.

$$\begin{aligned} m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\ddot\theta &\simeq m\ell_0\ddot\theta \quad &\text{($R\theta\ddot\theta$ is of second order)} \\ mR\dot\theta^2 &\simeq 0 \quad &\text{(second order)} \\ mg\sin\theta &\simeq mg\theta \quad &\text{($\sin\theta \simeq \theta$)} \\ 2k\left[R\cos^2\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\sin\theta\cos\theta\right] &\simeq 2k\left(R + \ell_0\theta\right) \quad &\text{($\cos^2\theta \simeq 1$; $\sin\theta\cos\theta \simeq \theta$; $R\theta^2$ dropped)} \\ m\ell_0\ddot\theta + \left(mg + 2k\ell_0\right)\theta + 2kR &= 0 \quad &\text{(linearised equation)} \\ \left(mg + 2k\ell_0\right)\theta_0 + 2kR &= 0 \quad &\text{(equilibrium: $\ddot\theta = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_0 \simeq -\frac{2kR}{mg + 2k\ell_0}, \qquad \psi_0 = \pi - \theta_0 \simeq \pi + \frac{2kR}{mg + 2k\ell_0}, \qquad \xi_0 = \zeta_0 \simeq \ell_0 - \frac{2kR^2}{mg + 2k\ell_0}}$$

Units: $[kR]/[mg] = [\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}]/[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}] = [1]$ and $[k\ell_0] = [mg]$, so $\theta_0$ is dimensionless; $[kR^2/(mg)] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $\theta_0$ is negative: $A$ sits slightly below the horizontal diameter and the segments lean inwards, because the spring pulls the particles together and the horizontal part of the tension must balance it. The approximation holds when $2kR$ is small compared with the sum of $mg$ and $2k\ell_0$.

Linearisation for nearly vertical segments: frequency Law: deviation from the equilibrium in the linearised equation; harmonic oscillator Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6; [Lecture 6, Slide 6]; [Lecture 6, Slide 15]
$$\begin{aligned} \theta &= \theta_0 + \delta, \quad \ddot\theta = \ddot\delta \quad &\text{(deviation from the equilibrium; $\theta_0$ constant)} \\ m\ell_0\ddot\delta + \left(mg + 2k\ell_0\right)\delta + \left[\left(mg + 2k\ell_0\right)\theta_0 + 2kR\right] &= 0 \quad &\text{(substitution in the linearised equation)} \\ m\ell_0\ddot\delta + \left(mg + 2k\ell_0\right)\delta &= 0 \quad &\text{(the bracket is the equilibrium condition: zero)} \\ \ddot\delta + \left(\frac{g}{\ell_0} + \frac{2k}{m}\right)\delta &= 0 \quad &\text{(divided by $m\ell_0$: the form of Eq. 5.6)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\omega_0 = \sqrt{\frac{g}{\ell_0} + \frac{2k}{m}}, \qquad \ell_0 = \frac{L - \pi R}{2}}$$

Units: $[g/\ell_0] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[k/m] = [\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}]/[\mathrm{M}] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; so $[\omega_0] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: two restoring effects add: gravity, as for a pendulum of length $\ell_0$, and the spring, which pulls both particles inwards when they swing apart. This is the frequency to leading order; the equilibrium shift $\theta_0$ corrects the frequency squared by a first-order factor that comes from the inertia of the segment at the shifted equilibrium (box "Correction of the frequency from the equilibrium shift" below), which is the 2.6 percent of the numerical check.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Degrees of freedom3: $\theta$ (polar angle of $A$), $\psi$ (polar angle of $B$), $\xi$ (length of $AP$); $\zeta = L - \xi - R(\psi - \theta)$
(b) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_A - \xi\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf v_0^P = \xi\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_A + (R\dot\theta - \dot\xi)\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf a_0^P = (\xi\ddot\theta + 2\dot\xi\dot\theta - R\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf e_A + (R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi + \xi\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf e_\theta$; $\mathbf r_0^Q = R\,\mathbf e_B + \zeta\,\mathbf e_\psi$, $\mathbf v_0^Q = -\zeta\dot\psi\,\mathbf e_B + (R\dot\theta - \dot\xi)\,\mathbf e_\psi$, $\mathbf a_0^Q = -(\zeta\ddot\psi + 2\dot\zeta\dot\psi + R\dot\psi^2)\,\mathbf e_B + (R\ddot\theta - \ddot\xi - \zeta\dot\psi^2)\,\mathbf e_\psi$
(c) Forces$P$: $-mg\,\mathbf j$, $T\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $k\overrightarrow{PQ}$; $Q$: $-mg\,\mathbf j$, $-T\,\mathbf e_\psi$, $-k\overrightarrow{PQ}$; $k\overrightarrow{PQ} = kR(\mathbf e_B - \mathbf e_A) + k[L - \xi - R(\psi - \theta)]\,\mathbf e_\psi + k\xi\,\mathbf e_\theta$
(d) Energyof $P$ alone: not conserved (tension power $T(R\dot\theta - \dot\xi)$, spring force depends on $Q$); of the pair: conserved, $V = mg(y_P + y_Q) + \frac12 k|\overrightarrow{PQ}|^2$
(e) Symmetric motion1 degree of freedom; $\psi = \pi - \theta$, $\xi = \zeta = \ell_0 + R\theta$, $\ell_0 = (L - \pi R)/2$
(f) Equilibrium and frequency$\theta_0 \simeq -2kR/(mg + 2k\ell_0)$, $\psi_0 = \pi - \theta_0$, $\xi_0 \simeq \ell_0 - 2kR^2/(mg + 2k\ell_0)$; $\omega_0 = \sqrt{g/\ell_0 + 2k/m}$

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 75, item 35, gives only the spring forces on $P$ and $Q$, written in the same coordinates $\theta$, $\psi$, $\xi$ and the same unit vectors: the key's vector is term by term the one of the box of part (c) (match). The other parts have no key; the box below checks the linearisation of part (f) against the exact nonlinear equation with sample data.

Independent check of the linearisation with sample data Law: exact equilibrium of the nonlinear symmetric equation (root found by bisection) and its exact stiffness, compared with the first-order formulas Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; [Lecture 6, Slide 15]

Sample data: $R$ of 1 m, $\ell_0$ of 2 m, $m$ of 1 kg, $k$ of 0.3 N/m, $g$ of 9.81 m/s². The nonlinear equilibrium condition is $f(\theta)$ equal to zero, with $f$ the sum of the weight term and the spring term of the symmetric equation of motion.

$$\begin{aligned} \theta_0^{\text{lin}} &= -\frac{2(0.3)(1)}{(1)(9.81) + 2(0.3)(2)} = -\frac{0.6}{11.01} = -0.05450\ \text{rad} \quad &\text{(formula of part (f))} \\ f(\theta) &= mg\sin\theta + 2k\cos\theta\left[R\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\sin\theta\right] = 0 \ \Rightarrow\ \theta_0 = -0.05453\ \text{rad} \quad &\text{(exact root, bisection)} \\ \omega_0^{2,\text{lin}} &= \frac{9.81}{2} + \frac{2(0.3)}{1} = 4.905 + 0.600 = 5.505\ \text{s}^{-2} \quad &\text{(formula of part (f))} \\ \omega_0^2 &= \frac{f'(\theta_0)}{m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta_0\right)} = 5.648\ \text{s}^{-2} \quad &\text{(exact stiffness over the exact inertia at the root)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_0:\ -0.05453 \ \text{(exact)}\ \text{vs}\ -0.05450 \ \text{(linear)}; \qquad \omega_0^2:\ 5.648 \ \text{vs}\ 5.505\ \text{s}^{-2}}$$

Units: angles in rad; $[\omega_0^2] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the equilibrium agrees to 0.06 percent and the frequency squared to 2.6 percent, a relative difference of the order of the small angle itself (5 percent), as a first-order approximation allows: the linearised results are confirmed.

Correction of the frequency from the equilibrium shift Law: linearisation of the symmetric equation of motion about the exact equilibrium (first-order Taylor expansion); product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6; [Lecture 6, Slide 15]

The symmetric equation of motion of part (f) is written with the function $f$ of the check box (weight term plus spring term), and the motion is taken about the exact equilibrium $\theta_0$, where $f$ vanishes.

$$\begin{aligned} \theta &= \theta_0 + \delta, \quad \ddot\theta = \ddot\delta \quad &\text{(deviation from the equilibrium; $\theta_0$ constant)} \\ f\left(\theta_0 + \delta\right) &\simeq f\left(\theta_0\right) + f'\left(\theta_0\right)\delta = f'\left(\theta_0\right)\delta \quad &\text{(first-order Taylor expansion; $f(\theta_0) = 0$ at the equilibrium)} \\ m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta_0\right)\ddot\delta + f'\left(\theta_0\right)\delta &= 0 \quad &\text{(substitution in the equation of motion; $R\delta\ddot\delta$ and $mR\dot\delta^2$ of second order)} \\ \frac{d}{d\theta}\left\{\cos\theta\left[R\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\sin\theta\right]\right\} &= -\sin\theta\left[R\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\sin\theta\right] + \cos\theta\left[-R\sin\theta + R\sin\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\cos\theta\right] \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule inside the bracket)} \\ &= -R\sin\theta\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\left(\cos^2\theta - \sin^2\theta\right) \quad &\text{(brackets expanded; $-R\sin\theta + R\sin\theta$ cancels)} \\ f'\left(\theta\right) &= mg\cos\theta + 2k\left[-R\sin\theta\cos\theta + \left(\ell_0 + R\theta\right)\left(\cos^2\theta - \sin^2\theta\right)\right] \quad &\text{(derivative of $f$ term by term)} \\ f'\left(\theta_0\right) &\simeq mg + 2k\left(-R\theta_0 + \ell_0 + R\theta_0\right) = mg + 2k\ell_0 \quad &\text{($\cos\theta_0 \simeq 1$, $\sin\theta_0 \simeq \theta_0$; terms in $\theta_0^2$ dropped)} \\ \omega_0^2 &= \frac{f'\left(\theta_0\right)}{m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta_0\right)} \simeq \frac{mg + 2k\ell_0}{m\left(\ell_0 + R\theta_0\right)} \quad &\text{(stiffness over inertia, form of Eq. 5.6)} \\ &= \left(\frac{g}{\ell_0} + \frac{2k}{m}\right)\frac{1}{1 + R\theta_0/\ell_0} \quad &\text{(numerator and denominator divided by $m\ell_0$)} \\ &\simeq \left(\frac{g}{\ell_0} + \frac{2k}{m}\right)\left(1 - \frac{R\theta_0}{\ell_0}\right) \quad &\text{($1/(1 + \varepsilon) \simeq 1 - \varepsilon$ for a small $\varepsilon$)} \\ &= 5.505\left(1 + 0.5\cdot 0.05453\right) = 5.655\ \text{s}^{-2} \quad &\text{(sample data of the check box)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\omega_0^2 \simeq \left(\frac{g}{\ell_0} + \frac{2k}{m}\right)\left(1 - \frac{R\theta_0}{\ell_0}\right) + O\left(\theta_0^2\right); \qquad \text{sample data: } 5.655 \ \text{vs}\ 5.648\ \text{s}^{-2} \ \text{(exact)}}$$

Units: $[R\theta_0/\ell_0] = [\mathrm{L}]/[\mathrm{L}] = [1]$, so the factor is dimensionless and $[\omega_0^2] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the stiffness at the shifted equilibrium is unchanged to first order, but the inertia is that of the segment length $\ell_0 + R\theta_0$, shorter than $\ell_0$ because $\theta_0$ is negative, so the oscillation is slightly faster; this factor accounts for the 2.6 percent of the check box, and the remaining 0.1 percent is of second order.

Limiting cases of the frequency Law: the equilibrium and frequency of part (f) at the ends of the range of the data Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 5.6; Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7
$$\begin{aligned} k \to 0:\quad \theta_0 &\to 0, \quad \omega_0 \to \sqrt{\frac{g}{\ell_0}} \quad &\text{(no spring)} \\ R \to 0:\quad \theta_0 &\to 0, \quad \ell_0 \to \frac{L}{2}, \quad \omega_0 \to \sqrt{\frac{2g}{L} + \frac{2k}{m}} \quad &\text{(cylinder shrunk to a point)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{k \to 0:\ \omega_0 = \sqrt{g/\ell_0}\ \text{(simple pendulum)}; \qquad R \to 0:\ \omega_0 = \sqrt{2g/L + 2k/m}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$ in both limits

Meaning: without the spring each particle is a simple pendulum of length $\ell_0$ hanging vertically from its tangency point; with a vanishing cylinder the two particles hang from one point on segments of length $L/2$, and the spring adds its stiffness to the symmetric swing.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 38

Particle on Torus

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 26
Toroidal GeometryIn-Class Session 12

A heavy point particle $P$ with mass $m_P = 1$ moves without friction on a vertically-standing torus as shown in the figure. The particle cannot separate from the surface of this torus. The torus is defined in the Cartesian coordinates of the inertial reference frame $S_0$ by the following expressions:

$$ \begin{cases} x_0 = (2 + \cos\phi)\sin\theta \\ y_0 = \sin\phi \\ z_0 = 4 + (2 + \cos\phi)\cos\theta \end{cases} $$

A second particle $Q$ with mass $m_Q = 2$ is constrained to move on the $Ox_0y_0$ plane without friction. The two particles $P$ and $Q$ attract each other with a force that is proportional to the distance between them. The proportionality constant of this force is $k = 2$. Initially, the particle $P$ is located at $\mathbf{r}_0^P(t_0) = 2\mathbf{i}_0 + \mathbf{j}_0 + 4\mathbf{k}_0$ with $\mathbf{v}_0^P(t_0) = -\mathbf{k}_0$ and the particle $Q$ is located at $\mathbf{r}_0^Q(t_0) = 3\mathbf{i}_0 + \mathbf{j}_0$ with $\mathbf{v}_0^Q(t_0) = -\mathbf{i}_0 + \mathbf{j}_0$.

  • (a)
    Pick a minimal, valid set of coordinates to describe the configuration of the system. Indicate what is the geometrical meaning of the chosen coordinates.
  • (b)
    Obtain a general expression for the position, velocity, and acceleration vectors of $P$ and $Q$ in $S_0$ using the chosen coordinates and their derivatives.
  • (c)
    Obtain two independent tangent vectors and one normal vector to the torus as a function of the chosen coordinates.
  • (d)
    Identify and express mathematically all the forces acting on $P$ and $Q$.
  • (e)
    Find down a set of second order differential equations to determine the evolution of the chosen coordinates in time, free of any other unknowns.
  • (f)
    Obtain a scalar conservation law.
  • (g)
    Attending to this conservation law alone, can $P$ ever reach the highest point of the torus?
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m_P$ moves without friction on a vertically standing torus and cannot leave it; a second particle $Q$ of mass $m_Q$ moves without friction on the horizontal plane $Ox_0y_0$; the two attract each other with a force proportional to their distance, of constant $k$ (Problems.pdf, p. 26). The statement asks for a minimal set of coordinates, the kinematics of both particles, two tangent vectors and a normal vector of the torus, the forces, the equations of motion free of reactions, a conservation law and whether $P$ can reach the top of the torus. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i_0, \mathbf j_0, \mathbf k_0\}$, $\mathbf k_0$ vertical upward, $\mathbf i_0$ and $\mathbf j_0$ horizontal, right-handed
Torusread from its parametric equations: the centre circle of the tube is $(2\sin\theta, 0, 4 + 2\cos\theta)$, of radius 2, in the vertical plane $Ox_0z_0$, centred at $C = (0, 0, 4)$; the tube has radius 1. The axis of symmetry of the torus is the horizontal line through $C$ parallel to $\mathbf j_0$
Toroidal basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j_1, \mathbf k_1\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf j_0$ ($\mathbf j_1 = \mathbf j_0$): $\mathbf k_1$ from $C$ towards the centre of the tube section that holds $P$, $\mathbf i_1$ along increasing $\theta$
Poloidal basis$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf i_2, \mathbf j_2, \mathbf k_2\}$, $\mathcal B_1$ turned by the angle $\phi$ about $-\mathbf i_1$ ($\mathbf i_2 = \mathbf i_1$): $\mathbf k_2$ the outward normal of the torus at $P$, $\mathbf j_2$ along increasing $\phi$
Coordinates (a)$\theta$: angle of the meridian half-plane of $P$ about the axis of the torus, from $\mathbf k_0$ towards $\mathbf i_0$; $\phi$: angle round the tube in that half-plane, from the outward direction $\mathbf k_1$ towards $\mathbf j_0$; $\xi$, $\eta$: Cartesian coordinates of $Q$ in the plane $Ox_0y_0$ (the letters of the official key)
Constraints$P$ on the torus (1 equation, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral: it cannot separate); $Q$ on the plane, $z_Q = 0$ (1 equation, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral) ([Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom (a)6 coordinates of two particles minus 2 constraint equations: 4, namely $\theta$, $\phi$, $\xi$, $\eta$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m_P = 1$, $m_Q = 2$, $k = 2$, lengths as in the equations of the torus; read as SI units (kg, N m$^{-1}$, m); $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]
Initial conditions$\mathbf r_0^P = 2\mathbf i_0 + \mathbf j_0 + 4\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf v_0^P = -\mathbf k_0$; $\mathbf r_0^Q = 3\mathbf i_0 + \mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf v_0^Q = -\mathbf i_0 + \mathbf j_0$
Applied forcesweights $-m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0$ and $-m_Qg\,\mathbf k_0$; mutual attraction $k\overrightarrow{PQ}$ on $P$ and $-k\overrightarrow{PQ}$ on $Q$ (a zero-length spring, Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5)
Constraint forcesnormal reaction of the torus $N\,\mathbf k_2$ on $P$ (one unknown, Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4); normal reaction of the plane $N_Q\,\mathbf k_0$ on $Q$
Figure 1. Meridian half-plane of $P$, $\mathbf i_1$ out of the paper. The triangle of $DP$ (hypotenuse 1, legs $\cos\phi$ along $\mathbf k_1$ and $\sin\phi$ along $\mathbf j_1$) gives $\mathbf r_0^P = 4\,\mathbf k_0 + (2 + \cos\phi)\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1$; $\phi$ is clockwise here, a rotation by $-\phi$ about $\mathbf i_1$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotation by $\theta$ about $\mathbf j_0$ (into the paper, so the rotation is clockwise as seen). The triangle of $\mathbf i_1$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf k_0$, which gives $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0$..
Toroidal basis: rotation about j0 Law: elementary rotation about the second axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19

View of the vertical plane $Ox_0z_0$ with $\mathbf i_0$ to the right and $\mathbf k_0$ up: $\mathbf j_0$, the cross product of $\mathbf k_0$ and $\mathbf i_0$, points into the paper (⊗), so a positive rotation about $\mathbf j_0$ is clockwise as seen. The angle $\theta$ goes from $\mathbf k_0$ (up) towards $\mathbf i_0$ (right): clockwise, a positive rotation. $\mathbf k_1$ is $\mathbf k_0$ turned by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf k_0$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along $\mathbf i_0$. $\mathbf i_1$ is $\mathbf i_0$ turned by $\theta$: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf i_0$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along minus $\mathbf k_0$, because the vector to the right turned clockwise points downwards (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i_1 &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf i_1$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf k_0$)} \\ \mathbf k_1 &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf k_1$)} \\ \mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_0 &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf j_0 = \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 = \mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right-handed; $\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf j_0 = -\mathbf i_0$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= \cos\theta\left(\cos\theta\right) - 0 + \sin\theta\left(0 + \sin\theta\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + 0 + \sin^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{13} &= -\cos\theta\sin\theta + \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf j_0$)} \\ \mathbf i_0 &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \mathbf k_0 = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(rows 1 and 3 of the matrix, Eq. 1.14)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & 0 & \sin\theta \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr -\sin\theta & 0 & \cos\theta \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf k_1$ points from the centre $C$ of the torus to the centre of the tube section through $P$; $\mathbf i_1$ is the direction in which $P$ moves when only $\theta$ changes.

Poloidal basis: rotation in the meridian half-plane Law: elementary rotation about the first axis, with a negative angle; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.18

View of the meridian half-plane with $\mathbf j_1$ to the right and $\mathbf k_1$ up: $\mathbf i_1$, the cross product of $\mathbf j_1$ and $\mathbf k_1$, points out of the paper (⊙), so positive angles about $\mathbf i_1$ are counterclockwise. The angle $\phi$ goes from $\mathbf k_1$ (up) towards $\mathbf j_1$ (right): clockwise, so the poloidal basis is Eq. 1.18 with the angle minus $\phi$. $\mathbf k_2$ is $\mathbf k_1$ turned clockwise by $\phi$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ along $\mathbf k_1$, opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along $\mathbf j_1$. $\mathbf j_2$ is $\mathbf j_1$ turned clockwise by $\phi$: adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ along $\mathbf j_1$, opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along minus $\mathbf k_1$, since the vector to the right turned clockwise points downwards (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf j_2 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf j_2$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf k_1$)} \\ \mathbf k_2 &= \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf k_2$)} \\ \mathbf i_2\times\mathbf j_2 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1\times\mathbf k_1 = \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 = \mathbf k_2 \quad &\text{(right-handed; $\mathbf i_1\times\mathbf k_1 = -\mathbf j_1$)} \\ \det[{}_1R_2] &= 1\cdot\left(\cos\phi\cos\phi - \sin\phi\left(-\sin\phi\right)\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{22} &= 0 + \cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi = 1 \quad &\text{(row 2 times row 2)} \\ \left([{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T\right)_{23} &= -\cos\phi\sin\phi + \sin\phi\cos\phi = 0 \quad &\text{(row 2 times row 3; row 1 is the unit row of $\mathbf i_1$)} \\ \mathbf j_1 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_2 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_2, \quad \mathbf k_1 = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf j_2 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_2 \quad &\text{(rows 2 and 3 of the matrix)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_1R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \cr 0 & \cos\phi & \sin\phi \cr 0 & -\sin\phi & \cos\phi \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_1R_2] = 1, \quad [{}_1R_2][{}_1R_2]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf k_2$ is the outward normal of the tube at $P$ and $\mathbf j_2$ its tangent along the small circle of the section.

Position vectors of both particles Law: position vector as a sum of partial vectors; constraints substituted Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4; Problems.pdf, p. 26

$\overrightarrow{OC}$ is $4\mathbf k_0$; the centre $D$ of the tube section of $P$ is at the distance 2 from $C$ along $\mathbf k_1$; $P$ is at the distance 1 from $D$ along $\mathbf k_2$. In the right triangle of $DP$ (meridian figure) the hypotenuse is 1, the leg along $\mathbf k_1$ is $\cos\phi$ (adjacent to $\phi$) and the leg along $\mathbf j_1$ is $\sin\phi$ (opposite).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OC} + \overrightarrow{CD} + \overrightarrow{DP} = 4\,\mathbf k_0 + 2\,\mathbf k_1 + \mathbf k_2 \quad &\text{(sum of partial vectors)} \\ &= 4\,\mathbf k_0 + \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_2$ in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ &= \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_0 + \left[4 + \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\cos\theta\right]\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$; the equations of the statement)} \\ \mathbf r_0^Q &= \xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \eta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(on the plane $z_Q = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = 4\,\mathbf k_0 + \varrho\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1, \quad \varrho = 2 + \cos\phi; \qquad \mathbf r_0^Q = \xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \eta\,\mathbf j_0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $\varrho$ is the distance from $P$ to the axis of the torus; the third line reproduces the parametric equations of the statement, which confirms the reading of $\theta$ and $\phi$.

Coordinates at the initial instant Law: the initial position and velocity written with the coordinates Source: Problems.pdf, p. 26
$$\begin{aligned} \sin\phi_0 &= 1 \Rightarrow \phi_0 = \tfrac{\pi}{2}, \quad \varrho_0 = 2 + 0 = 2 \quad &\text{($y_P = 1$)} \\ 2\sin\theta_0 &= 2, \quad 4 + 2\cos\theta_0 = 4 \Rightarrow \theta_0 = \tfrac{\pi}{2} \quad &\text{($x_P = 2$, $z_P = 4$)} \\ \mathbf i_1 &= -\mathbf k_0, \quad \mathbf j_2 = -\mathbf k_1 = -\mathbf i_0, \quad \mathbf k_2 = \mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(bases at $\theta = \phi = \pi/2$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 = -2\dot\theta\,\mathbf k_0 - \dot\phi\,\mathbf i_0 = -\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(velocity of the kinematics box below at $t_0$)} \\ \xi_0 &= 3, \quad \eta_0 = 1, \quad \dot\xi_0 = -1, \quad \dot\eta_0 = 1 \quad &\text{(position and velocity of $Q$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta_0 = \phi_0 = \tfrac{\pi}{2}, \quad \dot\theta_0 = \tfrac12, \quad \dot\phi_0 = 0; \qquad \xi_0 = 3, \quad \eta_0 = 1, \quad \dot\xi_0 = -1, \quad \dot\eta_0 = 1}$$

Units: angles in rad, rates in $[\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$; $[\xi] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $P$ starts at the meridian angle $\theta = \pi/2$, where its tube section is the horizontal circle centred at $(2, 0, 4)$; the angle $\phi = \pi/2$ is the point of that circle farthest along $+\mathbf j_0$. The normal there is $\mathbf j_0$, perpendicular to the initial velocity $-\mathbf k_0$ (as it must be), and $P$ starts moving straight down along the toroidal direction $\mathbf i_1$, which is $-\mathbf k_0$ at that instant.

Kinematics

Derivatives of the toroidal basis Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ turns about the fixed $\mathbf j_0$ with $\theta$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf k}_1 &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf k_1 = \dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf k_1 = \mathbf i_1$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf i}_1 &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf i_1 = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf i_1 = -\mathbf k_1$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf j}_1 &= \mathbf 0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_1 = \mathbf j_0$ is fixed)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf i}_1 = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf k_1, \qquad \dot{\mathbf j}_1 = \mathbf 0, \qquad \dot{\mathbf k}_1 = \dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1}$$

Units: $[\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the meridian half-plane of $P$ turns about the axis of the torus.

(b) Position, velocity and acceleration of P Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product and chain rules; projection on the poloidal basis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \dot\varrho &= -\sin\phi\,\dot\phi \quad &\text{(chain rule on $2 + \cos\phi$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \dot\varrho\,\mathbf k_1 + \varrho\,\dot{\mathbf k}_1 + \cos\phi\,\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; $4\,\mathbf k_0$ and $\mathbf j_1$ fixed)} \\ &= \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 - \sin\phi\,\dot\phi\,\mathbf k_1 + \cos\phi\,\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(derivatives of $\varrho$ and $\mathbf k_1$ substituted)} \\ &= \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \dot\phi\left(\cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1\right) \quad &\text{(common factor $\dot\phi$)} \\ &= \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_2 + \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 \quad &\text{($\mathbf i_2 = \mathbf i_1$; the bracket is $\mathbf j_2$ of the poloidal basis)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \left(\dot\varrho\dot\theta + \varrho\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf i_1 + \varrho\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf i}_1 + \left(\cos\phi\,\ddot\phi - \sin\phi\,\dot\phi^2\right)\mathbf j_1 - \left(\sin\phi\,\ddot\phi + \cos\phi\,\dot\phi^2\right)\mathbf k_1 - \sin\phi\,\dot\phi\,\dot{\mathbf k}_1 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules on the velocity in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ &= \left(\varrho\ddot\theta - 2\sin\phi\,\dot\phi\dot\theta\right)\mathbf i_1 + \left(\cos\phi\,\ddot\phi - \sin\phi\,\dot\phi^2\right)\mathbf j_1 - \left(\varrho\dot\theta^2 + \sin\phi\,\ddot\phi + \cos\phi\,\dot\phi^2\right)\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(derivatives of $\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf k_1$; grouped)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf j_2 &= \cos^2\phi\,\ddot\phi - \cos\phi\sin\phi\,\dot\phi^2 + \sin\phi\,\varrho\dot\theta^2 + \sin^2\phi\,\ddot\phi + \sin\phi\cos\phi\,\dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{(dot product with $\cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1$, expanded)} \\ &= \left(\cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi\right)\ddot\phi + \varrho\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(the terms in $\dot\phi^2$ cancel; terms in $\ddot\phi$ collected)} \\ &= \ddot\phi + \varrho\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{($\cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi = 1$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf k_2 &= \sin\phi\cos\phi\,\ddot\phi - \sin^2\phi\,\dot\phi^2 - \cos\phi\,\varrho\dot\theta^2 - \cos\phi\sin\phi\,\ddot\phi - \cos^2\phi\,\dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{(dot product with $\sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1$, expanded)} \\ &= -\left(\sin^2\phi + \cos^2\phi\right)\dot\phi^2 - \varrho\cos\phi\,\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(the terms in $\ddot\phi$ cancel; terms in $\dot\phi^2$ collected)} \\ &= -\dot\phi^2 - \varrho\cos\phi\,\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{($\sin^2\phi + \cos^2\phi = 1$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^P = 4\,\mathbf k_0 + \varrho\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1, \quad \mathbf v_0^P = \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_2 + \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 \cr \mathbf a_0^P = \left(\varrho\ddot\theta - 2\sin\phi\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right)\mathbf i_2 + \left(\ddot\phi + \varrho\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf j_2 - \left(\dot\phi^2 + \varrho\cos\phi\,\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf k_2, \quad \varrho = 2 + \cos\phi \end{array}}$$

Units: lengths in m (the tube radius 1 m multiplies $\dot\phi$): $[\varrho\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the velocity lies in the tangent plane of the torus (no $\mathbf k_2$ component) while the acceleration has a normal part, the centripetal acceleration of the two circular motions, which the reaction and the forces must supply.

(b) Position, velocity and acceleration of Q Law: time derivatives in $S_0$ of Cartesian components Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^Q &= \dot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \dot\eta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(fixed unit vectors)} \\ \mathbf a_0^Q &= \ddot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \ddot\eta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(derivative of the velocity)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^Q = \xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \eta\,\mathbf j_0, \quad \mathbf v_0^Q = \dot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \dot\eta\,\mathbf j_0, \quad \mathbf a_0^Q = \ddot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \ddot\eta\,\mathbf j_0}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $Q$ moves freely in the horizontal plane.

(c) Tangent and normal vectors of the torus Law: tangent vectors as partial derivatives of the parametric form; normal as their cross product Source: [Lecture 3, Slide 9]; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf t_\theta &= \frac{\partial\mathbf r_0^P}{\partial\theta} = \varrho\,\frac{\partial\mathbf k_1}{\partial\theta} = \varrho\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{($\partial\mathbf k_1/\partial\theta = \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0 = \mathbf i_1$)} \\ \mathbf t_\phi &= \frac{\partial\mathbf r_0^P}{\partial\phi} = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 = \mathbf j_2 \quad &\text{(derivative of $\varrho\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1$)} \\ \mathbf t_\theta\times\mathbf t_\phi &= \varrho\,\mathbf i_2\times\mathbf j_2 = \varrho\,\mathbf k_2 \quad &\text{(right-handed $\mathcal B_2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf t_\theta = \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\mathbf i_1, \quad \mathbf t_\phi = \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1, \quad \mathbf n = \mathbf k_2 = \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1}$$

Units: $[\mathbf t_\theta] = [\mathbf t_\phi] = [\mathrm{L}]$ per radian; $\mathbf n$ dimensionless

Meaning: the two tangent vectors are orthogonal and $\varrho$ never vanishes (its minimum is 1), so the parametrisation is regular everywhere; in $\mathcal B_0$, $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0$ and $\mathbf k_1 = \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagrams show on $P$ its weight, the attraction towards $Q$ and the normal reaction of the torus; on $Q$ its weight, the attraction towards $P$ and the normal reaction of the plane. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ and $Q$ at the initial instant. Each reaction is along the single normal of its smooth surface ($\mathbf k_2$, here $\mathbf j_0$, for the torus; $\mathbf k_0$ for the plane), so projections on the tangent directions remove them..
(d) Forces on both particles Law: weight; attraction proportional to the distance (zero-length spring); normal reactions of smooth surfaces Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; Problems.pdf, p. 75
$$\begin{aligned} \overrightarrow{PQ} &= \mathbf r_0^Q - \mathbf r_0^P = \left(\xi - \varrho\sin\theta\right)\mathbf i_0 + \left(\eta - \sin\phi\right)\mathbf j_0 - \left(4 + \varrho\cos\theta\right)\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(difference of the position vectors)} \\ \mathbf F_P &= k\overrightarrow{PQ} = 2\left[\xi - \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\sin\theta\right]\mathbf i_0 + 2\left(\eta - \sin\phi\right)\mathbf j_0 - 2\left[4 + \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\cos\theta\right]\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(attraction on $P$, $k = 2$)} \\ \mathbf F_Q &= -k\overrightarrow{PQ} \quad &\text{(action and reaction)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} P:\ -m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0, \quad \mathbf F_P = k\overrightarrow{PQ}, \quad N\,\mathbf k_2 \cr Q:\ -m_Qg\,\mathbf k_0, \quad -k\overrightarrow{PQ}, \quad N_Q\,\mathbf k_0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[k\,|\overrightarrow{PQ}|] = [\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L}] = [mg] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two unknown reactions, $N$ and $N_Q$, each along the single normal direction of its smooth surface.

Projections of the forces on the tangent plane of the torus Law: dot products with the poloidal basis, using the rows of the change of basis matrices Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.16

From the two matrices: $\mathbf i_0$ has the components $\cos\theta$, $-\sin\theta\sin\phi$, $\sin\theta\cos\phi$ on $\mathbf i_2$, $\mathbf j_2$, $\mathbf k_2$; $\mathbf j_0$ has $0$, $\cos\phi$, $\sin\phi$; $\mathbf k_0$ has $-\sin\theta$, $-\cos\theta\sin\phi$, $\cos\theta\cos\phi$; $\mathbf k_1$ has $0$, $-\sin\phi$, $\cos\phi$.

$$\begin{aligned} \overrightarrow{PQ} &= \xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \eta\,\mathbf j_0 - 4\,\mathbf k_0 - \varrho\,\mathbf k_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{(position vectors in mixed bases)} \\ \overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf i_2 &= \xi\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta \quad &\text{(components on $\mathbf i_2$; $\mathbf k_1$, $\mathbf j_1$ have none)} \\ \overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf j_2 &= -\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + \varrho\sin\phi - \sin\phi\cos\phi \quad &\text{(components on $\mathbf j_2$)} \\ &= -\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\sin\phi \quad &\text{($\varrho\sin\phi - \sin\phi\cos\phi = 2\sin\phi$)} \\ -m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf i_2 &= m_Pg\sin\theta, \quad -m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf j_2 = m_Pg\cos\theta\sin\phi \quad &\text{(weight)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \left(\mathbf F_P - m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0\right)\cdot\mathbf i_2 = m_Pg\sin\theta + k\left(\xi\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta\right) \cr \left(\mathbf F_P - m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0\right)\cdot\mathbf j_2 = m_Pg\cos\theta\sin\phi + k\left(-\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\sin\phi\right) \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the reaction $N\,\mathbf k_2$ has no component on $\mathbf i_2$ or $\mathbf j_2$, so these two projections carry the applied forces only.

(e) Equations of motion free of reactions Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame; projections on the tangent plane of the torus and on the plane of $Q$, free of the reactions Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} P,\ \mathbf i_2:\ m_P\left(\varrho\ddot\theta - 2\sin\phi\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right) &= m_Pg\sin\theta + k\left(\xi\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(toroidal direction)} \\ P,\ \mathbf j_2:\ m_P\left(\ddot\phi + \varrho\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2\right) &= m_Pg\cos\theta\sin\phi + k\left(-\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\sin\phi\right) \quad &\text{(poloidal direction)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf i_0:\ m_Q\ddot\xi &= -k\left(\xi - \varrho\sin\theta\right) \quad &\text{(horizontal: no reaction)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf j_0:\ m_Q\ddot\eta &= -k\left(\eta - \sin\phi\right) \quad &\text{(horizontal: no reaction)} \\ m_P = 1,\ m_Q = 2,\ k = 2 &\Rightarrow \text{the system below} \quad &\text{(data substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\ddot\theta = 2\sin\phi\,\dot\theta\dot\phi + g\sin\theta + 2\left(\xi\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta\right) \cr \ddot\phi = -\left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2 + g\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\left(-\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\sin\phi\right) \cr \ddot\xi = -\xi + \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\sin\theta, \qquad \ddot\eta = -\eta + \sin\phi \end{array}}$$

Units: with the data in SI, $[\ddot\phi] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$ and every term of its equation (divided by the tube radius 1 m) is $[\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[\ddot\xi] = [\xi]\,[k/m_Q] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: four second-order equations for the four coordinates, free of $N$ and $N_Q$; the reactions follow afterwards from the projections on $\mathbf k_2$ (for $P$) and on $\mathbf k_0$ (for $Q$, which gives $N_Q = m_Qg - k\,z_P$).

Answers and Checks

(f) A scalar conservation law

Mechanical energy of the pair Law: theorem of kinetic energy for each particle; stationary smooth constraints do no work; internal conservative attraction Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; Notes.pdf, Eq. 6.12; [Lecture 3, Slide 16]
$$\begin{aligned} N\,\mathbf k_2\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= N\,\mathbf k_2\cdot\left(\varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_2 + \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2\right) = 0, \quad N_Q\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q = 0 \quad &\text{(reactions normal to the velocities)} \\ k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf v_0^P - k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q &= -k\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\frac{d\overrightarrow{PQ}}{dt} = -\frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2\right) \quad &\text{(power of the attraction on both; chain rule)} \\ -m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf v_0^P - m_Qg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left(m_Pg\,z_P\right) \quad &\text{($z_Q = 0$ always)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12 m_P|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + \tfrac12 m_Q|\mathbf v_0^Q|^2\right] &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left[m_Pg\,z_P + \tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2\right] \quad &\text{(sum of all powers)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= \varrho^2\dot\theta^2 + \dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{(orthonormal $\mathcal B_2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E = \tfrac12\left[\left(2 + \cos\phi\right)^2\dot\theta^2 + \dot\phi^2\right] + \left(\dot\xi^2 + \dot\eta^2\right) + g\left[4 + \left(2 + \cos\phi\right)\cos\theta\right] + \left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2 = \text{const}}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$ with the masses and $k$ in SI ($\frac12 m_Q = 1$ kg, $\frac12 k = 1$ N m$^{-1}$)

Meaning: the torus and the plane are fixed and smooth, so their reactions do no work; the weight of $Q$ does no work because $Q$ stays at zero height; the attraction is internal and conservative. The total mechanical energy is the scalar conservation law.

Value of the energy at the initial instant Law: the conservation law evaluated with the initial conditions Source: Problems.pdf, p. 26
$$\begin{aligned} \tfrac12 m_P|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= \tfrac12(1)(1)^2 = 0.5 \quad &\text{($\mathbf v_0^P = -\mathbf k_0$)} \\ \tfrac12 m_Q|\mathbf v_0^Q|^2 &= \tfrac12(2)\left[(-1)^2 + 1^2\right] = 2 \quad &\text{($\mathbf v_0^Q = -\mathbf i_0 + \mathbf j_0$)} \\ m_Pg\,z_P &= (1)\,g\,(4) = 4g \quad &\text{($z_P = 4$)} \\ \left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2 &= (3 - 2)^2 + (1 - 1)^2 + (0 - 4)^2 = 17 \quad &\text{(initial positions)} \\ \tfrac12 k\left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2 &= \tfrac12(2)(17) = 17 \quad &\text{(attraction potential)} \\ E_0 &= 0.5 + 2 + 4g + 17 = 19.5 + 4g \quad &\text{(sum)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E_0 = 19.5 + 4g\ \text{J} = 58.74\ \text{J} \ (g = 9.81\ \text{m s}^{-2})}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$, joules

Meaning: most of the initial energy is the potential of the attraction (17 J) and of the height of $P$ (39.24 J).

(g) Can P reach the highest point of the torus?

Energy needed at the top of the torus Law: the kinetic energies are never negative, so at any reachable state the potential energy cannot exceed the constant energy Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31

The highest point of the torus is where $z_P$ is largest: $\cos\theta = 1$ and $\cos\phi = 1$, that is $\theta = \phi = 0$, with $z_P = 4 + 3 = 7$. There $P$ is 7 above the plane of $Q$, so the distance $|PQ|$ is at least 7.

$$\begin{aligned} z_{P,\max} &= 4 + \left(2 + 1\right)\left(1\right) = 7 \quad &\text{($\theta = \phi = 0$)} \\ \left|\overrightarrow{PQ}\right|^2 &= \left(\xi - 0\right)^2 + \left(\eta - 0\right)^2 + \left(0 - 7\right)^2 \ge 49 \quad &\text{(at the top; $\xi$, $\eta$ free)} \\ E &\ge m_Pg\,(7) + \tfrac12 k\,(49) = 7g + 49 \quad &\text{(kinetic energies dropped)} \\ E_0 - \left(7g + 49\right) &= 19.5 + 4g - 7g - 49 = -29.5 - 3g \lt 0 \quad &\text{(for every $g \ge 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E_0 = 58.74\ \text{J} \lt 7g + 49 = 117.67\ \text{J}: \ P \ \text{can never reach the top of the torus}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$, joules

Meaning: the energy falls short by 58.9 J; even without the attraction term (which only strengthens the conclusion) the gravity term alone would need $7g$ = 68.67 J against 58.74 J available. The answer does not depend on the value of $g$.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Coordinates$\theta$ (meridian angle about the axis of the torus, the horizontal line through $(0, 0, 4)$ parallel to $\mathbf j_0$), $\phi$ (angle round the tube), $\xi$, $\eta$ (Cartesian coordinates of $Q$): 4 degrees of freedom
(b) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^P = 4\,\mathbf k_0 + \varrho\,\mathbf k_1 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1$, $\mathbf v_0^P = \varrho\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_2 + \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2$, $\mathbf a_0^P = (\varrho\ddot\theta - 2\sin\phi\,\dot\theta\dot\phi)\,\mathbf i_2 + (\ddot\phi + \varrho\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf j_2 - (\dot\phi^2 + \varrho\cos\phi\,\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf k_2$, $\varrho = 2 + \cos\phi$; $\mathbf r_0^Q = \xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \eta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf v_0^Q = \dot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \dot\eta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf a_0^Q = \ddot\xi\,\mathbf i_0 + \ddot\eta\,\mathbf j_0$
(c) Tangent and normal$\mathbf t_\theta = (2 + \cos\phi)\,\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf t_\phi = \cos\phi\,\mathbf j_1 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_1$, $\mathbf n = \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1$, with $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf k_1 = \sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0$
(d) Forces$P$: $-m_Pg\,\mathbf k_0$, $k\overrightarrow{PQ} = 2[\xi - (2 + \cos\phi)\sin\theta]\,\mathbf i_0 + 2(\eta - \sin\phi)\,\mathbf j_0 - 2[4 + (2 + \cos\phi)\cos\theta]\,\mathbf k_0$, $N\,\mathbf n$; $Q$: $-m_Qg\,\mathbf k_0$, $-k\overrightarrow{PQ}$, $N_Q\,\mathbf k_0$
(e) Equations of motion$(2 + \cos\phi)\ddot\theta = 2\sin\phi\,\dot\theta\dot\phi + g\sin\theta + 2(\xi\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta)$; $\ddot\phi = -(2 + \cos\phi)\sin\phi\,\dot\theta^2 + g\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2(-\xi\sin\theta\sin\phi + \eta\cos\phi + 4\cos\theta\sin\phi + 2\sin\phi)$; $\ddot\xi = -\xi + (2 + \cos\phi)\sin\theta$; $\ddot\eta = -\eta + \sin\phi$
(f) Conservation law$E = \frac12[(2 + \cos\phi)^2\dot\theta^2 + \dot\phi^2] + \dot\xi^2 + \dot\eta^2 + g\,z_P + |\overrightarrow{PQ}|^2$, $E_0 = 19.5 + 4g$
(g) Top of the torusno: it needs at least $7g + 49$, more than $E_0$ for any $g$
