Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 75, item 38, gives the force on $P$ due to $Q$ in the same coordinates (the key's letters $\xi$, $\eta$ for $Q$): identical, component by component, to the box of part (d) (match). The other parts have no key; the independent checks are the reproduction of the parametric equations of the statement by the position vector, the orthogonality of the initial velocity to the normal $\mathbf j_0$ in the box of the initial coordinates, and the limiting case below.

Limiting case: P alone at the top of its tube section Law: the equations of motion with the attraction removed, at a point of symmetry Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.32; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4

Without the attraction ($k$ set to zero) $P$ is a heavy particle on a fixed smooth torus. The highest point, $\theta = \phi = 0$, must then be an equilibrium, since every tangent direction there is horizontal.

$$\begin{aligned} \left(2 + 1\right)\ddot\theta &= 0 + g\sin 0 + 0 = 0 \quad &\text{(toroidal equation at rest, $k = 0$)} \\ \ddot\phi &= 0 + g\cos 0\sin 0 + 0 = 0 \quad &\text{(poloidal equation at rest, $k = 0$)} \\ m_P\,\mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf k_2 &= 0 = -m_Pg\cos 0\cos 0 + N \quad &\text{(normal projection at rest: $\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf k_2 = \cos\theta\cos\phi$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{k = 0,\ \theta = \phi = 0,\ \text{at rest}:\ \ddot\theta = \ddot\phi = 0, \quad N = m_Pg}$$

Units: $[N] = [m_Pg] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the equations recognise the top as an equilibrium where the torus carries the whole weight, as it must; this confirms the signs of the gravity terms ($g\sin\theta$ and $g\cos\theta\sin\phi$ push $P$ away from the top).

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 39

Particle on a String with a Plate

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 27
Plate Edge Contact

A heavy point particle $P$ with mass $m$ is connected with point $O$ on the upper surface of a thin square plate by a massless, inextensible string of length $\ell$. The distance between $O$ and the edge of the plate is $d$. The string hangs over the edge of the plate and can slide without friction. You can assume that the string remains tense and in contact with the plate at all times. We denote the geometric point where the string is in contact with the plate edge as $Q$. We define the inertial reference frame $S_0 : \{O, \mathcal{B}_0\}$, with $\mathcal{B}_0 = \{\mathbf{i}_0, \mathbf{j}_0, \mathbf{k}_0\}$ as shown in the figure. We define the angle $\theta$ between the $Ox_0$ axis and the string segment $OQ$, and the angle $\phi$ between a vertical plane containing the plate edge and the line perpendicular to the plate edge that passes through $P$. Initially at time $t = 0$, $\theta(0) = \phi(0) = \pi/3\text{ rad}$, and the modulus of the velocity of $P$ is $v_0^P(0) = \sqrt{\ell g}$, where $g$ is the magnitude of the acceleration due to gravity.

(Hint: Note that under the conditions of the problem, the two angles marked with dashed lines are equal).

  • (a)
    How many degrees of freedom does the system have?
  • (b)
    Obtain the general expression of a unit vector in the direction from $Q$ to $P$ as a function of $\theta$ and $\phi$.
  • (c)
    Obtain the general expression for the position, velocity, and acceleration vectors of $Q$ and $P$ in $S_0$ as a function of $\theta, \phi$, their derivatives, and the parameters of the problem.
  • (d)
    Identify and express mathematically all the forces acting on $P$.
  • (e)
    Find a set of second order differential equations to determine the evolution of $\theta$ and $\phi$ in time, free of any other unknowns.
  • (f)
    Does the tension force exert work on $P$? Provide proof of your answer.
  • (g)
    Derive the general expression of the mechanical energy of $P$ in $S_0$ and compute its value initially. Is this a conserved quantity of motion? Justify your answer.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

A heavy particle $P$ of mass $m$ hangs from a massless inextensible string of length $\ell$ tied at the point $O$ of the upper surface of a thin horizontal plate; the string runs on the plate to the point $Q$ of its straight edge, at the distance $d$ from $O$, and hangs over that edge, sliding on it without friction (Problems.pdf, p. 27). The statement asks for the degrees of freedom, the unit vector from $Q$ to $P$, the kinematics of $Q$ and $P$, the forces on $P$, the equations of motion, the work of the tension and the mechanical energy. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i_0, \mathbf j_0, \mathbf k_0\}$, $\mathbf k_0$ vertical upward, the plate in the plane $z_0 = 0$
Edge of the plateread from the figure: the straight line $x_0 = d$, $z_0 = 0$, parallel to $\mathbf j_0$ ($Ox_0$ is the perpendicular from $O$ to the edge); the plate occupies $x_0 \lt d$
Basis of the hanging plane$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j_0, \mathbf k_1\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\phi$ about $-\mathbf j_0$: minus $\mathbf k_1$ is the direction of the line from the edge to $P$ perpendicular to the edge
Coordinates$\theta$: angle from $Ox_0$ to the segment $OQ$, in the plane of the plate; $\phi$: angle from the downward vertical to the line through $P$ perpendicular to the edge, positive away from the plate (towards $\mathbf i_0$), read from the figure
Hypothesesthe string is tense, lies straight on the plate from $O$ to $Q$ and straight in the air from $Q$ to $P$; the edge is smooth, so the tension has the same magnitude $T$ on both sides of $Q$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 17]); the hanging length stays positive, $\ell\cos\theta \gt d$ (at $t = 0$ this needs $\ell \gt 2d$)
Constraintsinextensible string: $|OQ| + |QP| = \ell$ with $Q$ on the edge (1 equation, holonomic, stationary, unilateral) ([Lecture 3, Slide 21]); the position of $Q$ on the edge is fixed by the balance of the string at the smooth edge (box of part (b))
Degrees of freedom (a)3 coordinates of $P$ minus 1 constraint equation: 2, namely $\theta(t)$ and $\phi(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4]); $Q$ is a geometric point, not a particle, and adds no unknown
Data$m$ [kg], $\ell$ [m], $d$ [m], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]; at $t = 0$: $\theta = \phi = \pi/3$ and speed of $P$ equal to $\sqrt{\ell g}$
Applied forceweight $-mg\,\mathbf k_0$
Constraint forcetension $T \ge 0$ on $P$, from $P$ towards $Q$ (Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5); the edge acts on the string only
Figure 1. The string runs from $O$ to $Q$ on the plate (legs $d$ and $d\tan\theta$, inset) and hangs from $Q$ to $P$. The smooth edge makes the two segments meet it at equal angles, so $\mathbf e_{QP}\cdot\mathbf j_0 = \sin\theta$, and $P$ lies at the distance $u = \ell\cos\theta - d$ from the edge, swung out by $\phi$..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. View along the edge, $\mathbf j_0$ into the paper. $\phi$ is counterclockwise here, a rotation by $-\phi$ about $\mathbf j_0$; the triangle of $\mathbf k_1$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf i_0$, which gives $\mathbf k_1 = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0$..
Basis of the hanging plane: rotation about minus j0 Law: elementary rotation about the second axis, with a negative angle; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.19

View of the vertical plane through $P$ perpendicular to the edge, with $\mathbf i_0$ to the right (away from the plate) and $\mathbf k_0$ up: $\mathbf j_0$, the cross product of $\mathbf k_0$ and $\mathbf i_0$, points into the paper (⊗), and a positive rotation about $\mathbf j_0$ is clockwise as seen. The angle $\phi$ goes from the downward vertical towards $\mathbf i_0$: counterclockwise, so the basis is Eq. 1.19 with the angle minus $\phi$ (a positive rotation about minus $\mathbf j_0$, which points out of the paper). $\mathbf i_1$ is $\mathbf i_0$ turned counterclockwise by $\phi$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ along $\mathbf i_0$, opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along $\mathbf k_0$. $\mathbf k_1$ is $\mathbf k_0$ turned counterclockwise by $\phi$: adjacent leg $\cos\phi$ along $\mathbf k_0$, opposite leg $\sin\phi$ along minus $\mathbf i_0$, because the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left (the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i_1 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf i_1$)} \\ \mathbf k_1 &= -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf k_1$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf i_0$)} \\ \mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_0 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf j_0 = \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 - \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 = \mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(right-handed; $\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf j_0 = -\mathbf i_0$)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= \cos\phi\left(\cos\phi\right) - 0 + \left(-\sin\phi\right)\left(0 - \sin\phi\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the first row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\phi + 0 + \sin^2\phi = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{13} &= \cos\phi\sin\phi - \sin\phi\cos\phi = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 3; row 2 is the unit row of $\mathbf j_0$)} \\ \mathbf k_0 &= \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(third row of the matrix, Eq. 1.14)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\phi & 0 & -\sin\phi \cr 0 & 1 & 0 \cr \sin\phi & 0 & \cos\phi \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: minus $\mathbf k_1$, equal to $\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 - \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0$, points from the edge down to $P$ at the angle $\phi$ from the vertical; $\mathbf i_1$ is perpendicular to it in the same vertical plane.

Position of Q and the two lengths of the string Law: right triangle in the plane of the plate; inextensible string Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]; Problems.pdf, p. 27

In the plane of the plate the triangle $O$, foot of the perpendicular on the edge, $Q$ is right-angled at the foot: the hypotenuse is $|OQ|$, the leg adjacent to $\theta$ is $d$ (along $\mathbf i_0$) and the opposite leg is $d\tan\theta$ (along the edge, $\mathbf j_0$).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^Q &= d\,\mathbf i_0 + d\tan\theta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(the two legs of the triangle)} \\ |OQ| &= \frac{d}{\cos\theta} \quad &\text{(adjacent leg over the cosine)} \\ \rho &= |QP| = \ell - \frac{d}{\cos\theta} \quad &\text{(constant total length)} \\ \rho\cos\theta &= \ell\cos\theta - d \equiv u \quad &\text{(definition of $u$, used below)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^Q = d\,\mathbf i_0 + d\tan\theta\,\mathbf j_0, \qquad \rho = \ell - \frac{d}{\cos\theta}, \qquad u = \ell\cos\theta - d \gt 0}$$

Units: $[d] = [\rho] = [u] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: turning $OQ$ away from $Ox_0$ uses more string on the plate and shortens the hanging part.

(b) Unit vector from Q to P Law: balance of the massless string at the smooth edge (no force along the edge); unit norm Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]; Problems.pdf, p. 27

The edge is a smooth straight curve, and the reaction of a smooth curve lies in its normal subspace (Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]), so the force of the edge on the string at $Q$ is perpendicular to the edge (to $\mathbf j_0$); the massless string at $Q$ is pulled by $T$ towards $O$ and by $T$ towards $P$. The balance along the edge gives the hint of the statement (equal angles of both segments with the edge). The rest of the unit vector lies in the vertical plane perpendicular to the edge, along minus $\mathbf k_1$.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_{QO} &= -\cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 - \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(from $Q$ back to $O$: minus the direction of $OQ$)} \\ T\left(\mathbf e_{QO} + \mathbf e_{QP}\right)\cdot\mathbf j_0 &= 0 \quad &\text{(no force along the edge on the massless string at $Q$)} \\ \mathbf e_{QP}\cdot\mathbf j_0 &= -\mathbf e_{QO}\cdot\mathbf j_0 = \sin\theta \quad &\text{(divided by $T \gt 0$; $\mathbf e_{QO}\cdot\mathbf j_0 = -\sin\theta$)} \\ \mathbf e_{QP} &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + c\left(-\mathbf k_1\right) \quad &\text{(the rest lies along the line from the edge to $P$)} \\ c^2 + \sin^2\theta &= 1 \Rightarrow c = \cos\theta \quad &\text{(unit norm; $c \gt 0$ since $P$ hangs below the edge)} \\ \mathbf e_{QP} &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\left(-\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0\right) \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf e_{QP} = \cos\theta\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 = \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1}$$

Units: dimensionless; $|\mathbf e_{QP}|^2 = \cos^2\theta\left(\sin^2\phi + \cos^2\phi\right) + \sin^2\theta = 1$

Meaning: the hanging segment continues the direction of $OQ$ along the edge: its component along $\mathbf j_0$ equals that of $OQ$, as if the plate were unfolded about the edge.

Position vector of P Law: position vector as a sum of partial vectors Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OQ} + \overrightarrow{QP} = d\,\mathbf i_0 + d\tan\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \rho\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) \quad &\text{(sum of partial vectors)} \\ d\tan\theta + \rho\sin\theta &= d\tan\theta + \ell\sin\theta - \frac{d\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = \ell\sin\theta \quad &\text{(component along $\mathbf j_0$)} \\ \rho\cos\theta &= u \quad &\text{(component along $-\mathbf k_1$)} \\ \mathbf r_0^P &= d\,\mathbf i_0 + \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - u\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(both components substituted)} \\ &= \left(d + u\sin\phi\right)\mathbf i_0 + \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - u\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = d\,\mathbf i_0 + \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\mathbf k_1, \qquad z_P = -\left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\cos\phi}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $P$ sits on the vertical plane perpendicular to the edge at the abscissa $\ell\sin\theta$ along the edge, at the distance $u$ from the edge, swung out by $\phi$; in the right triangle of that distance the vertical leg is $u\cos\phi$ (adjacent to $\phi$) and the horizontal leg $u\sin\phi$ (opposite).

Kinematics

Derivatives of the basis of the hanging plane Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= -\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(rotation by $\phi$ about $-\mathbf j_0$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf i}_1 &= -\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_0\times\left(\cos\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_0\right) = -\dot\phi\left(-\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0\right) = \dot\phi\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_0\times\mathbf i_0 = -\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf j_0\times\mathbf k_0 = \mathbf i_0$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf k}_1 &= -\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_0\times\left(-\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0\right) = -\dot\phi\left(\sin\phi\,\mathbf k_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf i_0\right) = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(same products)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf i}_1 = \dot\phi\,\mathbf k_1, \qquad \dot{\mathbf k}_1 = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1, \qquad \dot{\mathbf j}_0 = \mathbf 0}$$

Units: $[\dot\phi] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: the hanging plane does not turn; only the direction of the swing inside it changes.

(c) Velocity and acceleration of Q Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; chain and quotient rules Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\tan\theta &= \frac{\dot\theta}{\cos^2\theta} \quad &\text{(chain rule; derivative of the tangent)} \\ \mathbf v_0^Q &= \frac{d\dot\theta}{\cos^2\theta}\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{($d\,\mathbf i_0$ is constant)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\dot\theta}{\cos^2\theta}\right) &= \frac{\ddot\theta\cos^2\theta - \dot\theta\cdot 2\cos\theta\left(-\sin\theta\right)\dot\theta}{\cos^4\theta} = \frac{\ddot\theta\cos\theta + 2\dot\theta^2\sin\theta}{\cos^3\theta} \quad &\text{(quotient rule; chain rule on $\cos^2\theta$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^Q = d\,\mathbf i_0 + d\tan\theta\,\mathbf j_0, \quad \mathbf v_0^Q = \frac{d\dot\theta}{\cos^2\theta}\,\mathbf j_0, \quad \mathbf a_0^Q = \frac{d\left(\ddot\theta\cos\theta + 2\dot\theta^2\sin\theta\right)}{\cos^3\theta}\,\mathbf j_0}$$

Units: $[d\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$, $[d\ddot\theta] = [d\dot\theta^2] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the contact point slides along the edge only.

(c) Velocity and acceleration of P Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product and chain rules Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \dot u &= -\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta \quad &\text{(chain rule on $\ell\cos\theta - d$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \ell\cos\theta\,\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \dot u\,\mathbf k_1 - u\,\dot{\mathbf k}_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; $d\,\mathbf i_0$ constant)} \\ &= u\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \ell\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{($\dot u$ and the derivative of $\mathbf k_1$ substituted)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(u\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1\right) &= \left(u\ddot\phi - \ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right)\mathbf i_1 + u\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; derivative of $\mathbf i_1$)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\ell\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0\right) &= \ell\left(\ddot\theta\cos\theta - \dot\theta^2\sin\theta\right)\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\ell\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) &= \ell\left(\ddot\theta\sin\theta + \dot\theta^2\cos\theta\right)\mathbf k_1 - \ell\dot\theta\dot\phi\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; derivative of $\mathbf k_1$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \left(u\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right)\mathbf i_1 + \ell\left(\ddot\theta\cos\theta - \dot\theta^2\sin\theta\right)\mathbf j_0 + \left[\ell\left(\ddot\theta\sin\theta + \dot\theta^2\cos\theta\right) + u\dot\phi^2\right]\mathbf k_1 \quad &\text{(sum of the three derivatives)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf v_0^P = \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\dot\theta\left(\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) \cr \mathbf a_0^P = \left[\left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right]\mathbf i_1 + \ell\left(\ddot\theta\cos\theta - \dot\theta^2\sin\theta\right)\mathbf j_0 + \left[\ell\left(\ddot\theta\sin\theta + \dot\theta^2\cos\theta\right) + \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\dot\phi^2\right]\mathbf k_1 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[u\dot\phi] = [\ell\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the velocity has two orthogonal parts: the swing $u\dot\phi$ in the hanging plane and $\ell\dot\theta$ along $\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1$, which is perpendicular to the string; so the speed squared is $\ell^2\dot\theta^2 + u^2\dot\phi^2$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagram of $P$ shows its weight and the tension of the string, from $P$ towards $Q$. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts; the plate and its edge act only on the string.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$. The tension acts only along $\mathbf e_{QP}$, so the projections of Newton's law on $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf e_\perp$ give the two equations of motion free of $T$..
(d) Forces on P and a basis adapted to the string Law: weight; tension of a massless string along the string, pulling Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]

The unit vector $\mathbf e_\perp$, equal to $\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1$, completes with $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf e_{QP}$ an orthonormal triad: both $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf e_\perp$ are perpendicular to the string.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf T &= -T\,\mathbf e_{QP}, \quad T \ge 0 \quad &\text{(from $P$ towards $Q$; a string can only pull)} \\ \mathbf e_\perp\cdot\mathbf e_{QP} &= \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf e_\perp$ perpendicular to the string)} \\ \mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf e_{QP} &= \mathbf i_1\cdot\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) = 0 \quad &\text{($\mathbf i_1$ perpendicular to the string)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf i_1 &= -mg\sin\phi \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_0 = \sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1$)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf e_\perp &= -mg\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1\cdot\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) = -mg\cos\phi\sin\theta \quad &\text{(same expression of $\mathbf k_0$)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf e_{QP} &= -mg\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_1\cdot\left(-\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) = mg\cos\phi\cos\theta \quad &\text{(same expression of $\mathbf k_0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf F = -mg\,\mathbf k_0 - T\,\mathbf e_{QP} = -mg\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_1 - mg\sin\theta\cos\phi\,\mathbf e_\perp + \left(mg\cos\theta\cos\phi - T\right)\mathbf e_{QP}}$$

Units: $[mg] = [T] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the tension appears only along $\mathbf e_{QP}$; the projections on $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf e_\perp$ are free of it.

(e) Equations of motion for the two angles Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame, projected on the two directions perpendicular to the string Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf i_1 &= u\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi \quad &\text{(component along $\mathbf i_1$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf e_\perp &= \ell\cos^2\theta\,\ddot\theta - \ell\cos\theta\sin\theta\,\dot\theta^2 + \ell\sin^2\theta\,\ddot\theta + \ell\sin\theta\cos\theta\,\dot\theta^2 + u\sin\theta\,\dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{(components on $\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf k_1$ times $\cos\theta$, $\sin\theta$)} \\ &= \ell\ddot\theta + u\sin\theta\,\dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{($\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1$; the $\dot\theta^2$ terms cancel)} \\ \mathbf i_1:\ m\left(u\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi\right) &= -mg\sin\phi \quad &\text{(Newton, no tension)} \\ \mathbf e_\perp:\ m\left(\ell\ddot\theta + u\sin\theta\,\dot\phi^2\right) &= -mg\sin\theta\cos\phi \quad &\text{(Newton, no tension)} \\ \mathbf e_{QP}:\ -m\left(\ell\dot\theta^2 + u\cos\theta\,\dot\phi^2\right) &= mg\cos\theta\cos\phi - T \quad &\text{($\mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf e_{QP}$ by the same products)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \ell\ddot\theta + \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\sin\theta\,\dot\phi^2 + g\sin\theta\cos\phi = 0 \cr \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi + g\sin\phi = 0 \cr T = m\left[\ell\dot\theta^2 + \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\cos\theta\,\dot\phi^2\right] + mg\cos\theta\cos\phi \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\ell\ddot\theta] = [u\dot\phi^2] = [g] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[T] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: three equations for $\theta$, $\phi$ and $T$; the first two contain only the angles and give the motion, the third the tension, which must stay positive.

Answers and Checks

(f) Work of the tension

Power of the tension on P Law: power of a force, the dot product with the velocity Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; [Lecture 3, Slide 16]; Problems.pdf, p. 76
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_{QP}\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= \left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right)\cdot\left(u\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \ell\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1\right) \quad &\text{(the two vectors in $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ &= \ell\dot\theta\sin\theta\cos\theta - \ell\dot\theta\cos\theta\sin\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(orthonormal $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ \dot W_T &= -T\,\mathbf e_{QP}\cdot\mathbf v_0^P = 0 \quad &\text{(power of the tension)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf v_0^P = 0 \ \text{for every motion: the tension does no work on } P}$$

Units: power $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-3}}]$, identically zero

Meaning: $P$ never moves along the string: the plate is fixed and the string slides freely over the edge, so the constraint is stationary and its force is normal to every possible velocity.

(g) Mechanical energy

Mechanical energy of P, its initial value and its conservation Law: theorem of kinetic energy; potential of the weight; zero power of the tension Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12 m|\mathbf v_0^P|^2\right) &= -mg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf v_0^P + 0 = -\frac{d}{dt}\left(mg\,z_P\right) \quad &\text{(power of the weight and of the tension)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= u^2\dot\phi^2 + \ell^2\dot\theta^2\left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right) = \ell^2\dot\theta^2 + u^2\dot\phi^2 \quad &\text{(orthonormal $\mathcal B_1$)} \\ E &= \tfrac12 m\left(\ell^2\dot\theta^2 + u^2\dot\phi^2\right) - mg\,u\cos\phi \quad &\text{(kinetic plus potential energy; $z_P = -u\cos\phi$)} \\ u(0) &= \ell\cos\tfrac{\pi}{3} - d = \tfrac{\ell}{2} - d \quad &\text{($\cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac12$)} \\ z_P(0) &= -\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2} - d\right)\cos\tfrac{\pi}{3} = -\tfrac{\ell}{4} + \tfrac{d}{2} \quad &\text{(height of $P$ at $t = 0$)} \\ E(0) &= \tfrac12 m\ell g - mg\left(\tfrac{\ell}{4} - \tfrac{d}{2}\right) = \frac{mg\ell}{4} + \frac{mgd}{2} \quad &\text{(speed squared $\ell g$ at $t = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E = \tfrac12 m\left[\ell^2\dot\theta^2 + \left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)^2\dot\phi^2\right] - mg\left(\ell\cos\theta - d\right)\cos\phi = \frac{mg\ell}{4} + \frac{mgd}{2} = \text{const}}$$

Units: $[m\ell^2\dot\theta^2] = [mg\ell] = [mgd] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the energy is conserved: the weight is conservative and the only other force, the tension, does no work (part (f)), because the plate is at rest.

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Degrees of freedom2: $\theta$ and $\phi$
(b) Unit vector $Q \to P$$\mathbf e_{QP} = \cos\theta\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0$
(c) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^Q = d\,\mathbf i_0 + d\tan\theta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf v_0^Q = d\dot\theta\cos^{-2}\theta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf a_0^Q = d(\ddot\theta\cos\theta + 2\dot\theta^2\sin\theta)\cos^{-3}\theta\,\mathbf j_0$; $\mathbf r_0^P = (d + u\sin\phi)\,\mathbf i_0 + \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - u\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0$ with $u = \ell\cos\theta - d$; $\mathbf v_0^P = (\ell\cos\theta - d)\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\dot\theta(\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf k_1)$, $\mathbf a_0^P = [(\ell\cos\theta - d)\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi]\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell(\ddot\theta\cos\theta - \dot\theta^2\sin\theta)\,\mathbf j_0 + [\ell(\ddot\theta\sin\theta + \dot\theta^2\cos\theta) + (\ell\cos\theta - d)\dot\phi^2]\,\mathbf k_1$, in the basis $\mathbf i_1 = \cos\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf k_1 = -\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0$
(d) Forces on $P$weight $-mg\,\mathbf k_0$; tension $-T\,\mathbf e_{QP}$, $T \ge 0$
(e) Equations of motion$\ell\ddot\theta + (\ell\cos\theta - d)\sin\theta\,\dot\phi^2 + g\sin\theta\cos\phi = 0$; $(\ell\cos\theta - d)\ddot\phi - 2\ell\sin\theta\,\dot\theta\dot\phi + g\sin\phi = 0$
(f) Work of the tensionnone: $\mathbf e_{QP}\cdot\mathbf v_0^P = 0$ identically
(g) Mechanical energy$E = \frac12 m[\ell^2\dot\theta^2 + (\ell\cos\theta - d)^2\dot\phi^2] - mg(\ell\cos\theta - d)\cos\phi$; $E(0) = mg\ell/4 + mgd/2$; conserved

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 76, item 39, answers only part (f): the tension does no work, which is proved with the dot product of the unit vector of the string and the velocity of $P$ vanishing; the box of part (f) is that proof (match). The other parts have no key; the limiting cases below are independent checks.

Limiting case: motion in the vertical plane through Ox0 Law: an invariant solution of the equations of motion Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4

With $\theta$ identically zero, $Q$ stays at the foot $(d, 0, 0)$ of the perpendicular and the string hangs from the edge in the plane $y_0 = 0$.

$$\begin{aligned} \ell\cdot 0 + \left(\ell - d\right)\cdot 0\cdot\dot\phi^2 + g\cdot 0\cdot\cos\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(first equation with $\theta \equiv 0$: satisfied for every $\phi(t)$)} \\ \left(\ell - d\right)\ddot\phi - 0 + g\sin\phi &= 0 \quad &\text{(second equation with $\theta \equiv 0$)} \\ T &= m\left(\ell - d\right)\dot\phi^2 + mg\cos\phi \quad &\text{(tension with $\theta \equiv 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\theta \equiv 0:\quad \ddot\phi + \frac{g}{\ell - d}\sin\phi = 0, \qquad T = m\left(\ell - d\right)\dot\phi^2 + mg\cos\phi}$$

Units: $[g/(\ell - d)] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$; $[T] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: a simple pendulum of length $\ell - d$ hanging from the edge, with its classical tension (Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7): the general equations contain the expected special case.

Limiting case: edge through the anchor point Law: the results with the distance d set to zero Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7
$$\begin{aligned} d &\to 0:\quad \rho = \ell, \quad u = \ell\cos\theta \quad &\text{($Q$ coincides with $O$)} \\ \mathbf r_0^P &= \ell\left(\cos\theta\sin\phi\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 - \cos\theta\cos\phi\,\mathbf k_0\right) = \ell\,\mathbf e_{QP} \quad &\text{(position box)} \\ E(0) &= \frac{mg\ell}{4} + 0 = \tfrac12 m\ell g - mg\ell\cos\tfrac{\pi}{3}\cos\tfrac{\pi}{3} \quad &\text{(energy box)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{d \to 0:\ |\mathbf r_0^P| = \ell, \quad E(0) = \frac{mg\ell}{4}}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$; $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $P$ stays on the sphere of radius $\ell$ centred at $O$: a spherical pendulum described by two angles, whose height is $-\ell\cos\theta\cos\phi$; the energy matches the general value with zero $d$.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 48

Two Connected Particles, One on Paraboloid

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 33
Multi-Particle Manifold

Two heavy particles $P$ and $Q$, each of mass $m$, are connected with an inextensible, massless string of length $\ell$ as shown in the figure. Particle $P$ is constrained to stay on a paraboloid, given implicitly by equation $z = cR^2$, where $c$ is a known constant and we have introduced the usual cylindrical coordinates $\{R, \theta, z\}$. The string passes through a small hole at the origin $O$, and particle $Q$ is restricted to hang along the vertical axis. The reference frame $S_0 = \{O; \mathbf{i}_0, \mathbf{j}_0, \mathbf{k}_0\}$ is inertial. There is no friction in this problem. Assume that the string is long enough so that $Q$ never reaches $O$ and that it is always tense.

  • (a)
    How many effective degrees of freedom does the system have? justify your answer.
  • (b)
    Give the expressions for the position, velocity, and acceleration of $P$ and $Q$ with respect to $S_0$ as a function of $R, \theta$ and their derivatives. If you use any vector basis other than $\{\mathbf{i}_0, \mathbf{j}_0, \mathbf{k}_0\}$ to write your solution, define it first.
  • (c)
    Find a normal vector and two linearly independent tangent vectors to the paraboloid as a function of $R, \theta$. They do not need to be unitary.
  • (d)
    Identify and write down the general expression of all the forces acting on $P$ and $Q$, introducing new unknowns as needed.
  • (e)
    Find the condition that the solution $R(t)$ must satisfy so that the string is indeed always in tension.
  • (f)
    Using $S_0$ for your analysis, write down two second-order differential equations for $R$ and $\theta$, that do not depend on any other unknowns.
  • (g)
    Find two scalar conservation laws (i.e., first integrals).
  • (h)
    Assuming that initially $R = 1/c, \theta = 0, \dot{R} = 0, \dot{\theta} = d$, where $d \neq 0$ is a given value, write down an equation in the form $f(R) = 0$ whose roots determine the minimum and maximum values of $R$ in the subsequent motion. You do not need to solve this equation.
Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

Two heavy particles $P$ and $Q$ of mass $m$ are joined by an inextensible massless string of length $\ell$ that passes through a small hole at the vertex $O$ of a paraboloid; $P$ moves without friction on the paraboloid of height $cR^2$ at the radius $R$ and $Q$ hangs on the vertical axis below $O$ (Problems.pdf, p. 33). The statement asks for the degrees of freedom, the kinematics, a normal and two tangent vectors of the paraboloid, the forces, the condition for a tense string, the equations of motion for $R$ and $\theta$, two first integrals and the equation of the extreme radii. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$, origin at the vertex of the paraboloid and the hole (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i_0, \mathbf j_0, \mathbf k_0\}$, $\mathbf k_0$ vertical upward, along the axis of the paraboloid
Cylindrical basis$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf e_R, \mathbf e_\theta, \mathbf k_0\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k_0$; $\mathbf e_R$ horizontal, towards the meridian plane of $P$ (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24)
Coordinates$R$, distance from $P$ to the axis, and $\theta$, azimuth of $P$ from $\mathbf i_0$ (statement)
Hypotheses$c \gt 0$ (the paraboloid opens upwards, figure); the string is straight from $O$ to $P$ (the bowl is convex, so the chord $OP$ does not touch the surface), always tense, and $Q$ never reaches $O$ (statement); no friction anywhere
Constraints$P$ on the paraboloid, $z_P = cR^2$ (1 equation, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral: $P$ is constrained to stay on it); $Q$ on the axis, $x_Q = y_Q = 0$ (2 equations, holonomic, stationary, smooth, bilateral); string, $|OP| + |OQ| = \ell$ (1 equation joining the particles, holonomic, stationary, unilateral) ([Lecture 3, Slide 17]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom (a)6 coordinates of two particles minus 4 constraint equations: 2 effective degrees of freedom, $R(t)$ and $\theta(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $\ell$ [m], $c$ [m$^{-1}$], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]; for part (h), $R(0) = 1/c$, $\theta(0) = 0$, $\dot R(0) = 0$, $\dot\theta(0) = d$ [s$^{-1}$], $d \ne 0$
Applied forcesweights $-mg\,\mathbf k_0$ on each particle
Constraint forceson $P$: tension towards $O$ and normal reaction of the paraboloid; on $Q$: tension along $\mathbf k_0$ and the reaction of the guide, normal to the axis; the same tension $T$ at both ends (massless string, small smooth hole, [Lecture 3, Slide 17])
Figure 1. Meridian plane of $P$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ into the paper. The triangle of legs $R$ and $cR^2$ gives the length $\rho = R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}$ of string on the side of $P$; the tangent $\mathbf t_R = \mathbf e_R + 2cR\,\mathbf k_0$ rises with the slope $2cR$ and the normal points into the bowl..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotation by $\theta$ about $\mathbf k_0$ (out of the paper); the triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$ has its opposite leg along $-\mathbf i_0$, which gives its minus sign..
Cylindrical basis: rotation about k0 Law: elementary rotation about the third axis; change of basis matrix and its checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.24

View from above along $\mathbf k_0$, which points out of the paper (⊙) with $\mathbf i_0$ to the right and $\mathbf j_0$ up; positive angles are counterclockwise. $\mathbf e_R$ is $\mathbf i_0$ turned by $\theta$: hypotenuse 1, adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf i_0$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along $\mathbf j_0$. $\mathbf e_\theta$ is $\mathbf j_0$ turned by $\theta$: adjacent leg $\cos\theta$ along $\mathbf j_0$, opposite leg $\sin\theta$ along minus $\mathbf i_0$ (the upward vector turned counterclockwise leans to the left: the minus sign).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf e_R &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \mathbf e_\theta &= -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(right triangle of $\mathbf e_\theta$: opposite leg on $-\mathbf i_0$)} \\ \mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta &= \left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right)\mathbf k_0 = \mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= 1\cdot\left(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} &= \cos\theta\sin\theta - \sin\theta\cos\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 2; row 3 is the unit row of $\mathbf k_0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta & 0 \cr \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det[{}_0R_1] = 1, \quad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: $\mathbf e_R$ and $\mathbf k_0$ span the meridian plane of $P$, where the string, the weight and the normal reaction all lie.

Position vectors and the length of string on each side Law: position vectors with the constraints substituted; Pythagoras in the meridian triangle; inextensible string Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.28; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]

In the meridian plane the segment $OP$ is the hypotenuse of the right triangle with the horizontal leg $R$ (along $\mathbf e_R$) and the vertical leg $cR^2$ (along $\mathbf k_0$).

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= R\,\mathbf e_R + cR^2\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(cylindrical position with $z = cR^2$)} \\ \rho &= |OP| = \sqrt{R^2 + c^2R^4} = R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2} \quad &\text{(hypotenuse of the meridian triangle; $R \ge 0$)} \\ |OQ| &= \ell - \rho \quad &\text{(inextensible string)} \\ \mathbf r_0^Q &= -\left(\ell - \rho\right)\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(on the axis, below $O$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_R + cR^2\,\mathbf k_0, \qquad \mathbf r_0^Q = -\left(\ell - R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}\right)\mathbf k_0}$$

Units: $[cR^2] = [\mathrm{L^{-1}}][\mathrm{L^2}] = [\mathrm{L}]$; $cR$ is dimensionless

Meaning: when $P$ climbs the bowl (larger $R$), the string on its side lengthens and $Q$ rises by the same amount.

Derivatives of the length of string on the side of P Law: chain rule; quotient rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3

Write $w$ for the square root of $1 + c^2R^2$, so that $\rho = Rw$ and $\rho'$, $\rho''$ denote derivatives with respect to $R$.

$$\begin{aligned} w' &= \frac{1}{2w}\cdot 2c^2R = \frac{c^2R}{w} \quad &\text{(chain rule on the square root)} \\ \rho' &= w + Rw' = w + \frac{c^2R^2}{w} = \frac{1 + 2c^2R^2}{w} \quad &\text{(product rule; common denominator $w$)} \\ \rho'' &= \frac{4c^2R\,w - \left(1 + 2c^2R^2\right)w'}{w^2} = \frac{4c^2R\,w^2 - \left(1 + 2c^2R^2\right)c^2R}{w^3} \quad &\text{(quotient rule; $w'$ substituted, times $w/w$)} \\ &= \frac{c^2R\left[4 + 4c^2R^2 - 1 - 2c^2R^2\right]}{w^3} = \frac{c^2R\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{w^3} \quad &\text{($w^2 = 1 + c^2R^2$)} \\ \dot\rho &= \rho'\dot R \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \ddot\rho &= \rho''\dot R\cdot\dot R + \rho'\ddot R \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $\rho'(R)$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\rho' = \frac{1 + 2c^2R^2}{\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}}, \qquad \rho'' = \frac{c^2R\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^{3/2}}}$$

Units: $\rho'$ dimensionless; $[\rho''] = [c^2R] = [\mathrm{L^{-1}}]$

Meaning: $\rho'$ is at least 1: the string on the side of $P$ grows at least as fast as the radius.

Kinematics

(b) Position, velocity and acceleration of both particles Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; derivatives of the cylindrical unit vectors; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.25; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.26; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4
$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathbf e}_R &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_R = \dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{($\mathcal B_1$ turns about $\mathbf k_0$ with $\theta$; $\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_R = \mathbf e_\theta$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^P &= \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + 2cR\dot R\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on $cR^2$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \ddot R\,\mathbf e_R + \dot R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + \left(\dot R\dot\theta + R\ddot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta - R\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_R + 2c\left(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R\right)\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \\ &= \left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_R + \left(R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + 2c\left(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R\right)\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(grouped)} \\ \mathbf v_0^Q &= \dot\rho\,\mathbf k_0 = \rho'\dot R\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{($\ell$ constant; chain rule on $\rho$)} \\ \mathbf a_0^Q &= \ddot\rho\,\mathbf k_0 = \left(\rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right)\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_R + cR^2\,\mathbf k_0, \quad \mathbf v_0^P = \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + 2cR\dot R\,\mathbf k_0 \cr \mathbf a_0^P = \left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right)\mathbf e_R + \left(R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta\right)\mathbf e_\theta + 2c\left(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R\right)\mathbf k_0 \cr \mathbf r_0^Q = -\left(\ell - \rho\right)\mathbf k_0, \quad \mathbf v_0^Q = \rho'\dot R\,\mathbf k_0, \quad \mathbf a_0^Q = \left(\rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right)\mathbf k_0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\dot R] = [R\dot\theta] = [cR\dot R] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; every acceleration term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$, e.g. $[\rho''\dot R^2] = [\mathrm{L^{-1}}][\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: these are the derivatives of the cylindrical position with $z = cR^2$; $Q$ moves only along the axis, at the rate at which the string on the side of $P$ changes.

(c) Tangent and normal vectors of the paraboloid Law: tangent vectors as partial derivatives of the parametric form; normal as their cross product Source: [Lecture 3, Slide 9]; Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.3
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf t_R &= \frac{\partial\mathbf r_0^P}{\partial R} = \mathbf e_R + 2cR\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(derivative at constant $\theta$)} \\ \mathbf t_\theta &= \frac{\partial\mathbf r_0^P}{\partial\theta} = R\,\frac{\partial\mathbf e_R}{\partial\theta} = R\,\mathbf e_\theta \quad &\text{(derivative at constant $R$)} \\ \mathbf t_R\times\mathbf t_\theta &= R\,\mathbf e_R\times\mathbf e_\theta + 2cR^2\,\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_\theta = R\,\mathbf k_0 - 2cR^2\,\mathbf e_R \quad &\text{($\mathbf k_0\times\mathbf e_\theta = -\mathbf e_R$)} \\ \hat{\mathbf n} &= \frac{-2cR\,\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k_0}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}} \quad &\text{(normalised; it points into the bowl)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf t_R = \mathbf e_R + 2cR\,\mathbf k_0, \qquad \mathbf t_\theta = R\,\mathbf e_\theta, \qquad \mathbf n = R\left(-2cR\,\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k_0\right)}$$

Units: $\mathbf t_R$ dimensionless; $[\mathbf t_\theta] = [\mathbf n] = [\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: the meridian tangent rises with the slope $2cR$ (right triangle of legs 1 and $2cR$ in the meridian figure); the normal is perpendicular to both tangents, and only along it can the smooth paraboloid push.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagrams show on $P$ its weight, the tension towards the hole and the normal reaction; on $Q$ its weight, the tension upwards and the reaction of the guide. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ and $Q$. The string pulls each particle towards $O$ with the same tension $T$; the paraboloid acts on $P$ only along $\hat{\mathbf n}$, and the guide on $Q$ only normal to the axis (it turns out to be zero)..
(d) Forces on both particles Law: weight; tension of a massless string through a smooth hole; reactions in the normal subspaces Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.5; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]

In the meridian triangle of $OP$ the cosine of the angle of the string with the horizontal is $R/\rho$ (adjacent leg over hypotenuse) and its sine is $cR^2/\rho$ (opposite leg over hypotenuse).

$$\begin{aligned} -\frac{\mathbf r_0^P}{\rho} &= -\frac{R}{\rho}\,\mathbf e_R - \frac{cR^2}{\rho}\,\mathbf k_0 \quad &\text{(unit vector from $P$ towards $O$)} \\ \mathbf F^P &= -mg\,\mathbf k_0 - \frac{T}{\rho}\left(R\,\mathbf e_R + cR^2\,\mathbf k_0\right) + N\,\hat{\mathbf n} \quad &\text{(weight, tension $T \ge 0$, normal reaction $N$)} \\ \mathbf F^Q &= -mg\,\mathbf k_0 + T\,\mathbf k_0 + R_x\,\mathbf i_0 + R_y\,\mathbf j_0 \quad &\text{(weight, tension towards $O$, guide reaction normal to the axis)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf F^P = -mg\,\mathbf k_0 - \frac{T}{\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}}\left(\mathbf e_R + cR\,\mathbf k_0\right) + N\,\frac{-2cR\,\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k_0}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}}, \qquad \mathbf F^Q = \left(T - mg\right)\mathbf k_0 + R_x\,\mathbf i_0 + R_y\,\mathbf j_0}$$

Units: every force $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$; the fractions are dimensionless

Meaning: four new unknowns, $T$, $N$, $R_x$, $R_y$; none of the forces on $P$ has a component along $\mathbf e_\theta$.

Newton's second law for each particle Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame; projections on the tangent vectors (free of $N$) and on the axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\mathbf t_R &= \ddot R - R\dot\theta^2 + 4c^2R\left(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R\right) \quad &\text{(components on $\mathbf e_R$ and $\mathbf k_0$ times 1 and $2cR$)} \\ -\frac{\mathbf r_0^P}{\rho}\cdot\mathbf t_R &= -\frac{R + 2c^2R^3}{R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}} = -\rho' \quad &\text{(tension direction on the meridian tangent)} \\ P,\ \mathbf t_R:\ m\left[\left(1 + 4c^2R^2\right)\ddot R + 4c^2R\dot R^2 - R\dot\theta^2\right] &= -2mgcR - T\rho' \quad &\text{($-mg\,\mathbf k_0\cdot\mathbf t_R = -2mgcR$; $\hat{\mathbf n}\cdot\mathbf t_R = 0$)} \\ P,\ \mathbf e_\theta:\ m\left(R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta\right) &= 0 \quad &\text{(no force along $\mathbf e_\theta$)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf k_0:\ m\left(\rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right) &= T - mg \quad &\text{(along the axis)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf i_0,\ \mathbf j_0:\ 0 &= R_x, \quad 0 = R_y \quad &\text{($\mathbf a_0^Q$ has no horizontal part)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{T = m\left(g + \rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right), \qquad R_x = R_y = 0}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: five of the six scalar equations; the sixth, along $\hat{\mathbf n}$, gives $N$ afterwards. The equation of $Q$ gives the tension in terms of the motion of $R$.

(f) Equations of motion for R and theta, free of other unknowns Law: elimination of the tension between the equations of P and Q Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.7
$$\begin{aligned} m\left[\left(1 + 4c^2R^2\right)\ddot R + 4c^2R\dot R^2 - R\dot\theta^2\right] &= -2mgcR - m\rho'\left(g + \rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right) \quad &\text{(tension of $Q$ substituted)} \\ \left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\ddot R + \left(4c^2R + \rho'\rho''\right)\dot R^2 - R\dot\theta^2 + 2gcR + g\rho' &= 0 \quad &\text{(divided by $m$; all terms on the left)} \\ \rho'^2 &= \frac{\left(1 + 2c^2R^2\right)^2}{1 + c^2R^2} \quad &\text{(square of $\rho'$)} \\ \rho'\rho'' &= \frac{1 + 2c^2R^2}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^{1/2}}\cdot\frac{c^2R\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^{3/2}} = \frac{c^2R\left(1 + 2c^2R^2\right)\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^2} \quad &\text{(product of the two derivatives)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\ddot R + \left(4c^2R + \rho'\rho''\right)\dot R^2 - R\dot\theta^2 + 2gcR + g\rho' = 0 \cr R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta = 0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\ddot R] = [R\dot\theta^2] = [gcR] = [g\rho'] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$; $[c^2R\dot R^2] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two second-order equations in $R$ and $\theta$ only; the coefficient of the radial acceleration is the effective inertia of the pair: $1 + 4c^2R^2$ from $P$ moving along the meridian and $\rho'^2$ from $Q$ moving along the axis.

Normal reaction of the paraboloid Law: projection of Newton's second law for P on the unit normal Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf a_0^P\cdot\hat{\mathbf n} &= \frac{-2cR\left(\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2\right) + 2c\left(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R\right)}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}} = \frac{2c\left(\dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2\right)}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}} \quad &\text{(the terms in $\ddot R$ cancel)} \\ \mathbf F^P\cdot\hat{\mathbf n} &= \frac{-mg}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}} - \frac{T}{\rho}\,\frac{-2cR^2 + cR^2}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}} + N \quad &\text{(weight, tension, reaction)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{N = \frac{2mc\left(\dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2\right) + mg - \dfrac{TcR}{\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}}}{\sqrt{1 + 4c^2R^2}}}$$

Units: $[mc\dot R^2] = [\mathrm{M\,L^{-1}\,L^2\,T^{-2}}] = [mg] = [T] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the bowl carries the weight and supplies the normal part of the acceleration; the string, which pulls $P$ partly away from the surface, reduces the reaction.

Answers and Checks

(e) Tense string

Condition for the string to stay tense Law: a string can only pull: positive tension (unilateral constraint) Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 21]
$$\begin{aligned} T &= m\left(g + \rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2\right) \gt 0 \quad &\text{(equation of $Q$)} \\ \ddot z_Q &= \frac{d^2\rho}{dt^2} = \rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2 \gt -g \quad &\text{(the bracket is the vertical acceleration of $Q$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\frac{1 + 2c^2R^2}{\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}}\,\ddot R + \frac{c^2R\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^{3/2}}\,\dot R^2 \gt -g \quad \text{for all } t}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the solution $R(t)$ must never make $Q$ accelerate downwards faster than free fall; if it did, the string would go slack and $Q$ would fall freely.

(g) Two first integrals

Areal integral and energy integral derived from the equations of motion Law: exact derivatives (product and chain rules); Barrow's rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(R^2\dot\theta\right) &= 2R\dot R\dot\theta + R^2\ddot\theta = R\left(R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta\right) = 0 \quad &\text{(product and chain rules; equation of $\theta$)} \\ \left[R^2\dot\theta\right]_0^t &= 0 \Rightarrow R^2\dot\theta = h \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule; $h$ constant)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12\left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\dot R^2\right] &= \left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\dot R\ddot R + \left(4c^2R + \rho'\rho''\right)\dot R^3 \quad &\text{(product rule; chain rule on the coefficient)} \\ -R\dot\theta^2\,\dot R &= -\frac{h^2}{R^3}\,\dot R = \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{h^2}{2R^2}\right) \quad &\text{($\dot\theta = h/R^2$; chain rule)} \\ \left(2gcR + g\rho'\right)\dot R &= \frac{d}{dt}\left[g\left(cR^2 + \rho\right)\right] \quad &\text{(chain rule)} \\ \left[\tfrac12\left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\dot R^2 + \frac{h^2}{2R^2} + g\left(cR^2 + \rho\right)\right]_0^t &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of $R$ times $\dot R$: a sum of exact derivatives; Barrow's rule)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{R^2\dot\theta = h, \qquad E = \tfrac12 m\left[\left(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2\right)\dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2\right] + mg\left(cR^2 + R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}\right) - mg\ell = \text{const}}$$

Units: $[h] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-1}}]$; every term of $E$ $[\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the first is the angular momentum of $P$ about the axis divided by $m$, constant because no force on $P$ has a moment about the axis; the second is the mechanical energy of the pair (kinetic energy of $P$ and $Q$ plus the potential $mg\,z_P + mg\,z_Q$, with $z_Q = \rho - \ell$): the smooth stationary constraints do no work and the string only transfers energy between the particles.

(h) Extreme values of R

Equation of the turning radii Law: at an extreme of R its rate vanishes; the two first integrals with the initial data Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.27; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28; Problems.pdf, p. 33
$$\begin{aligned} h &= R(0)^2\,\dot\theta(0) = \frac{d}{c^2} \quad &\text{($R(0) = 1/c$, $\dot\theta(0) = d$)} \\ \rho(0) &= \frac{1}{c}\sqrt{1 + 1} = \frac{\sqrt2}{c} \quad &\text{(string on the side of $P$ at $t = 0$)} \\ \frac{E}{m} + g\ell &= 0 + \frac{h^2c^2}{2} + g\left(\frac{1}{c} + \frac{\sqrt2}{c}\right) = \frac{d^2}{2c^2} + \frac{\left(1 + \sqrt2\right)g}{c} \quad &\text{($\dot R(0) = 0$; $R^2\dot\theta^2 = h^2/R^2$)} \\ \frac{h^2}{2R^2} + g\left(cR^2 + R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}\right) &= \frac{d^2}{2c^2} + \frac{\left(1 + \sqrt2\right)g}{c} \quad &\text{(energy integral at a radius where $\dot R = 0$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{f(R) = \frac{d^2}{2c^4R^2} + g\left(cR^2 + R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}\right) - \frac{d^2}{2c^2} - \frac{\left(1 + \sqrt2\right)g}{c} = 0}$$

Units: $[d^2/(c^4R^2)] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L^4}][\mathrm{L^{-2}}] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$; $[gcR^2] = [g/c] = [\mathrm{L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the energy integral gives one half of the effective inertia times the square of the radial rate equal to minus $f(R)$, so the motion is confined to the radii where $f$ is negative or zero; $R = 1/c$ is always a root (the start), and the next box proves that there is at most one other root, which is then the other turning radius.

At most two turning radii Law: a strictly convex function that tends to plus infinity at both ends of its interval has at most two roots Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28; Problems.pdf, p. 33

The function $f$ of the previous box is differentiated twice with respect to $R$ on the interval of positive radii, with $\rho'$ and $\rho''$ from the box of the derivatives of the length of string; the initial azimuthal rate $d$ is taken as nonzero, as in the statement.

$$\begin{aligned} f'(R) &= -\frac{d^2}{c^4R^3} + g\left(2cR + \rho'\right) \quad &\text{(derivative term by term; $\frac{d}{dR}R^{-2} = -2R^{-3}$; constants drop out)} \\ f''(R) &= \frac{3d^2}{c^4R^4} + g\left(2c + \rho''\right) \quad &\text{(derivative again; $\frac{d}{dR}R^{-3} = -3R^{-4}$)} \\ \rho'' &= \frac{c^2R\left(3 + 2c^2R^2\right)}{\left(1 + c^2R^2\right)^{3/2}} \gt 0 \quad &\text{(box of the derivatives, with $R \gt 0$ and $c \gt 0$)} \\ f''(R) &\gt 0 \quad &\text{(each of the three terms is positive: $f$ is strictly convex)} \\ \lim_{R\to 0^+} f(R) &= +\infty, \qquad \lim_{R\to\infty} f(R) = +\infty \quad &\text{(the terms $\frac{d^2}{2c^4R^2}$ and $gcR^2$ dominate)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{f'' \gt 0 \ \text{on}\ R \gt 0 \ \text{and}\ f \to +\infty \ \text{at both ends:}\ f \ \text{has at most two roots; one is}\ R = 1/c, \ \text{the other (if}\ f'(1/c) \ne 0\text{) is the other turning radius}}$$

Units: $[f''] = [3d^2/(c^4R^4)] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}][\mathrm{L^4}][\mathrm{L^{-4}}] = [gc] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: a strictly convex function crosses zero at most twice; since $f$ is positive near the axis and far away, it is negative only between its two roots, the band of radii in which $P$ moves. If the derivative of $f$ vanished at the start the two roots would merge and $P$ would stay on its initial parallel (circular motion).

Part of the statementAnswer
(a) Degrees of freedom2 effective ($R$, $\theta$): 6 coordinates minus 4 constraint equations (paraboloid, axis of $Q$ twice, string)
(b) Kinematics$\mathbf r_0^P = R\,\mathbf e_R + cR^2\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf v_0^P = \dot R\,\mathbf e_R + R\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta + 2cR\dot R\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf a_0^P = (\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf e_R + (R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta)\,\mathbf e_\theta + 2c(\dot R^2 + R\ddot R)\,\mathbf k_0$; $\mathbf r_0^Q = -(\ell - \rho)\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf v_0^Q = \rho'\dot R\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf a_0^Q = (\rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2)\,\mathbf k_0$, with $\rho = R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}$, $\mathbf e_R = \cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf e_\theta = -\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0 + \cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0$
(c) Tangent and normal$\mathbf t_R = \mathbf e_R + 2cR\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf t_\theta = R\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\mathbf n = R(-2cR\,\mathbf e_R + \mathbf k_0)$
(d) Forces$P$: $-mg\,\mathbf k_0$, $-T\,\mathbf r_0^P/\rho$, $N\,\hat{\mathbf n}$; $Q$: $-mg\,\mathbf k_0$, $T\,\mathbf k_0$, $R_x\,\mathbf i_0 + R_y\,\mathbf j_0$ (zero)
(e) Tense string$\rho'\ddot R + \rho''\dot R^2 \gt -g$ at all times
(f) Equations of motion$(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2)\ddot R + (4c^2R + \rho'\rho'')\dot R^2 - R\dot\theta^2 + 2gcR + g\rho' = 0$; $R\ddot\theta + 2\dot R\dot\theta = 0$
(g) First integrals$R^2\dot\theta = h$; $\frac12[(1 + 4c^2R^2 + \rho'^2)\dot R^2 + R^2\dot\theta^2] + g(cR^2 + \rho) = \text{const}$
(h) Extreme radii$f(R) = \frac{d^2}{2c^4R^2} + g(cR^2 + R\sqrt{1 + c^2R^2}) - \frac{d^2}{2c^2} - \frac{(1 + \sqrt2)g}{c} = 0$

Check against the official key: Problems.pdf, p. 76 lists item 48 with no answer, so there is no key to compare with. Independent checks: the energy integral was obtained from the equation of motion itself (box of part (g)), so the two are consistent by construction; the turning radii are checked numerically below, and the flat limit reproduces the classical problem of the table with a hole.

Numerical check of the turning radii Law: roots of the equation of part (h) with unit data, located by the change of sign of f Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.28

Unit data: $c$ of 1 m$^{-1}$, $d$ of 1 s$^{-1}$, $g$ of 1 m s$^{-2}$ (so that every term is of order one).

$$\begin{aligned} f(1) &= \tfrac12 + \left(1 + \sqrt2\right) - \tfrac12 - \left(1 + \sqrt2\right) = 0 \quad &\text{(the start is a root)} \\ f(0.7) &= \frac{1}{0.98} + 0.49 + 0.7\sqrt{1.49} - 0.5 - 2.4142 = 1.0204 + 0.49 + 0.8545 - 2.9142 = -0.5493 \quad &\text{(negative: allowed radius)} \\ f(0.4828) &\simeq 0 \quad &\text{(second root, by bisection of the change of sign)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{c = d = g = 1:\quad 0.4828 \le R \le 1}$$

Units: in these units $f$ is in m$^2$ s$^{-2}$

Meaning: with these data the start $R = 1/c$ is the largest radius: the initial azimuthal speed is too small to hold $P$ on that parallel, so $P$ slides down the bowl to the radius 0.4828 and back, precessing round the axis.

Limiting case: flat table with a hole Law: the equation of motion of R with the curvature c set to zero Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4
$$\begin{aligned} c &\to 0:\quad \rho = R, \quad \rho' = 1, \quad \rho'' = 0 \quad &\text{(the bowl becomes the horizontal plane through $O$)} \\ \left(1 + 0 + 1\right)\ddot R + 0 - R\dot\theta^2 + 0 + g &= 0 \quad &\text{(equation of $R$)} \\ T &= m\left(g + \ddot R\right) \quad &\text{(tension)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{c \to 0:\quad 2\ddot R - R\dot\theta^2 + g = 0, \qquad \text{circular motion when } R\dot\theta^2 = g}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the classical pair on a table with a hole: both particles move with the rate of $R$ (effective mass $2m$), and on a circle the weight of $Q$, carried by the string, is the centripetal force of $P$.

Worked Solution Source: Problems.pdf
Problem 50

Double Pendulum

Original statement · Problems.pdf, p. 35
Kinematic LinkageOscillations - Lecture 6 (part f)

A double pendulum consists of two heavy point masses $P$ and $Q$, each of mass $m$, connected to the origin $O$ and to each other by two rigid, massless bars of length $\ell$ as shown in the figure. We define the inertial frame $S_0$ with origin $O$ and vector basis $\mathcal{B}_0$. We define the two angles $\theta$ and $\phi$ shown in the figure, to describe the configuration of the system.

  • (a)
    Write down the general expressions of the position, velocity, and acceleration vectors of $P$ and $Q$, using $\theta, \phi$ and their [derivatives].
  • (b)
    Write down all forces acting on $P$ and $Q$, introducing new unknowns as needed.
  • (c)
    Write down the equations of motion for $\theta$ and $\phi$, free of other unknowns.
  • (d)
    Find and write down one conservation law of this problem.
  • (e)
    If the initial conditions are $\theta = \phi = \pi/4, \dot{\theta} = \dot{\phi} = 0$, will $Q$ ever cross the $Ox$ axis? Justify your answer.
  • (f)
    Consider now that we impose the additional constraint $\phi = 2\theta$. Compute the natural frequency of oscillation of the system under the assumption of small angles.

(Hint: The mechanical energy of an ideal harmonic oscillator of natural frequency $\omega_0$ and mass $m$ can be written as $\frac{1}{2}m(\dot{x}^2 + \omega_0^2 x^2)$).

Worked Solution [ Click to Expand ]

Reference Frames, Bases and Coordinates

Two heavy particles $P$ and $Q$ of mass $m$ hang from the origin $O$ by two rigid massless bars of length $\ell$, $OP$ and $PQ$; the angles $\theta$ and $\phi$ of the bars with the downward vertical describe the configuration (Problems.pdf, p. 35). The statement asks for the kinematics, the forces, the equations of motion free of other unknowns, a conservation law, whether $Q$ can cross the axis $Ox$ for a given start and, with the extra constraint that $\phi$ is twice $\theta$, the natural frequency of small oscillations. The solution follows the Newtonian approach of Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.1 to Sec. 7.7.

ItemDefinition
Fixed (inertial) frame$S_0 = \{O;\ \mathcal B_0\}$ of the figure (Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.1)
Fixed basis$\mathcal B_0 = \{\mathbf i, \mathbf j, \mathbf k\}$: $\mathbf i$ horizontal to the right (axis $Ox$), $\mathbf j$ vertical upward, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper
Basis of the bar $OP$$\mathcal B_1 = \{\mathbf i_1, \mathbf j_1, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned about $\mathbf k$ by the polar angle $\theta - \pi/2$: $\mathbf i_1$ from $O$ to $P$, $\mathbf j_1$ along increasing $\theta$
Basis of the bar $PQ$$\mathcal B_2 = \{\mathbf i_2, \mathbf j_2, \mathbf k\}$, $\mathcal B_0$ turned about $\mathbf k$ by $\phi - \pi/2$: $\mathbf i_2$ from $P$ to $Q$, $\mathbf j_2$ along increasing $\phi$
Coordinates$\theta$, angle of $OP$ from the downward vertical; $\phi$, angle of $PQ$ from the downward vertical; both positive counterclockwise, towards $\mathbf i$ from below (figure)
Hypothesesplanar motion in the plane $Oxy$ of the figure; joints without friction; bars rigid and massless
Constraintsplane of motion for each particle (2 equations); bars, $|\overrightarrow{OP}| = \ell$ and $|\overrightarrow{PQ}| = \ell$ (2 equations, holonomic, stationary, bilateral: a bar can push and pull) ([Lecture 3, Slide 17]; [Lecture 3, Slide 21])
Degrees of freedom6 coordinates minus 4 constraint equations: 2, namely $\theta(t)$ and $\phi(t)$ ([Lecture 3, Slide 4])
Data$m$ [kg], $\ell$ [m], $g$ [m s$^{-2}$]; part (e): $\theta = \phi = \pi/4$ at rest; part (f): extra constraint, $\phi$ equal to $2\theta$
Applied forcesweights $-mg\,\mathbf j$ on each particle
Constraint forcesforces of the bars along the bars: $T_1$ in $OP$ and $T_2$ in $PQ$, positive when the bar pulls (tension), negative when it pushes (Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4)
Figure 1. Plane of the motion, $\mathbf k$ out of the paper. The triangles give $\mathbf r_0^P = \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf i - \ell\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ and $\mathbf r_0^Q = \ell(\sin\theta + \sin\phi)\,\mathbf i - \ell(\cos\theta + \cos\phi)\,\mathbf j$; $Q$ reaches the axis $Ox$ only if $\cos\theta + \cos\phi$ vanishes..

Change of Basis and Position Vector

Figure 2. Rotations about $\mathbf k$ (out of the paper). An angle from the downward vertical is the polar angle plus $\pi/2$; with $\cos(\theta - \pi/2) = \sin\theta$ and $\sin(\theta - \pi/2) = -\cos\theta$ the triangle gives $\mathbf i_1 = \sin\theta\,\mathbf i - \cos\theta\,\mathbf j$..
Bases of the two bars: angles from the downward vertical Law: elementary rotation about the third axis, with the angle related to the usual polar angle; change of basis matrices and their checks Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.14; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.15; Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20; Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7

View along $\mathbf k$, out of the paper (⊙), since $\mathbf k$ is the cross product of $\mathbf i$ (to the right) and $\mathbf j$ (up): positive angles are counterclockwise. $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical, minus $\mathbf j$, which is itself at the polar angle minus $\pi/2$ from $\mathbf i$; so the polar angle of $OP$ is $\theta - \pi/2$, and $\mathbf i_1$ is Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.20 with that angle. The right triangle of $OP$ has hypotenuse $\ell$, horizontal leg $\ell\sin\theta$ (opposite to $\theta$, along $\mathbf i$) and vertical leg $\ell\cos\theta$ (adjacent to $\theta$, along minus $\mathbf j$): this is the minus sign on $\mathbf j$. $\mathbf j_1$ is $\mathbf i_1$ turned a quarter turn counterclockwise. The bar $PQ$ is the same construction with $\phi$.

$$\begin{aligned} \cos\left(\theta - \tfrac{\pi}{2}\right) &= \cos\theta\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2} + \sin\theta\sin\tfrac{\pi}{2} = \sin\theta \quad &\text{(cosine of a difference)} \\ \sin\left(\theta - \tfrac{\pi}{2}\right) &= \sin\theta\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2} - \cos\theta\sin\tfrac{\pi}{2} = -\cos\theta \quad &\text{(sine of a difference)} \\ \mathbf i_1 &= \sin\theta\,\mathbf i - \cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(opposite leg along $\mathbf i$, adjacent leg along $-\mathbf j$, over $\ell$)} \\ \mathbf j_1 &= \cos\theta\,\mathbf i + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\mathbf i_1$ turned by $\pi/2$ counterclockwise)} \\ \mathbf i_2 &= \sin\phi\,\mathbf i - \cos\phi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(same construction with $\phi$: triangle of $PQ$)} \\ \mathbf j_2 &= \cos\phi\,\mathbf i + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{($\mathbf i_2$ turned by $\pi/2$ counterclockwise)} \\ \mathbf i_1\times\mathbf j_1 &= \sin^2\theta\,\mathbf i\times\mathbf j - \cos^2\theta\,\mathbf j\times\mathbf i = \left(\sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta\right)\mathbf k = \mathbf k \quad &\text{(right-handed)} \\ \det[{}_0R_1] &= 1\cdot\left(\sin\theta\sin\theta - \cos\theta\left(-\cos\theta\right)\right) = 1 \quad &\text{(expansion along the third row)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{11} &= \sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta = 1 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 1)} \\ \left([{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T\right)_{12} &= -\sin\theta\cos\theta + \cos\theta\sin\theta = 0 \quad &\text{(row 1 times row 2; row 3 is the unit row of $\mathbf k$)} \\ \mathbf j &= -\cos\theta\,\mathbf i_1 + \sin\theta\,\mathbf j_1 = -\cos\phi\,\mathbf i_2 + \sin\phi\,\mathbf j_2 \quad &\text{(second rows of the matrices, Eq. 1.14)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{[{}_0R_1] = \begin{pmatrix} \sin\theta & \cos\theta & 0 \cr -\cos\theta & \sin\theta & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad [{}_0R_2] = \begin{pmatrix} \sin\phi & \cos\phi & 0 \cr -\cos\phi & \sin\phi & 0 \cr 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det = 1, \quad RR^T = I}$$

Units: dimensionless direction cosines: $[1]$

Meaning: at rest, hanging, $\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf i_2$ are minus $\mathbf j$; the checks of $[{}_0R_2]$ are those of $[{}_0R_1]$ with $\phi$.

Dot products between the two bases Law: dot products of components in the orthonormal basis $\mathcal B_0$; angle-difference identities Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.13
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf i_2 &= \sin\theta\sin\phi + \cos\theta\cos\phi = \cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(cosine of a difference)} \\ \mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf j_2 &= \cos\theta\cos\phi + \sin\theta\sin\phi = \cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(cosine of a difference)} \\ \mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf j_2 &= \sin\theta\cos\phi - \cos\theta\sin\phi = \sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(sine of a difference)} \\ \mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf i_2 &= \cos\theta\sin\phi - \sin\theta\cos\phi = -\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{(sine of a difference, with a minus sign)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf i_2 = \mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf j_2 = \cos\left(\theta - \phi\right), \qquad \mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf j_2 = -\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf i_2 = \sin\left(\theta - \phi\right)}$$

Units: dimensionless

Meaning: only the angle between the two bars, $\theta - \phi$, enters the coupling of the two particles.

Position vectors of both particles Law: position vectors as sums of partial vectors along the bars Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.2; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.4
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf r_0^P &= \overrightarrow{OP} = \ell\,\mathbf i_1 = \ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf i - \ell\cos\theta\,\mathbf j \quad &\text{(legs of the triangle of $OP$)} \\ \mathbf r_0^Q &= \overrightarrow{OP} + \overrightarrow{PQ} = \ell\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(sum of partial vectors)} \\ &= \ell\left(\sin\theta + \sin\phi\right)\mathbf i - \ell\left(\cos\theta + \cos\phi\right)\mathbf j \quad &\text{(legs of both triangles)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf r_0^P = \ell\,\mathbf i_1, \qquad \mathbf r_0^Q = \ell\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\,\mathbf i_2, \qquad y_P = -\ell\cos\theta, \qquad y_Q = -\ell\left(\cos\theta + \cos\phi\right)}$$

Units: $[\mathrm{L}]$

Meaning: $Q$ is on the axis $Ox$ when its height $y_Q$ is zero, which is the question of part (e).

Kinematics

Derivatives of the bases of the bars Law: derivative of a unit vector turning about a fixed axis Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 1.62
$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol\omega_{10} &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(the polar angle $\theta - \pi/2$ changes at the rate $\dot\theta$)} \\ \boldsymbol\omega_{20} &= \dot\phi\,\mathbf k \quad &\text{(the polar angle $\phi - \pi/2$ changes at the rate $\dot\phi$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf i}_1 &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf i_1 = \dot\theta\left(\sin\theta\,\mathbf j + \cos\theta\,\mathbf i\right) = \dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{($\mathbf k\times\mathbf i = \mathbf j$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf j = -\mathbf i$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf j}_1 &= \dot\theta\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf j_1 = \dot\theta\left(\cos\theta\,\mathbf j - \sin\theta\,\mathbf i\right) = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(same products)} \\ \dot{\mathbf i}_2 &= \dot\phi\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf i_2 = \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 \quad &\text{(same products with $\phi$)} \\ \dot{\mathbf j}_2 &= \dot\phi\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf j_2 = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(same products with $\phi$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\dot{\mathbf i}_1 = \dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf j}_1 = -\dot\theta\,\mathbf i_1, \quad \dot{\mathbf i}_2 = \dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2, \quad \dot{\mathbf j}_2 = -\dot\phi\,\mathbf i_2}$$

Units: $[\dot\theta] = [\dot\phi] = [\mathrm{T^{-1}}]$

Meaning: each bar turns about $\mathbf k$ with its own angular rate.

(a) Position, velocity and acceleration of both particles Law: time derivatives in $S_0$; product rule Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.3; Notes.pdf, Eq. 2.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.34; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.35
$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf v_0^P &= \ell\,\dot{\mathbf i}_1 = \ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 \quad &\text{($\ell$ constant)} \\ \mathbf a_0^P &= \ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 + \ell\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf j}_1 = \ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 - \ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(product rule; derivative of $\mathbf j_1$)} \\ \mathbf v_0^Q &= \ell\,\dot{\mathbf i}_1 + \ell\,\dot{\mathbf i}_2 = \ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 + \ell\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 \quad &\text{(sum of the two bar terms)} \\ \mathbf a_0^Q &= \ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 - \ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf i_1 + \ell\ddot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 - \ell\dot\phi^2\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(product rule on each term)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf r_0^P = \ell\,\mathbf i_1, \quad \mathbf v_0^P = \ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1, \quad \mathbf a_0^P = \ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 - \ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf i_1 \cr \mathbf r_0^Q = \ell\left(\mathbf i_1 + \mathbf i_2\right), \quad \mathbf v_0^Q = \ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 + \ell\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2, \quad \mathbf a_0^Q = \ell\left(\ddot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 - \dot\theta^2\,\mathbf i_1 + \ddot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 - \dot\phi^2\,\mathbf i_2\right) \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\ell\dot\theta] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-1}}]$; $[\ell\ddot\theta] = [\ell\dot\theta^2] = [\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: $P$ moves on a circle about $O$ (the pendulum of Notes.pdf, Sec. 4.7); $Q$ adds to the motion of $P$ its own circular motion about $P$.

Forces and Equations of Motion

The free-body diagrams show on each particle its weight and the forces of the bars attached to it; the bar $PQ$ acts on $P$ and on $Q$ with equal and opposite forces. $S_0$ is inertial, so no inertia force acts.

Figure 3. Forces on $P$ and $Q$. Each massless bar acts along its own direction; the bar $PQ$ pulls $P$ and $Q$ with $\pm T_2\,\mathbf i_2$, so adding the two equations of motion removes $T_2$..
(b) Forces on both particles Law: weight; constraint forces in the normal subspaces of the bar constraints, along the gradients of their equations Source: Notes.pdf, Sec. 6.4; [Lecture 3, Slide 13]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]

The bar $OP$ is the constraint whose equation is the square of the length of $OP$ minus $\ell^2$; its gradient with respect to the position of $P$ is twice $\overrightarrow{OP}$, along $\mathbf i_1$. The bar $PQ$ is the constraint on the square of the length of $PQ$; its gradient is twice $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ (along $\mathbf i_2$) with respect to $Q$ and minus that with respect to $P$, so its forces on $Q$ and $P$ are equal and opposite.

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbf N_{OP\to P} &= -T_1\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(along the bar; $T_1 \gt 0$ pulls $P$ towards $O$)} \\ \mathbf N_{PQ\to Q} &= -T_2\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(along the bar; $T_2 \gt 0$ pulls $Q$ towards $P$)} \\ \mathbf N_{PQ\to P} &= +T_2\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(equal and opposite: minus the gradient with respect to $Q$)} \\ \mathbf F^P &= -mg\,\mathbf j - T_1\,\mathbf i_1 + T_2\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(all forces on $P$)} \\ \mathbf F^Q &= -mg\,\mathbf j - T_2\,\mathbf i_2 \quad &\text{(all forces on $Q$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\mathbf F^P = -mg\,\mathbf j - T_1\,\mathbf i_1 + T_2\,\mathbf i_2, \qquad \mathbf F^Q = -mg\,\mathbf j - T_2\,\mathbf i_2}$$

Units: $[mg] = [T_1] = [T_2] = [\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two new unknowns, $T_1$ and $T_2$; a bar, unlike a string, may also push, so they may take either sign.

(c) Equations of motion free of the forces of the bars Law: Newton's second law in an inertial frame; projections chosen to eliminate $T_1$ and $T_2$ Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Sec. 7.6; [Lecture 3, Slide 14]

$\mathbf j_2$ is perpendicular to $\mathbf i_2$, so the equation of $Q$ along $\mathbf j_2$ is free of $T_2$. Adding the equations of $P$ and $Q$ cancels the pair $\pm T_2\,\mathbf i_2$, and $\mathbf j_1$ is perpendicular to $\mathbf i_1$, so the sum along $\mathbf j_1$ is free of $T_1$. The weight is projected with $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf j_1 = \sin\theta$ and $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf j_2 = \sin\phi$.

$$\begin{aligned} m\,\mathbf a_0^P + m\,\mathbf a_0^Q &= \mathbf F^P + \mathbf F^Q = -2mg\,\mathbf j - T_1\,\mathbf i_1 \quad &\text{(Newton for each particle, added: the pair $\pm T_2\,\mathbf i_2$ cancels)} \\ \mathbf a_0^Q\cdot\mathbf j_2 &= \ell\ddot\theta\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) - \ell\dot\theta^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \ell\ddot\phi \quad &\text{(dot products of the previous box)} \\ Q,\ \mathbf j_2:\ m\ell\left[\ddot\phi + \ddot\theta\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) - \dot\theta^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right)\right] &= -mg\sin\phi \quad &\text{(no $T_2$)} \\ \left(\mathbf a_0^P + \mathbf a_0^Q\right)\cdot\mathbf j_1 &= 2\ell\ddot\theta + \ell\ddot\phi\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \ell\dot\phi^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_2\cdot\mathbf j_1 = \cos(\theta - \phi)$, $\mathbf i_2\cdot\mathbf j_1 = -\sin(\theta - \phi)$)} \\ P + Q,\ \mathbf j_1:\ m\ell\left[2\ddot\theta + \ddot\phi\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dot\phi^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right)\right] &= -2mg\sin\theta \quad &\text{(no $T_1$, no $T_2$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} 2\ddot\theta + \ddot\phi\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dot\phi^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dfrac{2g}{\ell}\sin\theta = 0 \cr \ddot\phi + \ddot\theta\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) - \dot\theta^2\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dfrac{g}{\ell}\sin\phi = 0 \end{array}}$$

Units: $[\ddot\theta] = [\dot\phi^2] = [g/\ell] = [\mathrm{T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: two coupled second-order equations for the two angles; they contain no force of the bars.

Forces of the bars, afterwards Law: Newton's second law projected along the bars Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.4; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.37
$$\begin{aligned} Q,\ \mathbf i_2:\ m\ell\left[-\ddot\theta\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) - \dot\theta^2\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) - \dot\phi^2\right] &= mg\cos\phi - T_2 \quad &\text{($\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf i_2 = -\sin(\theta - \phi)$; $-\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf i_2 = \cos\phi$)} \\ P,\ \mathbf i_1:\ -m\ell\dot\theta^2 &= mg\cos\theta - T_1 + T_2\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) \quad &\text{($-\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf i_1 = \cos\theta$; $\mathbf i_2\cdot\mathbf i_1 = \cos(\theta - \phi)$)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{\begin{array}{l} T_2 = mg\cos\phi + m\ell\left[\ddot\theta\sin\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dot\theta^2\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) + \dot\phi^2\right] \cr T_1 = mg\cos\theta + m\ell\dot\theta^2 + T_2\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right) \end{array}}$$

Units: every term $[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: hanging at rest ($\theta$, $\phi$ and their rates zero) the lower bar carries the weight of $Q$, $T_2 = mg$, and the upper bar both weights, $T_1 = 2mg$.

Answers and Checks

(d) Conservation law

Mechanical energy of the double pendulum Law: theorem of kinetic energy; the forces of the stationary bars do no total work; potential of the weights Source: Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.11; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.29; Notes.pdf, Eq. 4.31; [Lecture 3, Slide 16]; [Lecture 3, Slide 17]
$$\begin{aligned} -T_1\,\mathbf i_1\cdot\mathbf v_0^P &= -T_1\,\mathbf i_1\cdot\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_1 = 0 \quad &\text{(power of the bar $OP$)} \\ T_2\,\mathbf i_2\cdot\mathbf v_0^P - T_2\,\mathbf i_2\cdot\mathbf v_0^Q &= -T_2\,\mathbf i_2\cdot\left(\mathbf v_0^Q - \mathbf v_0^P\right) = -T_2\,\mathbf i_2\cdot\ell\dot\phi\,\mathbf j_2 = 0 \quad &\text{(total power of the bar $PQ$)} \\ -mg\,\mathbf j\cdot\left(\mathbf v_0^P + \mathbf v_0^Q\right) &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left[mg\left(y_P + y_Q\right)\right] \quad &\text{(power of the weights)} \\ \frac{d}{dt}\left[\tfrac12 m|\mathbf v_0^P|^2 + \tfrac12 m|\mathbf v_0^Q|^2\right] &= -\frac{d}{dt}\left[mg\left(y_P + y_Q\right)\right] \quad &\text{(theorem of kinetic energy for each particle, added: sum of all powers; the bar powers are zero)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^P|^2 &= \ell^2\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf j_1 = \ell^2\dot\theta^2 \quad &\text{(square of $\mathbf v_0^P$; $\mathbf j_1$ unit)} \\ |\mathbf v_0^Q|^2 &= \ell^2\left[\dot\theta^2 + \dot\phi^2 + 2\dot\theta\dot\phi\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right)\right] \quad &\text{(square of $\mathbf v_0^Q$; $\mathbf j_1\cdot\mathbf j_2 = \cos(\theta - \phi)$)} \\ E &= \tfrac12 m\ell^2\dot\theta^2 + \tfrac12 m|\mathbf v_0^Q|^2 - mg\ell\cos\theta - mg\ell\left(\cos\theta + \cos\phi\right) \quad &\text{(Barrow's rule: the bracket of kinetic plus potential energy is constant; $|\mathbf v_0^P|^2$, $y_P$, $y_Q$ substituted)} \end{aligned}$$
$$\boxed{E = \tfrac12 m\ell^2\left[2\dot\theta^2 + \dot\phi^2 + 2\dot\theta\dot\phi\cos\left(\theta - \phi\right)\right] - mg\ell\left(2\cos\theta + \cos\phi\right) = \text{const}}$$

Units: $[m\ell^2\dot\theta^2] = [mg\ell] = [\mathrm{M\,L^2\,T^{-2}}]$

Meaning: the bars only transfer energy between the particles: the bar $OP$ is perpendicular to the velocity of $P$, and the bar $PQ$ is perpendicular to the velocity of $Q$ relative to $P$. Only the conservative weights work.

(e) Can Q cross the axis Ox?
